Title: A Minimum Exact Disjunctive Basis for Two-Robot Solvability Profiles

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17677v1

Published Time: Wed, 14 Oct 2026 04:34:25 GMT

Number of Pages: 100

Markdown Content:
# A Minimum Exact Disjunctive Basis for Two-Robot Solvability Profiles 

# Tsukasa Ninomiya 1, Shota Naito 1, Naoki Kitamura 2, Yuichi Sudo 1, and Koichi Wada 11Hosei University, Tokyo, Japan 

> 2

# The University of Osaka, Osaka, Japan 

Abstract 

We completely characterize the solvability profiles of tasks for two autonomous mobile robots in the plane under variable disorientation, chirality, and rigid movements. The four standard capability models and the schedulers considered here have a known quotient order Q of thirteen computational classes. A task’s solvability profile is the set of classes in which it is solvable, and is necessarily an upset of Q. We prove that every upset is realizable and construct a minimum exact disjunctive basis of thirteen concrete tasks. The basis contains one task for each principal upset. For every antichain of Q, the disjunction of the corresponding tasks has profile exactly equal to the union of their profiles. This exactness is not implied by the individual profiles: an algorithm may satisfy different disjuncts in different executions. We establish it by constructing, for every algorithm in each maximal class outside the target union, a single admissible fair execution violating all selected predicates simultaneously. Consequently, the realizable profiles form the entire finite distributive lattice Up( Q): there are exactly 54 profiles, counted by the antichain polynomial 1 + 13 z + 24 z2 + 16 z3, and each has a canonical disjunctive representation using at most three principal tasks. We also identify six tasks whose solvability signatures recover every inclusion and incomparability in Q, and prove that six is minimum even among arbitrary separating tasks. Their profiles are precisely the principal upsets generated by the join-irreducible elements of Q. In contrast, any task family generating every profile by exact disjunctions must contain all thirteen principal profiles. Thus our exact disjunctive basis has minimum size and is unique at the profile level. 

# 1 Introduction 

# 1.1 Computational landscapes of mobile robots 

The Look –Compute –Move (LCM ) model is a standard abstraction for studying distributed computation by autonomous mobile robots. Robots are anonymous, identical points in the Euclidean plane, each repeatedly observing the configuration, computing a destination, and moving toward it. The classical model, OBLOT , provides neither persistent memory nor explicit communication [SY99]. The luminous model, LUMI , equips robots with persistent visible lights, providing both resources [DFP +16]. Two intermediate models isolate their roles: FSTA robots observe only their own lights and have finite private memory, whereas FCOM robots observe only other robots’ lights and have finite communication without private memory [FSW20]. The activation scheduler supplies a second computational resource. Under full synchrony, all robots execute a cycle in every round; under semi-synchrony, an arbitrary nonempty subset is activated. Re-stricted synchrony limits consecutive activations, while asynchrony permits arbitrary finite delays be-tween phases. Partially atomic variants distinguish delayed computation from observation during move-ment. Cross-model studies have characterized how these timing assumptions interact with memory 1arXiv:2610.17677v1 [cs.DC] 13 Oct 2026 and communication, refining the synchronous landscape and extending it to asynchronous and energy-constrained settings [FSW20, BFK +21, FSSW24, BFK +25]. 

Related computational-landscape studies: The computational-landscape viewpoint has been developed along several orthogonal dimensions, including synchrony, memory, communication and energy constraints [DFP +16, FSW20, BFK +21, BFK +25, FSSW24], and more recently beyond the 

Fsynch –Ssynch –Asynch axis: sequential schedulers activating one robot per round [FFS26b, FFS26a], robots on graphs [DDFN18], and limited-visibility robots, where visibility itself is a computational resource [DGS +24]. 

Why two robots are structurally exceptional: Two robots form the smallest nontrivial population, but their computational landscape is not obtained merely by restricting separators designed for larger populations. Their initial geometry is a single segment, with no third robot to provide an asymmetric neighborhood or a richer geometric reference. Some separations therefore require new witnesses, while some distinctions between internal capabilities disappear. Kitamura, Sudo, and Wada [KSW25] established the complete pairwise computational landscape for two robots under variable disorientation, chirality, and rigid movements. In particular, they proved the full-synchrony collapse FSTA F ≡ FCOM F ≡ LUMI F , including its color- and time-preserving simu-lation, and introduced Dyadic-Multiple Stopped-Distance (DMSD) to separate atomic from unrestricted asynchrony. We use these known results as ingredients. 

From a Venn diagram to a profile lattice: Naito, Ninomiya, and Wada [NNW26] developed this global perspective through a Venn-diagram representation of solvable task sets. A problem’s position records all models that can solve it, exposing interactions that pairwise comparisons do not capture. Their study populated additional regions of the general landscape but left other regions unresolved. We complete this regional classification for the two-robot specialization, after identifying the settings that become computationally equivalent for two robots. Let Q be the resulting thirteen-class order, with X ≤ Y when every task solvable in X is solvable in 

Y . The solvability profile of a task P is Prof (P ) = {X ∈ Q : P ∈ Sol (X)}. Every profile is an upset of Q.Thus the finite distributive lattice Up( Q) describes all membership patterns consistent with the model order. A Venn region with pattern U consists of tasks whose profile is exactly U ; the region is populated only if such a task exists. Counting abstract upsets therefore does not establish that all regions are populated. The central question is whether every upset can be realized by a concrete two-robot task. A task is principal at X if its profile is the principal upset ↑X = {Y ∈ Q : X ≤ Y }. Every upset is the union of the principal upsets generated by its minimal elements, suggesting a task basis indexed by model classes. However, disjoining task specifications need not merely take the union of their profiles. An algorithm can potentially satisfy different disjuncts in different executions without solving any fixed disjunct. Consequently, constructing one principal task per class is not enough: exact realization by disjunction requires a separate common-failure argument. 

# 1.2 Contributions 

Under variable disorientation, chirality, and rigid movements, we construct a minimum exact disjunctive basis for the complete solvability-profile landscape of two robots. All basis tasks share the initial-instance space of two distinct positions. Our results combine an optimal principal representation of the known model order with a complete realization theorem for its profile lattice. 

An optimal principal representation of the model order: We identify six tasks B6 =

{EPG ∗, SRO , MCv , CFSM , VSS , DMSD } whose solvability signatures recover every inclusion and incomparability among the thirteen classes. SRO is inherited from earlier landscape studies [FSW20], MCv from the asynchronous landscape [KSW25], and DMSD from the complete two-robot character-ization [KSW25]. The other three are new or revised here: EPG ∗ (Perpetual Expansion Preserving Gravity) replaces Center of Gravity Expansion [FSW20], whose coordinatewise floor refers to the adversary’s local coordinate systems and therefore does not define a predicate on trajectories under variable disorientation; CFSM is a collision-free refinement of Single Move [KSW25]; and VSS (Visit Swap and Stop) is introduced for the present construction. We prove that six tasks are necessary, even 2when separating tasks need not be principal. Moreover, Q is a lattice with six nonleast join-irreducibles, and these tasks realize exactly the principal profiles generated by them. Those profiles form the unique minimum principal separating family. 

A principal task for every class: We extend B6 to a family B = {BX : X ∈ Q} of thirteen tasks satisfying Prof (BX ) = ↑X for every X ∈ Q . Three tasks come from earlier landscape studies (SRO, MCv and Cv), three from SSS 2025 (HET, ZCC and VEC) [NNW26], and DMSD from the complete two-robot characterization [KSW25]. The remaining six are new or revised here: EPG and EPG ∗, the trajectory-only revisions of Center of Gravity Expansion and its perpetual variant; CFSM, a collision-free refinement of Single Move; OSPO, a strengthened version of Oscillating Points [DFP +16] requiring exactly one nonzero Move per robot per phase; S-RDV (Swap Rendezvous); and VSS. 

Exact disjunctions via common-failure executions: For every antichain A of Q, we prove 

Prof (∨

↑X. The substantive step is the reverse inclusion. For each maximal class outside the target union and each algorithm in that class, we construct a single admissible fair execution violating all selected task predicates. Separate counterexamples to the individual tasks would not suffice. We organize these common-failure executions into families sharing expansion, rotation, or exchange obstructions, combining compatible finite violations with appropriate fair continuations. The resulting exactness is a property of the chosen tasks and their composition, not a consequence of the model order alone. 

Complete realization and minimum basis size: Every upset U is realized by the canonical disjunc-tion BU = ∨  

> X∈Min( U)

BX , so Real( Q) = Up( Q). The antichain polynomial is 1 + 13 z + 24 z2 + 16 z3.Hence there are exactly 53 nonempty profiles: 13 principal profiles, 24 with two minimal classes, and 16 with three. Including the empty profile gives 54. Every nonempty profile has an exact canonical representation using at most three principal tasks, and three are sometimes necessary. Any task family supplying exact disjunctive representations of every profile must contain all thirteen principal profiles. Our basis therefore has minimum size and is unique at the profile level among minimum exact generating families. 

# 2 Models, Tasks and Solvability Profiles 

# 2.1 Robots and Internal Capabilities 

We consider exactly two anonymous, identical, and homogeneous robots R = {a, b } moving in the Euclidean plane R2. The names a and b are used only for analysis; for r ∈ R, ¯ r denotes the other robot. The position of r at time t ≥ 0 is denoted by r(t). Each robot has a local coordinate system centred at its current position. Two attributes of such a system may differ between the robots, namely the orientation of its axes and its unit distance , and both may also change from one activation to the next; only the handedness is fixed, since the robots share chirality. Thus a robot’s local view is obtained from the global frame by a translation, a rotation, and a positive scaling, with the rotation angle and the scaling factor chosen independently for each robot and each activation ( variable disorientation , VD). In particular, no robot can rely on a common direction, on a common unit of length, or on the persistence of either across its own activations. When activated, a robot executes a Look –Compute –Move (LCM ) cycle: it takes an instantaneous snapshot in its local coordinates, computes a destination by a deterministic common algorithm, and moves continuously along the corresponding segment to that destination. Movements are rigid. We assume no general multiplicity-detection capability. We consider four standard capability models. In OBLOT , robots are oblivious and have no explicit communication. In LUMI , each robot has a persistent finite-valued visible light, providing both memory and communication. The intermediate models FSTA and FCOM retain only one of these capabilities: an FSTA robot observes only its own light, which acts as finite private memory, whereas an FCOM robot observes only the peer’s light, providing finite communication but no readable private state. Multiple finite lights, when convenient, are identified with one light whose color set is their Cartesian product. 3For the two-robot setting, let pr be the position of ¯ r in the current local coordinates of r, and let 

cr , c ¯r be their current colors. The information available at a Look is Obs OBLOT (r) = pr , Obs FSTA (r) = ( pr , c r ),

Obs FCOM (r) = ( pr , c ¯r ), Obs LUMI (r) = ( pr , c r , c ¯r ).

These definitions also apply when pr = 0: in the fixed two-robot setting the robots can therefore detect coincidence, and an externally visible peer light remains observable there. This does not amount to general multiplicity detection. In FCOM , the robot never reads its own light; at Compute it may either keep its current color or replace it by a color determined solely by its observation. 1

# 2.2 Schedulers and Executions 

All schedulers are fair: each robot is activated infinitely often. In synchronous executions, time is divided into rounds and each round activates a nonempty subset of the two robots. Under Fsynch both robots are activated in every round, whereas Ssynch allows any nonempty activation set. Under Rsynch , an execution is either fully synchronous throughout, or has a finite fully synchronous prefix followed by alternating singleton activations. Under Asynch , the Look , Compute , and Move phases of different robots may overlap arbitrarily, subject to their local phase order, finite operation durations, and fairness. Movements are continuous and rigid. We also use partially atomic subclasses of Asynch , distinguished by which phase of one robot is protected from a Look of the other: LC -atomic protects the Look–Compute interval, M -atomic protects the Move interval, and CM -atomic protects the Compute–Move interval. The strongest variant, LCM -atomic Asynch , protects the whole cycle and is equivalent to Ssynch . Their formal timing definitions are given in Appendix A.1. For the models considered here, S ≥ ALC ≥ A, S ≥ ACM ≥ AM ≥ A. Moreover, AM and 

ACM are equivalent for OBLOT and FSTA , and also for FCOM [FSSW24]; hence ACM is omitted from the quotient landscape. For compactness, we write F, R, S, A, A LC , A M for Fsynch , Rsynch , Ssynch , Asynch , LC -atomic Asynch , and M -atomic Asynch , respectively. Throughout the paper, unless explicitly stated otherwise, we assume variable disorientation, chirality, and rigid movements. 

# 2.3 Tasks, Solvability, and Profiles 

Let M = {LUMI , FCOM , FSTA , OBLOT } , S = {F, R, S, A, A LC , A M }.

Tasks and executions. A task P = ( IP , ΦP ) consists of a set of admissible initial geometric config-urations and a temporal predicate on the robots’ geometric motion execution, independent of internal colors, states, and local coordinate systems. All basis tasks use the common initial-instance space 

I⋆ = {(pa, p b) ∈ (R2)2 : pa̸ = pb}, and any auxiliary geometric objects are determined from the initial positions. We use Move r (t) to mean that r is inside a nonzero Move at time t, that is, that the Move phase containing t has a destination distinct from the position from which it starts. Throughout such a phase the robot is in motion at every instant: it travels along the segment without ever pausing, and, movements being rigid, it is not halted before the destination either. A robot is therefore at rest at time 

t precisely when t lies in no nonzero Move; we write Stop r (t) for this, so that Stop r (t) ⇐⇒ ¬ Move r (t), and a Move whose destination coincides with the current position leaves r at rest throughout. We write moves( r) for the number of its nonzero Moves and Stopped( r) for eventual permanent stationarity. Their 

> 1All previously established inclusions, equivalences, and separations used in this paper remain valid for exactly two robots under this observation convention and the initial-instance and movement assumptions adopted here [KSW25].

4formal definitions, together with the phase-time conventions used in the appendix proofs, are given in Appendix A.2. For a deterministic algorithm A in model M and scheduler K, let Exec M,K (A, I ) be the set of all admissible fair, time-divergent executions from I, including all permitted choices of schedules, local coordinate systems, phase timings, and movement speeds. Both robots start in the designated initial state or color, with no pending cycle. An algorithm A ∈ Alg( M ) solves P = ( IP , ΦP ) under ( M, K ) if ∀I ∈ I P ∀ρ ∈ Exec M,K (A, I ) : 

πgeom (ρ) |= Φ P , where πgeom (ρ) denotes the geometric motion execution obtained from ρ by forgetting internal states, colors, and local coordinate systems. The task P is solvable under ( M, K ) if such an algorithm exists: P ∈ Sol (M, K ) ⇐⇒ ∃A ∈ Alg( M ) : A solves P under ( M, K ).

We abbreviate πgeom (ρ) |= Φ P by ρ |= Φ P , and write Φ i for Φ Pi when the indexing family of tasks is clear from the context. For a setting μ = ( M, K ), also written M K , we write Sol (μ) = Sol (M, K ). 

# 2.4 Computational Order, Solvability Profiles and Disjunction of Tasks 

For settings μ, ν , define μ ≥ ν ⇐⇒ Sol (μ) ⊇ Sol (ν), μ ≡ ν ⇐⇒ Sol (μ) = Sol (ν). Strict comparison and incomparability have their usual meanings. Let M = M × S be the set of settings, and let Q = M/≡ be the quotient poset of computational equivalence classes, ordered by X ≤ Y ⇐⇒ Sol (X) ⊆ Sol (Y ).

For S ⊆ Q , let Min( S) and Max( S) denote its minimal and maximal elements, and let Ant( Q) be the family of antichains. For a task P , its solvability profile is Prof (P ) = {X ∈ Q : P ∈ Sol (X)}. Every solvability profile is an upset of Q. For X ∈ Q , write ↑X = {Y ∈ Q : X ≤ Y }. A task is principal at X if Prof (P ) = ↑X.A family B = {BX : X ∈ Q} is a principal task basis if Prof (BX ) = ↑X for every X. It is an exact disjunctive basis if, for every A ∈ Ant( Q), Prof (∨

Proposition 2.1. For every task P , Prof (P ) is an upset of Q.

The family of abstract profiles is therefore the finite distributive lattice Up( Q) under union and intersection. We write Real (Q) = {Prof (P ) : P is a two-robot task } ⊆ Up( Q).

Proposition 2.2. For every U ∈ Up( Q), U = ⋃

↑X, and Min( U ) is the unique antichain giving an irredundant representation of U by principal upsets. Consequently, antichains and upsets are in bijection.

P ∈ Sol (X) for every X ∈ Min( U ),P / ∈ Sol (Y ) for every Y ∈ Max( Q \ U ).

# 3 The Two-Robot Quotient and Minimum Separation 

# 3.1 Basic Inclusions and Known Equivalences 

Proposition 3.1. For every scheduler K and model M ,

LUMI K ≥ FSTA K ≥ OBLOT K , LUMI K ≥ FCOM K ≥ OBLOT K ,

5and M F ≥ M R ≥ M S , M S ≥ M ALC ≥ M A, M S ≥ M ACM ≥ M AM ≥ M A.

Proposition 3.2. FSTA A ≡ FSTA ALC , OBLOT A ≡ OBLOT ALC ,

FSTA AM ≡ FSTA ACM , OBLOT AM ≡ OBLOT ACM .

We also use the known equivalences LUMI R ≡ LUMI A ≡ FCOM R [DFP +16, FSW20, KSW25] ,

and FCOM A ≡ FCOM AM , FCOM ALC ≡ FCOM S [KSW25] . The elementary proofs of the two propositions are deferred to Appendix B.1. The following two-robot full-synchrony collapse follows from the stronger color- and round-preserving simulation of [KSW25], recalled in Appendix B.2. 

Theorem 3.3 (Computational collapse under full synchrony) . For exactly two robots under the assump-tions above, LUMI F ≡ FCOM F ≡ FSTA F .

The proof is deferred to Appendix B.2. 

# 3.2 The Thirteen Classes and Their Signatures 

Combining the basic capability and scheduler inclusions with the known equivalences and the two-robot full-synchrony collapse above yields the thirteen computational classes considered below. To distinguish these classes, we use the six principal tasks 

B6 = {EPG ∗, SRO , MCv , CFSM , VSS , DMSD }. Three tasks deserve explicit specification here. EPG ∗ and VSS are introduced in this paper, while CFSM is a collision-free refinement of Single Move. The remaining separators SRO [FSW20], MCv [KSW25], and DMSD [KSW25] are taken from earlier landscape results; their specifications and references are recalled in Appendix C. 

Perpetual Expansion Preserving Gravity ( EPG ∗): Let c0 = a(0)+ b(0) 2 , and let Ci = ( ai, b i) be the sequence of jointly stationary configurations with consecutive repetitions suppressed. The task requires this sequence to be infinite and, for every i ≥ 0, ai+1 +bi+1  

> 2

= c0, b i+1 − ai+1 = 2( bi − ai).

Collision-Free Single Move ( CFSM ): CFSM strengthens Single Move [FSSW24]: each robot must perform exactly one nonzero Move, and the robots must never collide. Thus moves( a) = moves( b) = 1, a (t)̸ = b(t) for all t ≥ 0.

Visit Swap and Stop ( VSS ): Let a0 = a(0) and b0 = b(0), and set A0 = 2 a0 − b0, B 0 = 2 b0 − a0. VSS requires the prescribed exchange phase to reach one of ( B0, b 0), (a0, A 0), (B0, A 0), after which the robots must eventually remain permanently stationary at one of the four designated configurations determined by A0, a 0, b 0, B 0. Every Move ends in {A0, a 0, b 0, B 0}; the complete move-count and terminal conditions are given in Appendix D.7. Table 1 records, for each class, which of these six tasks are solvable. Thus each row is a six-bit solvability signature of that class. Since the thirteen signatures are distinct, B6 separates all thirteen computational classes. 

Lemma 3.4 (Bounds for the six separators) . Under the assumptions of this paper, the following hold. 

(1) EPG ∗ ∈ Sol (OBLOT F ) and EPG ∗ /∈ Sol (LUMI R).

(2) SRO ∈ Sol (OBLOT R) and SRO /∈ Sol (FCOM S ) ∪ Sol (FSTA S ).

(3) MCv ∈ Sol (OBLOT S ) and MCv /∈ Sol (FCOM A) ∪ Sol (FSTA AM ).

(4) CFSM ∈ Sol (FSTA A) and CFSM /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ) ∪ Sol (FCOM S ).

(5) VSS ∈ Sol (FCOM A) and VSS /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ) ∪ Sol (FSTA R).

(6) DMSD ∈ Sol (OBLOT AM ) and DMSD /∈ Sol (FSTA A).Proof. Items (1)–(6) are proved in Appendices D.2, C.1, C.2, D.4, D.7 and C.6, respectively. 6Theorem 3.5 (The thirteen-class order from six signatures) . Write σ(μ) = {P ∈ B 6 : P ∈ Sol (μ)}. The settings under consideration form exactly thirteen computational equivalence classes, represented by the rows of Table 1, and for any two representatives μ, ν ,

μ ≥ ν ⇐⇒ σ(μ) ⊇ σ(ν).

The certificate is given in Appendix H.1. 

Theorem 3.6 (Six principal separators) . The tasks in B6 are principal, with 

Prof (EPG ∗) = ↑OBLOT F , Prof (SRO) = ↑OBLOT R,

Prof (MCv) = ↑OBLOT S , Prof (CFSM) = ↑FSTA A,

Prof (VSS) = ↑FCOM A, Prof (DMSD) = ↑OBLOT AM .

Proof. For each task, the positive and maximal negative bounds stated above, together with upward closure of solvability, determine exactly the corresponding principal upset. 

# 3.3 Minimum Separation 

Theorem 3.7. Any family of tasks separating all thirteen classes of Q contains at least six tasks. Hence 

B6 is minimum. Proof. The quotient contains the chain 

OBLOT A < OBLOT AM < OBLOT S < OBLOT R < FSTA R < LUMI R < LUMI F .

Along a chain, the solvability set of each task is an upper interval and hence can create at most one cut. Distinguishing the seven elements therefore requires at least six tasks. Table 1 shows that B6 achieves this bound. 

# 3.4 The Minimum Principal Separator Family 

Among principal profiles, the minimum separating family is unique: its generators are precisely the join-irreducible elements of Q.

Proposition 3.8. The unique minimum family of principal upsets separating all elements of Q is 

{↑OBLOT F , ↑OBLOT R, ↑OBLOT S , ↑FCOM A, ↑FSTA A, ↑OBLOT AM }.

Equivalently, the profiles of the six tasks in B6 form the unique minimum principal separating family. 

The lattice-theoretic characterization of Q, including its join-irreducible elements and width, is given in Appendix H. 

# 4 A Principal Task Basis for the Thirteen Classes 

Section 3 showed that six principal tasks suffice, and are necessary, to separate the thirteen computational classes. We now extend this family to one task BX for every X ∈ Q and prove that each BX is principal at X: ( Prof (BX ) = ↑X). Thus the thirteen tasks realize all principal upsets of the quotient. The required solvability and impossibility bounds are summarized in Table 2; full task specifications and proofs are deferred to the appendices. 

# 4.1 Principal Tasks and Boundary Certificates

# 4.2 The Principal Task Basis 

Write X6 = {OBLOT F , OBLOT R, OBLOT S , FSTA A, FCOM A, OBLOT AM } for the generators of B6

and X7 = Q \ X 6. Table 2 gives the complete assignment and the corresponding boundary certificates. 

Boundary results for the seven additional tasks. All thirteen tasks use I⋆. Their specifications and boundary proofs are collected in Appendices C and D, with references in Table 2. For Cv, the entry “Trivial” refers only to the empty negative boundary of the least class OBLOT A; the Go-to-midpoint algorithm is proved correct in Appendix C.7. 

Expansion Preserving Gravity ( EPG ): EPG is the one-shot version of EPG ∗: preserving the initial center of gravity at joint stops, the robots double the initial segment once and then stop forever. Its formal specification and bounds are given in Appendix D.1. 8Oscillating Points with One Move per Robot per Phase ( OSPO ): OSPO is a strengthened version of Oscillating Points [DFP +16]. Starting from distinct positions, the robots must alternate approach and retreat forever without ever colliding. The first approach phase starts at time 0, and every phase is delimited by joint stops. Within each phase, the distance is monotone and changes strictly between its endpoints, and each robot completes exactly one nonzero Move . The phase boundaries must tend to infinity. The full specification and boundary proofs are given in Appendix D.3. 

Lemma 4.1 (Bounds for OSPO) . Under the assumptions of this paper, OSPO ∈ Sol (FSTA R), whereas 

OSPO /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ) ∪ Sol (FSTA S ) ∪ Sol (FCOM S ).

Proof. The solvability algorithm in FSTA R and the three impossibility arguments are given in Ap-pendix D.3. 

Swap–Rendezvous ( S-RDV ): The two robots must achieve the common exchange first phase of Ap-pendix D.5, namely form one of the three configurations in which their order on the initial line has been reversed, and must then gather at a single point and stay there, never leaving the segment [ A, B ] de-termined by the initial positions. Its exchange phase is shared with VSS; their subsequent requirements differ. The full specification is Definition D.24 in Appendix D.6. 

Lemma 4.2 (Bounds for S-RDV) . Under the assumptions of this paper, S-RDV ∈ Sol (FCOM S ),

whereas S-RDV /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ) ∪ Sol (FSTA R) ∪ Sol (FCOM A).

Proof. The solvability algorithm in FCOM S and the three impossibility arguments are given in Ap-pendix D.6. 

# 4.3 Principal-Basis Theorem 

Theorem 4.3 (A principal task basis for the thirteen classes) . Let B = {BX : X ∈ Q} be the family specified in Table 2. Under the model assumptions and observation convention of this paper, every task 

BX has initial-instance space I⋆ and satisfies Prof (BX ) = ↑X. Consequently, B is a principal task basis consisting of thirteen tasks and extending the six-task separating family B6.Proof. If X ∈ X 6, the identity Prof (BX ) = ↑X is exactly one of the six statements of Theorem 3.6. If X ∈ X 7, the task-specific results referenced in Table 2, including Corollary D.7, establish solvability at X and impossibility at every class in NX . Applying Lemma 2.3 gives Prof (BX ) = ↑X. This also covers X = OBLOT A, where NX = ∅.Since Q = X6 ˙∪ X 7 and all tasks use I⋆, the conclusion follows. The six-task family determines the computational order, whereas the thirteen-task basis realizes ev-ery principal upset. These are distinct roles: the minimality of the former does not make the seven additional principal profiles redundant. Nor do the individual identities above establish exactness of task disjunctions. # 5 Representative Common-Failure Construction 

Section 4 established each principal profile individually. Refuting a disjunction requires one admissible fair execution on which all selected predicates fail. We fix an arbitrary deterministic algorithm A and an initial instance ι ∈ I ⋆; all predicates refer to this same instance, and execution blocks preserve the reached states and pending operations without reset. For the FSTA constructions below, normalization means presenting the peer at e = (1 , 0) at every Look with distinct positions, using the rotations and rescalings permitted by VD. The destination in these normalized coordinates and the next private state then depend only on the current private state. Finite safety violations persist under extension; liveness violations require a suitable fair infinite continuation. 95.1 Disjunctions with Perpetual Expansion 

We group the pairs EPG ∗ ∨ P with P ∈ C exp := {HET , OSPO , S-RDV , ZCC , VSS , VEC , CFSM }. Let 

C0, C 1, . . . be the joint-stop configurations in chronological order with consecutive repetitions removed, and put c0 = 12 (a(0) + b(0)): EPG ∗ asks this sequence to be infinite and every step of it to keep the center of gravity at c0 while doubling the segment. Keeping the center of gravity is exactly what rules out a solo expansion, and this is the component shared by all seven pairs. 

Lemma 5.1. Let ρ be an admissible execution with joint-stop sequence (Ci)i≥0.1. If one of the two robots completes no nonzero Move between the joint stop realizing Ci and the one realizing Ci+1 , then 12 (ai+1 + bi+1 )̸ = 12 (ai + bi).

2. Let K ∈ { R, S, A LC , A M , A }. For every algorithm there is a fair execution in which the LCM cycles of the two robots are pairwise disjoint in time. In such an execution either no nonzero Move ever occurs, in which case (Ci) has the single term C0 and the perpetuity condition fails, or  

> 12

(a1 + b1)̸ = c0 and the expansion condition fails at i = 0 . In both cases ΦEPG ∗ fails, and so does 

ΦEPG .

For (1), only one endpoint changes between two distinct joint-stop configurations, so their midpoints differ. For (2), alternate complete cycles of a and b, assigning each cycle unit duration. The execution is fair and time-divergent, belongs to R and S, and respects every atomicity constraint because cycles do not overlap. Either no robot moves and progress fails, or the first nonzero Move changes the midpoint by (1). Formal timing details are in Appendix E.6. 

Combining the obstruction with another failure. A solo first nonzero Move suffices to violate EPG ∗; later cycles need not remain serialized. To refute EPG ∗ ∨ P , we must also violate P on that same execution, from the state actually reached. An admissible fair tail preserves finite safety violations, whereas a liveness failure requires a specifically constructed fair continuation. The next proposition supplies a common execution for both exchange tasks. 

Proposition 5.2 (Perpetual expansion and exchange tasks) . For every deterministic algorithm in 

FSTA R, there is an admissible fair execution that violates EPG ∗, S-RDV , and VSS simultaneously. In particular, for each E ∈ { S-RDV , VSS },

EPG ∗ ∨ E / ∈ Sol (FSTA R).

Proof. Use coordinates on the initial line with a0 = d, b0 = 2 d, d > 0, so the outer points are 0 , 3d

and c0 = 3 d/ 2. Activate a, b alternately, completing each cycle before the next one starts; this is an admissible fair R execution with an empty fully synchronous prefix. Under normalized observations, the two robots traverse the same private state sequence until the first nonzero Move .If no such Move occurs, all three tasks fail their progress requirements. Otherwise a performs the first nonzero Move alone, changing the center of gravity at the next joint stop and violating EPG ∗.The shared exchange phase requires one of (3 d, 2d), (d, 0) , (3 d, 0) with cumulative nonzero-move counts (1 , 0) , (0 , 1) , (1 , 1), respectively. Thus this first solo Move of a must land at 3 d: any other landing would require another nonzero Move of a to reach an admissible exchange configuration, exceeding its allowed count. If a lands at 3 d, then b, at its next activation, has the same private state as a had before that Move 

and receives the same normalized input. Hence it performs the same normalized displacement, which in its current physical frame is twice its current distance to the peer, and therefore lands at 2 d + 2 d = 4 d.This lies outside the segment [0 , 3d] required by S-RDV and outside the allowed endpoint set {0, d, 2d, 3d}

of VSS. In either case both exchange tasks fail irreversibly. Continue with fair alternating activations; all three violations persist. 10 The remaining companions in Cexp use the same solo expansion obstruction, with task- and class-specific prefixes or fair tails. These applicability and continuation arguments are recorded in Ap-pendix F.1. The complete boundary coverage and the resulting profile equalities are assembled in Section 6; the obstruction alone does not supply those companion-task arguments. 

# 5.2 Shrinking Rotation and Exchange Tasks 

Let E = {S-RDV , VSS }. The two tasks share the exchange phase, so a construction that defeats the shared phase defeats both at once. 

Proposition 5.3 (Shrinking rotation and exchange tasks) . For every deterministic algorithm in FSTA S

there is an admissible fair execution that violates SRO and both tasks in E simultaneously. Consequently 

SRO ∨ E / ∈ Sol (FSTA S ) for every E ∈ E .Proof sketch. We first recall the geometric obstruction. SRO permits a clockwise 90 ◦ rotation of the segment, or a clockwise 45 ◦ rotation with scale 1 /√2. With the peer fixed at (0 , 0) and the mov-ing robot initially at ( −1, 0), the four possible two-step choices place the moving robot at one of (1 , 0) , (1 /2, 1/2) , (0 , 1/2) . All lie outside the square with the initial segment as diagonal, contrary to SRO’s containment condition. Thus two consecutive solo nonzero moves defeat SRO (Lemma E.19). Use the same initial positions and normalized observations as in Proposition 5.2. If the initial state orbit contains no nonzero output, a fair alternating execution never moves and violates all three tasks. Otherwise activate a alone until its first nonzero Move ; S permits any finite number of solo activations. If a lands at 3 d, the segment reverses without changing length, violating SRO. Activating b through its preceding zero-output states then reproduces the move to 4 d, violating both exchange tasks. For any other landing, both exchange tasks have already failed. Unless SRO also fails immediately, the robots remain distinct. If a has another nonzero output, execute it with b fixed and apply the two-move obstruction. Otherwise let b perform its corresponding first nonzero Move . Either this violates SRO, or both robots now follow the same zero-output state suffix. Fair alternating activations then leave the configuration stationary forever, violating SRO’s perpetuity. In the finite-violation cases, append a fair alternating suffix. Appendix F.2 gives the complete argument. This covers the entire maximal negative boundary of SRO ∨ VSS and the FSTA S boundary of SRO ∨ S-RDV; the latter pair also has the boundary class FCOM A, treated in Appendix F.2. 

# 6 Exact Disjunctions via Common-Failure Executions 

Section 4 established the individual principal profiles, and Section 5 exhibited representative executions violating several task predicates simultaneously. We now combine those constructions with the remaining boundary cases in the appendix to prove exactness for every antichain-indexed disjunction. The argument separates the execution constructions from the order-theoretic step that identifies the resulting profiles.

The matching lower bound is a property of the profile lattice alone and does not assume that a competing family consists of principal tasks. 

Theorem 7.2 (Minimality of the principal-upset basis) . Let G ⊆ Up( Q) be a family whose unions generate every element of Up( Q). Then ↑X ∈ G for every X ∈ Q . Consequently {↑ X : X ∈ Q} is the unique least union-generating family of profiles, of cardinality thirteen. Proof. Write ↑X = ⋃ 

> G∈G X

G with GX ⊆ G . Some G ∈ G X contains X, and being an upset it contains 

↑X; the displayed equality gives the reverse inclusion, so G = ↑X. Conversely the principal upsets generate every upset by canonical decomposition, and they are distinct. 

Corollary 7.3 (Minimum size and uniqueness at the profile level) . Any task family D supplying an exact representation 2 of every upset of Q contains at least thirteen tasks. The family B attains this bound, and if |D| = 13 its task profiles are exactly {↑ X : X ∈ Q} , each occurring once. Proof. The profiles {Prof (P ) : P ∈ D} generate every upset by union, so Theorem 7.2 forces all thirteen principal upsets to occur; B attains the bound by Theorem 7.1. Uniqueness here concerns the profiles, not the predicates realizing them: a task may be replaced by another with the same profile, though this need not preserve exactness of disjunctions. Nor does the lower bound claim minimality among families whose disjunctions may have profiles strictly larger than the union of their components.

By the antichain–upset correspondence and Theorem 7.1, the antichain polynomial 1 + 13 z + 24 z2 + 16 z3

counts the realizable profiles: thirteen principal, twenty-four with two minimal classes, sixteen with three, and the empty one, so |Real (Q)| = | Up( Q)| = 54. Every nonempty profile therefore has an exact canonical expression using at most three tasks from B, and three are sometimes necessary: for the three-element antichain A0 = {OBLOT F , FSTA R, FCOM S } the upset U0 = ⋃ 

> X∈A0

↑X has exactly these three minimal elements and canonical representative BU0 = EPG ∗ ∨OSPO ∨S-RDV , and no union of two principal profiles equals U0 by uniqueness of the irredundant decomposition. This bounds the number of principal terms, not the number of arbitrary tasks needed to realize a given profile. # 8 Conclusion 

We completely characterize the two-robot solvability-profile landscape: all 54 upsets of Q are realizable. Minimum separation requires six principal tasks, whereas minimum exact disjunctive generation requires thirteen.

# A.1 Formal Definition of Schedulers 

We define the schedulers considered in this paper for the two-robot system R = {a, b }. All schedulers are assumed to be fair: each robot performs infinitely many LCM cycles. For a robot r ∈ R and i ≥ 1, let tL(r, i ), tC (r, i ), tB (r, i ), and tE (r, i ) denote the times at which 

r performs its i-th Look , Compute , begins its i-th Move , and ends its i-th Move , respectively. We assume tL(r, i ) < t C (r, i ) < t B (r, i ) < t E (r, i ) < t L(r, i + 1) . The Look and Compute operations are instantaneous, while every Move operation has finite duration. Movements are continuous and rigid: if 

r computes destination pE from its current position pB , then it moves along the segment [ pB , p E ] during [tB (r, i ), t E (r, i )] and reaches pE at time tE (r, i ). A synchronous execution is described by an infinite activation sequence E1, E 2, . . . , where each Ej ∈{{a}, {b}, {a, b }} is the set of robots activated in round j. Robots in Ej perform one complete LCM 

cycle synchronously, while robots not in Ej remain inactive. The fully synchronous scheduler Fsynch is the case where Ej = {a, b } for all j. The semi-synchronous scheduler Ssynch allows any nonempty activation set in each round, sub-ject only to fairness. The restricted synchronous scheduler Rsynch consists of Fsynch or an optional finite prefix of fully synchronous rounds followed by alternating singleton activations: 

{a}, {b}, {a}, {b}, . . . or {b}, {a}, {b}, {a}, . . . . 

In the asynchronous scheduler Asynch , there are no rounds. The adversary chooses the times 

tL, t C , t B , t E and the duration of each Move operation arbitrarily, subject only to the ordering constraint above, finite duration of operations, and fairness. We also consider atomic subclasses of Asynch . Since there are only two robots, these subclasses can be defined by forbidding one robot from performing a Look operation during certain intervals of the other robot’s cycle. For all r ∈ R and all i, j ≥ 1, we define: • LC -atomic Asynch : tL(r, j ) /∈ (tL(r, i ), t C (r, i )] .

• M -atomic Asynch : tL(r, j ) /∈ [tB (r, i ), t E (r, i )] .

• CM -atomic Asynch : tL(r, j ) /∈ [tC (r, i ), t E (r, i )] .

• LCM -atomic Asynch : tL(r, j ) /∈ (tL(r, i ), t E (r, i )] .

The LCM -atomic Asynch scheduler is equivalent to Ssynch . In the OBLOT and FSTA models, 

M -atomic Asynch and CM -atomic Asynch are equivalent because robots cannot observe the other robot’s color. The same equivalence is known for FCOM robots [FSSW24]. Hence we focus on M -atomic Asynch and omit CM -atomic Asynch from the landscape. 

# A.2 Formal Definition of Temporal Predicates 

A colored configuration at time t is C(t) = ((a(t), color a(t)) , (b(t), color b(t)) ), where the ordering is used only for analysis. Its geometric and color components are ( a(t), b (t)) and (color a(t), color b(t)), respectively. In OBLOT , the color set is a singleton. For a fixed execution, we use the following predicates, suppressing its name from the notation. For each robot r ∈ { a, b }, its moving-time set and moving predicate are MoveSet( r) := ⋃  

> i≥1:
> r(tB(r,i )) ̸=r(tE(r,i ))

(tB (r, i ), t E (r, i )), Move r (t) :⇐⇒ t ∈ MoveSet( r),

16 the union being taken over the Move operations of r whose starting and ending positions differ. A robot is stationary exactly when it is not inside such a Move: 

Stop r (t) :⇐⇒ t / ∈ MoveSet( r).

Since MoveSet( r) is a union of open intervals, the endpoints of nonzero Move intervals are included among the stopping times. Writing the open set MoveSet( r) as the disjoint union of its maximal open intervals, we define the number of nonzero moves by MoveSet( r) = 

> Nr

⊔

> k=1

I(k) 

> r

, moves( r) := Nr ∈ Z≥0 ∪ {∞} ,

and, for t ≥ 0, the number of nonzero Moves of r completed by time t by 

mr (t) := ∣∣{ k ≤ Nr : sup I(k) 

> r

≤ t }∣∣.

Thus mr is non-decreasing with mr (0) = 0, and moves( r) = lim t→∞ mr (t). The strict separation between successive Move operations ensures that each such interval corresponds to one nonzero Move operation. Permanent stopping is expressed by Stopped( r) :⇐⇒ ∃T ≥ 0 ∀t ≥ T : Stop r (t).

Finally, the joint stops of the execution are the times at which neither robot is moving: Σab := { t ≥ 0 : Stop a(t) ∧ Stop b(t) } = [0 , ∞) \ (MoveSet( a) ∪ MoveSet( b)).

This set is closed in [0 , ∞) and contains 0, since no cycle is pending at time 0. The geometric configuration (a(t), b (t)) is constant on each maximal subinterval of Σ ab . Listing these configurations in chronological order and suppressing consecutive repetitions gives the joint-stop sequence C0, C 1, . . . of the execution, with Ci = ( ai, b i) and di = bi − ai.

and hence Y ∈ Prof (P ).

> X∈Min( U)

> X∈A

Proof of Lemma 2.3. Necessity is immediate. For sufficiency, every Z ∈ U lies above some X ∈ Min( U ), since Q is finite. Thus P ∈ Sol (Z) by Proposition 2.1. Every Z / ∈ U lies below some Y ∈ Max( Q \ U ). If 

P were solvable in Z, it would also be solvable in Y , since Z ≤ Y , contradicting the assumed impossibility at Y . Hence Prof (P ) = U .

# B.1 Basic inclusions 

Proof of Proposition 3.1. The cross-model inclusions follow by ignoring capabilities: a LUMI robot can execute an FSTA or FCOM algorithm, and either intermediate model can execute an OBLOT 

algorithm. The scheduler inclusions follow by restricting admissible executions. If every schedule and timing choice permitted by K1 is also permitted by K2, then any algorithm correct under K2 remains correct under K1. Thus, Sol (M, K 2) ⊆ Sol (M, K 1). Applying this observation to the scheduler restrictions in the model definitions proves the stated inclusions. 

Proof of Proposition 3.2. These equivalences follow directly from the observation capabilities of FSTA 

and OBLOT and from the definitions of the corresponding atomic scheduler classes. 

# B.2 Correctness of the Color- and Round-Preserving Simulation 

The following color- and round-preserving full-synchrony simulation for two robots was established in [KSW25]. 

Theorem B.1 ([KSW25]) . Let A be a deterministic two-robot LUMI F algorithm with a palette C of 

k colors and a designated initial color c0. There are algorithms AS in FSTA F and AC in FCOM F ,both using the palette C, such that, for every initial geometric configuration I and every execution ρ

of either simulator from I, there is an admissible execution ̂ρ of A from I with π geom (ρ) = πgeom (̂ρ).

The matching execution has the same round boundaries and movement timings. Each simulator round implements exactly one round of A, without auxiliary colors or initialization rounds. Proof. Write 

A(p, c r , c ¯r ) = ( q, c ′).

Fix e = (0 , 1). For p̸ = 0, let Np be the orientation-preserving similarity mapping p to e, and let N0 = id. 

Construction. Both simulators start with c0. In each round, a robot with peer position p reads the available color c: its own color in FSTA F , or its peer’s color in FCOM F . It computes (q, c ′) = A(Np(p), c, c ),

sets its state or external light to c′, and moves to N −1 

> p

(q) in its actual local coordinates. This uses only permitted observations and the palette C; Np is computed from the current snapshot and is not stored between rounds. 

Correctness. Fix an arbitrary execution ρ of either simulator. We construct a matching execution ̂ρ

of the LUMI F algorithm A by induction over rounds. At the beginning of round j, we maintain the following invariant: the corresponding robots in ρ and ̂

ρ occupy the same positions, and the two robots have a common color c j . The invariant holds initially by the designated common initial color. Let ψr,j be the map from global coordinates to robot r’s actual local coordinates at its Look in round 

j, and let pr,j be the observed peer position in these coordinates. In the corresponding execution of A,choose the virtual local coordinate system ̂

ψr,j = Npr,j ◦ ψr,j .

Because Npr,j is an orientation-preserving similarity, ̂ ψr,j is admissible under variable disorientation and preserves chirality. 18 By the induction hypothesis, the geometric configuration and the two colors at the beginning of the round coincide in the two executions. Hence, under the virtual coordinate system, each source robot receives exactly the normalized input used by the simulator. In particular, when the robots are at distinct positions, both receive (e, c j , c j ),

whereas at coincidence both receive (0 , c j , c j ).

Therefore, determinism of A implies that both corresponding computations produce the same next color 

cj+1 .Let qj be the destination computed by A in the normalized coordinates. The global destination of robot r in ̂ρ is ̂

ψ−1 

> r,j

(qj ) = ψ−1

> r,j

(N −1 

> pr,j

(qj )),

which is exactly the destination used by the simulator. We now choose the same round boundaries, phase timings, and progress along each rigid movement segment in ̂ρ as in ρ. Thus the two executions have identical geometric trajectories throughout round j,and the common-color invariant is preserved for round j + 1. Induction over all rounds therefore yields an admissible infinite execution ̂ρ of A satisfying 

πgeom (ρ) = πgeom (̂ρ).

Moreover, each simulator round implements exactly one round of A, and no auxiliary colors or initial-ization rounds are introduced. Hence the simulator uses the same palette and preserves the round and movement timings claimed in the theorem. 

Scope of the construction. The correspondence constructed above maps every simulator execution to an admissible execution of the original LUMI F algorithm with the same geometric trajectory. The virtual local coordinate systems of the source execution need not coincide with those of the simulator. The construction also relies on the designated common initial color; it does not address arbitrary initial color assignments. 

Proof of Theorem 3.3. The quantitative simulation theorem (Theorem B.1) shows that every LUMI F

algorithm can be simulated in both FSTA F and FCOM F , preserving the geometric execution. Hence 

Sol (LUMI F ) ⊆ Sol (FSTA F ) ∩ Sol (FCOM F ).

The reverse inclusions follow immediately because FSTA and FCOM are submodels of LUMI .

# C Definitions and Boundary Proofs of the Geometric Basis Tasks 

This appendix collects the formal specifications of the geometric basis tasks and the boundary proofs cited in Section 4.2. 

# C.1 Shrinking Rotation (SRO) 

This subsection records the formal specification of SRO and proves Lemma 3.4 (2). 

Definition C.1 (Shrinking Rotation) . Two robots a, b start from distinct positions. Let Σ ab be the set of times at which neither robot is inside a nonzero Move operation, list the configurations at the times of Σab in chronological order, and remove consecutive repetitions; call the resulting sequence C0, C 1, C 2, . . . 

with Ci = ( ai, b i). Put di := bi − ai, let Qi be the square with diagonal aibi, interior included, and set 19 Q−1 := R2. Let Rϕ denote the counterclockwise rotation by ϕ. The first condition (perpetuity) requires the sequence ( Ci) to be infinite. The second condition (rotation) requires that for every i the vector di+1 

be the clockwise 90 ◦ rotation of di (C1 ), or its clockwise 45 ◦ rotation scaled by 1 /√2 ( C2 ), and that 

ai+1 , b i+1 ∈ Qi−1 (C3 ). ΦSRO ≡ [ { (Ci)i≥0 is infinite }

and { ∀ i ≥ 0 : (di+1 = R−90 ◦ di or di+1 = 1√2 R−45 ◦ di

)

∧ (ai+1 , b i+1 ∈ Qi−1

) } ]

Here Σ ab is the set of joint stops of Definition C.24. The reference [FSW20] does not make the sequence ( Ci) explicit. We define ( Ci) as the configurations at the times at which neither robot is inside a nonzero Move , so that the intermediate configurations in which one robot has landed while the other is still in flight are excluded. With this convention each term of ( Ci) has both robots at their initial positions or at the landing points of their last completed nonzero Move operations, and the sequence does not depend on how the adversary distributes the movement speed inside a Move operation. In the proofs below we write c1(γ) and c2(γ) for the solo and simultaneous contraction ratios of Lemma E.2; these are not to be confused with the configurations C1, C 2 of the sequence ( Ci) or with the conditions 

C1 , C2 .

Impossibility Lemma C.2 ([FSW20, Lemma 23]) . SRO /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. Run a fully synchronous prefix, which is admissible under Ssynch . By Lemma E.14 the two robots always carry the same color. If λγ = 0 for every color γ occurring in that common sequence, this fair execution contains no nonzero Move , the configuration never changes, the sequence ( Ci) reduces to 

C0 alone, and the first condition fails. Otherwise let γ be the first color with λγ > 0 and truncate the prefix at the round in which both robots carry γ; the configuration is still the initial one. Now activate a alone twice in a row (Lemma E.4; under Ssynch the robot that is not activated has no pending operation), and write Ci for the configuration at the beginning of this interval. The visible state of an FCOM robot is the color of its peer, and b is not activated, so that color is constant; by Lemma E.16(b) the displacement rule of a is the single pair ( λγ , θ γ ), and λγ > 0 makes every activation a nonzero Move . If c1(γ) = 0 and a reaches the position of b, then d = 0 in that configuration and neither C1 nor C2 can hold, so the second condition fails. Otherwise d > 0 is preserved. For the first move to satisfy the second condition it must satisfy the rotation condition, and with the peer at rest only two displacements do so, one of type C1 and one of type C2 (Lemma E.19). The rule being single, the second move is of the same type, and among the four computations of that lemma the two relevant ones both give ai+2 /∈ Qi, so C3 fails. 

Lemma C.3 ([FSW20, Lemma 23]) . SRO /∈ Sol (FSTA S ).Proof. Under Ssynch the robot that is not activated has no pending operation, so the hypotheses of Lemma E.4 hold at every round boundary. The visible state of an FSTA robot is its own color, so in the normalized frame of Lemma E.1(1) the color sequence of each robot is c0, g (c0), g 2(c0), . . . 

independently of the schedule, and it is eventually periodic with preperiod p and period q (Lemma E.11). The displacement of each activation is likewise determined by the color through ( λγ , θ γ ). 

The periodic part contains a color with λγ > 0. Activating a alone p + 2 q times (Lemma E.4) makes a

perform at least two nonzero Move operations while b stays at rest; write Ci for the configuration at the beginning of this interval. Zero Move operations do not advance the sequence ( Ci), so these correspond to two consecutive terms of ( Ci). If at some point c1(γ) = 0 and a reaches the position of b, then d = 0 in that configuration and neither C1 nor C2 can hold, so the second condition fails. Otherwise d > 0 is preserved and the same robot performs two consecutive nonzero Move operations with its peer at rest, so by Lemma E.19 the rotation condition and C3 cannot both be satisfied and the second condition fails. 20 Suppose now that the periodic part contains no color with λγ > 0. After the preperiod the color sequence stays in the periodic part, where λγ = 0, so each robot performs at most p nonzero Move 

operations whatever the schedule. Hence the configuration changes at only finitely many times, the sequence ( Ci) is finite, and the first condition fails. 

Solvability Lemma C.4 ([BFK +25, Lemma 2]) . SRO ∈ Sol (OBLOT R).Proof. Each robot moves to the point obtained by rotating its own position clockwise by 90 ◦ about the midpoint of the two robots. If both robots are activated simultaneously, the segment is rotated by 90 ◦

and C1 holds; if only one robot is activated, the segment is scaled by 1 /√2 and rotated by 45 ◦, and C2 

holds. An Rsynch execution is either fully synchronous throughout, which is the case treated in [FSW20, Lemma 22], or a finite fully synchronous prefix followed by alternating singleton activations (Lemma E.8). Fully synchronous rounds preserve the square. If such a round is followed by a singleton round, both new positions lie in the same square. For two consecutive alternating singleton rounds, consider the square immediately before the first round: the first mover reaches one of its vertices, and the second reaches its centre. Thus ai+1 , b i+1 ∈ Qi−1 for every i ≥ 1; the case i = 0 is vacuous since Q−1 = R2. Hence C3 

holds as well. The destination is determined by the midpoint and the rotation alone and requires no unit of length, so it is computable in each robot’s own local frame. Lemma C.2, Lemma C.3 and Lemma C.4 together prove Lemma 3.4 (2). 

# C.2 Monotone Convergence (MCv) 

This subsection records the formal specification of MCv and proves Lemma 3.4 (3). 

Definition C.5 (Monotone Convergence) . Two robots a, b start from distinct positions, and we write 

d(t) := ∥a(t) − b(t)∥. The first condition (convergence) requires the two robots to converge to a common point l. The second condition (monotonicity) requires their distance never to increase. ΦMCv ≡ [ { ∃ l ∈ R2 : lim  

> t→∞

a(t) = lim  

> t→∞

b(t) = l } and { ∀ t, t ′ ≥ 0 : t ≤ t′ ⇒ d(t′) ≤ d(t) } ]

Monotonicity is required at every instant of the continuous execution, not only at the joint stops. This is Monotone Line Convergence of [FSSW24] with the two conditions confining the robots to the segment joining their initial positions and forbidding overtaking removed. 

Impossibility Lemma C.6 ([KSW25, Theorem 8], [FSSW24, Lemma 13]) . MCv /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Asynch . Run fully synchronous cycles with normalized frames. By Lemma E.14, the two robots carry the same color at every round boundary. If every output along this execution is zero, the resulting fair, time-divergent execution keeps the robots at their distinct initial positions, violating convergence. Otherwise, stop the zero-Move prefix just before the first round with a nonzero output. Let γ be the common color at this point, so that λγ > 0, and put c := c1(γ). Let b perform its Look , and delay its Comp . This finite delay is permitted under Asynch by Lemma E.5. The saved snapshot determines the nonzero displacement 

v = λγ Rθγ (wold ),

21 where wold is the vector from b to a at that Look . Although b has not yet performed its Comp , determinism ensures that it will compute this displacement from the saved snapshot when its Comp is eventually performed. Throughout the delay, both the position and the visible color γ of b remain unchanged. Since an 

FCOM robot does not read its own color, we may therefore activate a for any prescribed finite number of complete cycles, using normalized frames and the same nonzero displacement rule ( λγ , θ γ ) in each cycle. If c > 1, a single such Move increases the distance between its starting and ending times, violating monotonicity. If c = 1, the starting and ending positions of this nonzero Move are distinct points at the same distance from the stationary robot b. The midpoint of the movement segment is strictly closer to 

b than either endpoint. Thus the distance increases between the time a passes that midpoint and the completion of its Move, again violating monotonicity. It remains to consider 0 ≤ c < 1. Choose a finite k ≥ 1 such that, after k complete cycles of a,

∥wk∥ = ck∥wold ∥ < ∥v∥

2 ,

where wk is the vector from b to a at that time. For c = 0, take k = 1; otherwise every cycle starts with distinct robot positions, so the normalized rule can be applied repeatedly. Keep a stationary and let b complete its Comp using the saved snapshot, update its color, and perform the resulting Move with displacement v. The distance immediately before that Move is ∥wk∥, whereas at its completion it satisfies 

∥wk − v∥ ≥ ∥ v∥ − ∥ wk∥ > ∥wk∥.

Hence monotonicity fails. In every finite-violation case, complete any pending cycle and append an arbitrary admissible fair, time-divergent continuation. The violation persists, so no such algorithm solves MCv. 

Lemma C.7 ([KSW25, Theorem 8], [FSSW24, Lemma 20]) . MCv /∈ Sol (FSTA AM ).Proof. We prove the following stronger statement: from any configuration with distinct robot positions, a common internal color, and no pending operation, there is an admissible fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ MCv .Use normalized frames whenever the robots are distinct. At a configuration with common color s,write 

zs = λseiθs , cs = |1 − zs|.

We advance the common color by activating a and then b, one complete cycle at a time, except in the special case specified below. If zs = 0, both cycles are zero moves and both robots reach color g(s). If cs = 0, then zs = 1. Instead, let both robots Look and Compute before either moves, and let them execute their moves simultaneously with equal fractional progress. They exchange positions, so their distance becomes zero and then positive again. Monotonicity fails. If zs̸ = 0 and cs ≥ 1, a solo move of a already violates monotonicity. For cs > 1 its landing distance is larger than its starting distance. For cs = 1, the endpoints are distinct points at the same distance from the stationary peer; the interior of the movement segment contains points strictly closer to that peer, so the distance increases before landing. It remains to consider 0 < c s < 1. Execute the two singleton cycles. If either move increases the distance at any time, monotonicity has already failed. Otherwise both robots finish at distinct positions with common color g(s). Continue in this way through the transient and one period of the common normalized color orbit. Unless a violation has already occurred, every nonzero rule in the period has 0 < c s < 1. If every rule in the period is zero, an infinite fair continuation leaves the robots at distinct fixed positions, so convergence fails. 22 Otherwise, the product Γ of the solo distance ratios over one period satisfies 0 < Γ < 1. Advance both robots to a common periodic color with a nonzero rule. Let b Look and Compute a nonzero displacement v, and delay its Move until after a prescribed finite number of complete cycles of a. This is admissible under AM by Corollary E.6. During the delay, b remains fixed, while the private color of a

follows its periodic orbit. Consequently, after sufficiently many periods the vector w from b to a satisfies 

∥w∥ < ∥v∥/2. Keep a stationary and let b complete its pending Move. The distance increases, since 

∥w − v∥ ≥ ∥ v∥ − ∥ w∥ > ∥w∥.

After every finite violation, complete any pending cycle and append an arbitrary fair, time-divergent con-tinuation. The construction uses neither the designated initial color nor the original positions, provided that its starting configuration satisfies the three stated conditions. 

Solvability Lemma C.8 ([KSW25, Theorem 8], [FSSW24, Lemma 4]) . MCv ∈ Sol (OBLOT S ).Proof. Each robot moves to the midpoint of the two observed positions. Under Ssynch every round is atomic, so it suffices to follow the configuration at the round boundaries. If only one robot is activated in a round, it lands at the midpoint and the distance is halved; if both are activated, they land on the same midpoint and the distance becomes 0. In either case the distance never increases, so the monotonicity condition holds, and d is at least halved in every round, so d(t) → 0. Moreover each landing point lies on the segment joining the two current positions, so the segments [a(t), b (t)] form a nested family of compact sets whose diameters tend to 0. Their intersection is a single point l, and both robots converge to it, which is the convergence condition. Lemma C.6, Lemma C.7 and Lemma C.8 together prove Lemma 3.4 (3). 

# C.3 Hexagonal Edge Traversal (HET) 

HET [NNW26] places the two robots at opposite vertices of a regular hexagon and requires each of them to move to an adjacent vertex along an edge and back, alternating left and right forever. 

Definition C.9 (Hexagonal Edge Traversal) . Two robots a, b start from distinct positions. Let v0, . . . , v 5

be the vertices of the regular hexagon having a(0) and b(0) as opposite vertices, labelled counterclockwise from v0 = a(0) and v3 = b(0), the indices being read modulo 6. The cycles of the two robots are 

wa := ( v0, v −1, v 0, v 1), wb := ( v3, v 2, v 3, v 4).

The first condition (perpetuity) requires each robot to perform infinitely many nonzero Move operations. The second condition (edge traversal) requires each robot r, at every time at which it is not moving, to stand at the vertex of its own cycle indexed by the number mr (t) of nonzero Move operations it has performed so far. ΦHET ≡ [ { ∀ r ∈ { a, b } : moves( r) = ∞ } 

and { ∀ r ∈ { a, b }, ∀t ≥ 0 : ¬ Move r (t) ⇒ r(t) = wr

(mr (t) mod 4 ) } ]

The reference [NNW26] states the same requirement by exhibiting the time intervals spent at each vertex and the monotone progress along each edge. Since movements here are rigid and follow the segment joining the two endpoints, we have rewritten it by prescribing the sequence of landing points of the nonzero Move operations. The hexagon is uniquely determined by a(0) and b(0), and the two cycles are carried onto each other by the rotation by π about the midpoint. 23 Impossibility Lemma C.10 ([NNW26, Lemma 8]) . HET /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. Let A be any OBLOT algorithm and consider the fully synchronous execution of Lemma E.13, in which the two robots apply one and the same pair ( λ, θ ) in every round. If λ = 0 then moves( a) = 0 and the perpetuity condition fails. Assume λ > 0. If either robot ever leaves its prescribed cycle, the edge-traversal condition fails. Otherwise, normalizing the circumradius of the hexagon to 1, the configuration at the beginning of round 1 is ( v0, v 3) and at the beginning of round 2 it is ( v−1, v 2). In round 1 the robot a sees its peer at v3 − v0, of length 2, and must perform the displacement v−1 − v0, of length 1, so the required pair is ( λ, θ ) = (1 /2, +60 ◦). In round 2 it sees its peer at v2 − v−1, again of length 2, and must perform the displacement v0 − v−1, of length 1, so the required pair is (1 /2, −60 ◦); the same two pairs are required of b by the symmetry of the two cycles. The two rounds therefore demand opposite rotations relative to the peer, which a single pair ( λ, θ ) cannot deliver, so the edge-traversal condition fails in one of the two rounds. 

Lemma C.11 ([NNW26, Lemma 7]) . HET /∈ Sol (FSTA R).Proof. The adversary chooses the alternating singleton activation {a}, {b}, {a}, . . . with an empty fully synchronous prefix (Lemma E.8). 

First stage. The configuration is unchanged until the first nonzero Move . The hexagon and the two cycles of Definition C.9 are carried onto each other by the rotation by π about the midpoint combined with exchanging a and b, and this rotation preserves chirality. The local input of an FSTA robot is the pair consisting of the relative position of the peer and its own state, and does not contain the state of the peer, so in the normalized frame of Lemma E.1(1) both local inputs have the form ( e, own state) and are isomorphic. The two robots start from the same initial state s0, so a in round 2 k − 1 and b in round 2 k both pass from sk−1 to sk. If no state occurring in s0, s 1, . . . produces a nonzero Move , then moves( a) = 0 and the perpetuity condition fails. Let j be the least index that produces a nonzero Move ;then a performs the first nonzero Move in round 2 j + 1, from state sj .

Second stage. If the landing point of a is not v−1, the edge-traversal condition a(t) = wa(1) = v−1

fails at the time ma = 1. So assume a lands at v−1. Writing R for the circumradius of the hexagon, the edge length is R and the diagonal length is 2 R, so the constant of Lemma E.1(1) is 

λsj = ∥v−1 − v0∥∥v3 − v0∥ = 12 .

Third stage. The robot b, activated in round 2 j + 2, has not yet moved and is in state sj . The robots have no common unit of length, so in the normalized frame the local input of b is the same ( e, s j ) as that of a in the second stage: the change of the observed distance from 2 R to ∥v−1 − v3∥ = √3 R does not appear in the local input. Hence b applies the same λsj = 12 to its own peer vector v−1 − v3, and the length of its displacement is √32 R. The landing point required of b by the edge-traversal condition is 

wb(1) = v2, with ∥v2 − v3∥ = R. Since √32 R̸ = R, the robot b cannot land at v2 and the edge-traversal condition fails. 

Lemma C.12 ([NNW26, Lemma 9]) . HET /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. Run a fully synchronous prefix, which is admissible under Ssynch . By Lemma E.14 the two robots always carry the same color. If λγ = 0 for every color γ occurring in that common sequence, this fair execution contains no nonzero Move , so moves( a) = 0 and the perpetuity condition fails. Otherwise let γ be the first color with λγ > 0 and truncate the prefix at the round in which both robots carry γ;no nonzero Move has occurred, so the configuration is still the initial one. Now activate a alone repeatedly (Lemma E.4; under Ssynch the robot that is not activated has no pending operation). The visible state of an FCOM robot is the color of its peer, and b is not activated, so that color stays γ; by Lemma E.16(b) the displacement rule of a is the single pair ( λγ , θ γ ), and λγ > 024 makes every activation a nonzero Move . The cycle wa = ( v0, v −1, v 0, v 1) requires the two successive steps v0 → v−1 and v−1 → v0. With b standing still at v3, the first of them approaches the peer, since 

∥v−1 − v3∥ = √3 R < 2R = ∥v0 − v3∥, and the second retreats from it. By Lemma E.16 a single pair (λγ , θ γ ) cannot realize both, so the edge-traversal condition fails. 

Solvability Lemma C.13 ([NNW26, Lemma 10]) . HET ∈ Sol (LUMI R).Proof. Each robot records its position in its own cycle with a light of four colors. Since a LUMI robot sees both lights, it learns from the color of its peer which vertex the peer currently occupies, so even under the alternating singleton activations of an Rsynch execution it can reconstruct the hexagon from the observed relative position and move to the next vertex of its cycle. The destination is determined by the relative position of the peer and by chirality alone and requires no unit of length, so it is computable in each robot’s own local frame. Lemma C.10, Lemma C.11, Lemma C.12 and Lemma C.13 supply the boundary certificate of Table 2 for HET, which by Lemma 2.3 gives Prof (HET) = ↑LUMI R as asserted in Theorem 4.3. 

# C.4 Zig-Zag Collision-Free Convergence (ZCC) 

ZCC [NNW26] requires the two robots to converge to a single point without overtaking each other, while each of them perpetually repeats a zig-zag motion that alternately increases and decreases its distance from its own initial position. 

Definition C.14 (Zig-Zag Collision-Free Convergence) . Two robots a, b start from distinct positions. The first condition (convergence) requires the two robots to converge to a point l. The second condition (collision freedom) requires each robot never to go, as seen from its own initial position, farther than its peer. The third condition (zig-zag) requires, with fr (t) := ∥r(t) − r(0) ∥, that for each robot r there be times 0 = t−1 < t 0 < t 1 < · · · at which fr takes maxima fr (t2i) and minima fr (t2i+1 ) alternately, the minima increasing. ΦZCC ≡ [ { ∃ l ∈ R2 : lim  

> t→∞

a(t) = lim  

> t→∞

b(t) = l }

and { ∀ r ∈ { a, b }, ∀t ≥ 0 : fr (t) ≤ ∥ ¯r(t) − r(0) ∥ } 

and { ∀ r ∈ { a, b }, ∃ 0 = t−1 < t 0 < t 1 < · · · ,

∀i ≥ 0 : fr (t2i−1) < f r (t2i+1 ) < f r (t2i) } ]

There are two differences from [NNW26]. The quantification in the first condition is ∀ϵ ≥ 0 there, but including ϵ = 0 would require the two robots to coincide at l and stay there from some time on, which is incompatible with the third condition; we therefore use ∀ϵ > 0. Also, that paper imposes the third condition on one robot only, whereas we impose it on both. Along a single Move the position of r

traverses a segment, so fr is a convex function of the movement progress and hence non-increasing and then non-decreasing in time; outside the Move intervals fr is constant. Each nonzero Move therefore contributes at most one turning point of fr , and since executions are time-divergent, only finitely many Moves occur in any bounded time interval. Consequently the times ti of the zig-zag condition satisfy 

ti → ∞ .The three conditions differ in nature. Collision freedom is a safety condition: it is violated as soon as 

fr (t) > ∥¯r(t)−r(0) ∥ at a single time t, and no extension can repair this. The zig-zag condition is a liveness condition: beyond any T the sequence ( ti) still provides a strictly decreasing step fr (t2i) > f r (t2i+1 )and a strictly increasing one fr (t2i+1 ) < f r (t2i+2 ). Hence, if for some T the restriction of fr to [ T, ∞) is monotone for one of the two robots, then Φ ZCC fails. In particular, the monotonicity of fr along a finite solo activation interval does not by itself establish failure. 25 Impossibility Lemma C.15. ZCC /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. An OBLOT F robot applies the same rule in every round, so it cannot switch between the two directions that the zig-zag condition demands. Consider the fully synchronous execution of Lemma E.13, let m be the midpoint and v := a(0) − m. The difference vector is mapped in every round by the same similarity I − 2λR θ , of ratio c2 and rotation angle φ, and the midpoint is invariant (Lemma E.2(ii)), so 

a(k) − m = c k 

> 2

While they coincide the observed peer position is 0. If the local output is then zero, the robots never move again and fr is constant from round 1 on. Otherwise the adversary chooses their common global displacement (Lemma E.1(2)); choosing in every round one and the same direction w

with ⟨w, v ⟩ < 0 makes fa(t) = ∥a(t) − a(0) ∥ strictly increasing from round 1 on. In both cases the restriction of fa to a tail is monotone, so the zig-zag condition fails by the criterion stated after Definition C.14. Assume λ̸ = 0 and c2̸ = 0. If φ̸ ≡ 0 (mod 2 π), then by Lemma E.2(iii) applied to the nonzero angle φ there is k ≥ 1 with cos( kφ ) ≤ − 12 < 0, whence ⟨v, a (k) − m⟩ = c k 

> 2

cos( kφ )∥v∥2 < 0. Since

∥a(k) − a(0) ∥2 − ∥ b(k) − a(0) ∥2 = −4 ⟨v, a (k) − m⟩ > 0,

so at the end of round k we have fa > ∥b(k) − a(0) ∥ and the collision-freedom condition fails. This is a safety violation at a finite time. If φ ≡ 0, then Rφ = I and a(k) = m+c k

v, so all the positions of a lie on the ray from m through a(0) and advance monotonically along it, towards m when c2 < 1 and away from a(0) when c2 > 1; the value

> 2

− 1 | ∥ v∥ interpolates monotonically within each round, so fa is monotone on the whole execution and the zig-zag condition fails by the same criterion. This is a liveness violation, witnessed by the infinite fully synchronous execution itself. 

Lemma C.16 ([NNW26, Lemma 26]) . ZCC /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. We prove a continuation property that will also be used in the common-failure arguments. Fix the original initial positions a0, b 0. Suppose that a finite prefix ends with no pending operation and with the robots lying in [ a0, b 0] in their original order. Their current colors need not be equal. There is a fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ ZCC , with a0, b 0 kept fixed. First consider a configuration in which the robots coincide. Activate them alternately, one complete cycle at a time. If every output is zero, both positions remain constant forever, so the zig-zag condition fails by the criterion stated after Definition C.14. Otherwise, at the first nonzero output, choose the local frame so that the moving robot increases its distance from its own original initial position; this is possible by Lemma E.1(2). Its peer remains at the common position, so the collision-freedom inequality is violated. Append an arbitrary fair tail. We call this construction the coincidence continuation .Now suppose that the robots are distinct. Use alternating singleton activations as the default schedule, with normalized frames at every Look . For the currently activated robot r, put 

σ = 1 − λe iθ .

If σ ∈ (0 , 1], let the cycle complete and continue with the default schedule. Such a cycle either leaves 

r stationary or moves it towards its peer without reaching it. Thus both robots remain in the initial segment in their original order. At the first output with σ / ∈ (0 , 1], freeze the peer and repeatedly activate r. Its peer’s color is fixed, so the same normalized rule is used until a coincidence occurs. If σ = 0, the first cycle reaches the peer and the coincidence continuation above applies. 26 Assume σ̸ = 0, and write σ = ce iψ . At the start of this repetition, let ¯ r denote the peer and 

L = ∥¯r − r(0) ∥, h = ∥¯r − r∥.

The maintained order gives L ≥ h > 0. After j repetitions, 

∥rj − r(0) ∥2 = L2 + c2j h2 − 2Lc j h cos( jψ ).

If ψ̸ ≡ 0 (mod 2 π), some finite j satisfies cos( jψ ) ≤ 0 (Lemma E.2(iii)), so this quantity exceeds L2.If ψ ≡ 0, then necessarily c > 1, and for sufficiently large finite j we again have ∥rj − r(0) ∥ > L . In either case the stationary peer remains at distance L from r(0), so the collision-freedom condition fails. Append an arbitrary fair tail. Finally, if no output outside (0 , 1] ever occurs, the default alternating execution is fair and each 

fr (t) = ∥r(t) − r(0) ∥ is non-decreasing throughout. The zig-zag condition therefore fails. All infinite executions can be scheduled to be time-divergent, and every exceptional repetition above is finite. Applying this continuation property to the initial configuration proves the claim. 

Lemma C.17 ([NNW26, Lemmas 24–25]) . ZCC /∈ Sol (FSTA AM ).Proof. We prove a continuation statement with the original initial positions a0, b 0 kept fixed. Suppose that no operation is pending and that either the robots coincide, or their current normalized color orbits have the same eventual cycle. There is an admissible fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ ZCC .If a safety violation has already occurred, append any fair tail. If the robots coincide, use the coincidence continuation from the proof of Lemma C.16. Hence assume that they are distinct and that safety has not yet failed. If a state with solo ratio c1 = 0 is reachable along the normalized orbit of either robot, activate that robot alone until it first lands on its stationary peer. All preceding Look s see distinct positions. At the resulting coincidence neither robot has a pending operation, so the same coincidence continuation applies. We may therefore assume that all future solo ratios are positive. Let Γ > 0 be their product over the common eventual color cycle. Consider the fair execution E of alternating singleton activations under normalized frames. If Γ ≥ 1, then, after both color sequences have entered their periodic parts, every block of one full period of each robot multiplies the distance by Γ 2 ≥ 1. The distance thus has a positive subsequence bounded away from zero, and convergence fails. Assume 0 < Γ < 1. If E already violates Φ ZCC , use E. Otherwise E satisfies both safety and convergence. Put 

e = b0 − a0

∥b0 − a0∥ , xr (t) = ⟨r(t), e ⟩.

Adding the squared safety inequalities gives 2⟨a(t) − b(t), b 0 − a0⟩ ≤ 0,

hence xa(t) ≤ xb(t) at every time. The execution E must contain a Move of a with positive displacement along e, or a Move of b with negative displacement along e. Indeed, otherwise xa would be non-increasing and xb non-decreasing. Convergence would then require both projections to be equal and constant. Equality of the projections, together with safety, forces both squared safety inequalities to be equalities. During a singleton Move ,the moving robot would consequently have to remain on the intersection of two fixed circles centered at 

a0 and b0. This finite set admits no nonconstant continuous movement. Thus the distinct robots could not converge, a contradiction. Fix the first such Move in E.Suppose first that the selected Move is a Move of b with displacement v satisfying ⟨v, e ⟩ < 0. Follow 

E through its Look and Comp , but delay the start of its Move . While b stays fixed, activate a through 27 any remaining transient part of its color orbit and then for sufficiently many complete periods. Since Γ < 1, the vector w from b to a can be made to satisfy 

∥w∥ < −⟨ v, e ⟩/2.

If safety fails during this finite interval, we are done. Otherwise keep a stationary and release the pending 

Move of b. At its completion, 

xa − xb = ⟨w − v, e ⟩ > 0,

contradicting the necessary projection order. If the selected Move is a Move of a with ⟨v, e ⟩ > 0, delay that Move and instead activate b through any remaining transient part of its color orbit and then for sufficiently many complete periods, bringing it arbitrarily close to the fixed position of a. Choose the resulting vector w from a to b with ∥w∥ < ⟨v, e ⟩/2. Releasing the Move of a gives 

xa − xb = ⟨v − w, e ⟩ > 0,

again violating safety. All delays are finite and occur before the delayed Move starts, as permitted by AM and Corollary E.6. No moving robot is stopped inside its Move . After a finite violation, complete any pending operation and append an arbitrary fair, time-divergent tail. The initial configuration satisfies the continuation hypotheses, which proves the stated impossibility.
