Lemma C.18. ZCC ∈ Sol (FSTA S ), as witnessed by Algorithm 1. Proof. The algorithm of [NNW26, Lemma 27], which approaches by D/ 2 and retreats by D/ 4, makes the two robots coincide at the midpoint when they are activated simultaneously, after which every Move is zero and the zig-zag condition fails. We therefore approach by D/ 4 and retreat by D/ 8. Every movement takes place on the line through a(0) and b(0), so we put a coordinate x on that line with xa(0) = 0 and 

xb(0) = 1, and write D := xb − xa.

(i) The order is preserved. Under Ssynch each round is atomic, so the destinations of a round are computed from the configuration at its beginning. The factor by which D changes in one round is 1 /2when both approach, 3 /4 when only one approaches, 7 /8 when one approaches and the other retreats, 9/8 when only one retreats, and 5 /4 when both retreat; all of these are positive. Moreover the two movement segments of a round are disjoint, since a never goes beyond xa + D/ 4 while b never goes below 

xb − D/ 4 = xa + 3 D/ 4. Hence xa(t) < x b(t) at every time, movements included. 

(ii) No robot leaves the outer side of its own initial position. Let D1 be the distance at the Look of some approach of a and D2 the distance at the Look of its next retreat. After the approach round the 28 distance is at most 7 D1/8, and while a is not activated the robot b alternates approach and retreat, so the distance increases by a factor of at most 9 /8. Hence D2 ≤ 63 64 D1, and the retreat amount D2/8 is smaller than the preceding approach amount D1/4. Therefore the position of a after each retreat is larger than its position before the preceding approach, and inductively xa(t) ≥ 0. Symmetrically xb(t) ≤ 1. 

(iii) The collision-freedom condition. By (i) and (ii) we have 0 ≤ xa(t) < x b(t) ≤ 1, so fa(t) = 

xa(t) < x b(t) = ∥b(t) − a(0) ∥, and symmetrically for b.

(iv) The convergence condition. Give weight 1 to the state A and weight 6 /5 to the state B, and put 

H := D · wa · wb. The factor by which H changes in one round is 34 · 65 = 910 when only one approaches,  

> 98

· 56 = 15 16 when only one retreats, 12 · 36 25 = 18 25 when both approach, 54 · 25 36 = 125 144 when both retreat, and 78 when one approaches and the other retreats; all of these are at most 15 16 . At least one robot is activated in every round, and wa, w b ∈ [1 , 6/5], so D → 0 geometrically. Each displacement is at most 

D/ 4, so the total length travelled by each robot is finite and both converge to one and the same point l.

(v) The zig-zag condition. By (i) we have D > 0, so every activation produces a nonzero Move , and by fairness each robot performs infinitely many approaches and retreats alternately. By (ii) we have 

xa ≥ 0, so an approach of a increases fa and a retreat decreases it. Let tk−1 be the landing time of the k-th Move of a, with t−1 := 0. Then [ t2i−1, t 2i] is an approach and [ t2i, t 2i+1 ] a retreat, and each 

Move progresses monotonically towards its destination, so fa takes the maxima fa(t2i) and the minima 

fa(t2i+1 ) alternately. By (ii) the position after each retreat is larger than the position after the previous retreat, so fa(t2i+1 ) > f a(t2i−1). The same holds for b.Finally the displacement is a fixed ratio of the observed distance, so it requires no unit of length, and the state is private, so the algorithm is admissible in FSTA .Lemma C.15, Lemma C.16, Lemma C.17 and Lemma C.18 supply the boundary certificate of Table 2 for ZCC, which by Lemma 2.3 gives Prof (ZCC) = ↑FSTA S as asserted in Theorem 4.3. 

# C.5 Vertical Configuration (VEC) 

VEC [NNW26] requires the two robots to transform the segment joining them by the same rotations as in SRO and, once a configuration orthogonal to the initial segment has been reached, to stop there forever. 

Definition C.19 (Vertical Configuration) . Two robots a, b start from distinct positions. The sequence of configurations Ci = ( ai, b i), the vectors di := bi − ai, the squares Qi with Q−1 := R2, and the rotations Rϕ are defined as in Definition C.1. A vector u is orthogonal to d0, written u ⊥ d0, if u̸ = 0 and ⟨u, d 0⟩ = 0. The first condition (attainment) requires di ⊥ d0 for some i. The second condition (transition) requires that for every i the vector di+1 be equal to di, or its clockwise 90 ◦ rotation, or its clockwise 45 ◦ rotation scaled by 2 ∓1/2, and that ai+1 , b i+1 ∈ Qi−1. The third condition (stopping) requires that if at a time at which both robots are at rest the segment is orthogonal to d0, then neither robot ever moves again. ΦVEC ≡ [ { ∃ i ≥ 0 : di ⊥ d0 }

and 

{

∀i ≥ 0 : di+1 ∈ { di, R −90 ◦ di, 2−1/2R−45 ◦ di, 21/2R−45 ◦ di}∧ (ai+1 , b i+1 ∈ Qi−1

) }

and { ∀ t ∈ Σab : b(t) − a(t) ⊥ d0 ⇒ ∀ t′ ≥ t : a(t′) = a(t) ∧ b(t′) = b(t) } ]

Impossibility Lemma C.20. VEC /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. Once an OBLOT F robot moves it keeps moving in every round and cannot stop when the orthogonal configuration is reached. Consider the fully synchronous execution of Lemma E.13. If λ = 0 the robots do not move, the sequence reduces to C0, and d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition fails. If 29 c2 = 0 the two robots coincide in round 1 and d1 = 0, while all the transitions permitted by the second condition are nonzero, so that condition fails. If c2 > 0 both robots perform a nonzero Move in every round, so even if dk ⊥ d0 holds at the end of some round they move again in the next one and the stopping condition fails; and if no such round exists the attainment condition fails. 

Lemma C.21 ([NNW26, Lemma 21]) . VEC /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. Run a fully synchronous prefix, which is admissible under Ssynch . By Lemma E.14 the two robots always carry the same color. If λγ = 0 for every color γ occurring in that sequence, this fair execution contains no nonzero Move , the sequence reduces to C0, and d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition fails. Otherwise let γ be the first color with λγ > 0 and truncate the prefix at the round in which both robots carry γ; the configuration is still the initial one. Now activate a alone repeatedly (Lemma E.4; under Ssynch the robot that is not activated has no pending operation). The color of b stays γ, so by Lemma E.16(b) the robot a applies the same pair (λγ , θ γ ) at every activation. With the peer at rest, a single nonzero Move can satisfy the transition condition only by making di+1 equal to R−90 ◦ di or to 2 ∓1/2R−45 ◦ di, and each of these applies the same rotation to d at every activation. Hence a reaches a configuration with di ⊥ d0 in at most two moves, and since λγ > 0 it moves again at the very next activation, so the stopping condition fails. 

Lemma C.22 ([NNW26, Lemma 22]) . VEC /∈ Sol (FSTA A).Proof. By Proposition 3.2 we have FSTA A ≡ FSTA ALC , so it suffices to exhibit an admissible ALC 

execution. Run a fully synchronous prefix, which is admissible under ALC . By Lemma E.14 the two robots always carry the same state. If λs = 0 for every state s occurring in that common sequence, this fair execution contains no nonzero Move , the sequence reduces to C0, and d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition fails. Otherwise let s be the first state with λs > 0 and truncate the prefix at the round in which both robots carry s; no nonzero Move has occurred, so the configuration is still the initial one. Activate a alone. It performs a nonzero Move from pB := a0 to some pE̸ = pB . The robot b has finished its cycle and has no pending operation, so the hypotheses of Lemma E.7 are met and, for each 

μ ∈ (0 , 1), there is an execution in which b observes a at pμ = (1 − μ)pB + μp E . Let the Move of b begin before the Move of a ends; then the two Move intervals overlap, so no time of Σ ab occurs between the two landings and the configuration after both landings is C1. In state s the robot b lands, by Lemma E.1(1), at b0 + λsRθs (pμ − b0), so 

d1(μ) = b0 + λsRθs (pμ − b0) − pE

is an affine function of μ whose slope λsRθs (pE − pB ) is nonzero because λs > 0. Hence a suitable coordinate h(μ) of d1 is continuous and non-constant on (0 , 1). For i = 0 we have Q−1 = R2, so the transition condition only requires d1 ∈ { d0, R −90 ◦ d0, 2−1/2R−45 ◦ d0, 21/2R−45 ◦ d0}, and the corresponding values of h are only four. By Corollary E.22 there is a μ avoiding those four values, and for it the transition condition fails. 

Solvability Lemma C.23 ([NNW26, Lemma 23]) . VEC ∈ Sol (FSTA AM ), as witnessed by Algorithm 2. Proof. Since AM forbids a Look of one robot during a Move of the other, every configuration observed at a Look is one of the terms of the sequence ( Ci). Suppose first that the Look of b precedes the landing of a. Then both robots take as destinations the two remaining vertices of the square Q0. If there is a time at which both robots are at rest between the two landings, the intermediate configuration has d1 = 2 −1/2R−45 ◦ d0, and the next one has d2 = R−90 ◦ d0 =21/2R−45 ◦ d1; if there is no such time, the two landings produce a single term with d1 = R−90 ◦ d0.Suppose instead that the Look of b follows the landing of a. Then d1 = 2 −1/2R−45 ◦ d0, and b moves to a vertex of Q1, giving d2 = 2 −1/2R−45 ◦ d1.In every case, each transition has one of the permitted forms. In the first case, the final positions are the two remaining vertices of Q0; in the second case, they are a vertex and the centre of Q0. Thus, when 30 Algorithm 2 VEC for FSTA under AM (robot r)

# C.6 Dyadic-Multiple Stopped-Distance (DMSD) 

Definition C.24 (Dyadic-Multiple Stopped-Distance) . Let D0 = ∥a(0) − b(0) ∥ > 0, and let Z[1 /2] = 

{m/ 2k | m ∈ Z, k ∈ Z≥0} be the set of dyadic rationals. Using the moving predicate of Section 2.3, define the set of joint stops Σab = {t ≥ 0 | ¬ Move a(t) ∧ ¬ Move b(t)}, the set of times at which neither robot is inside a nonzero Move operation. Every execution must satisfy 

∀t ∈ Σab : ∥a(t) − b(t)∥

D0

∈ Z[1 /2] , (D1)

Impossibility Lemma C.25. DMSD /∈ Sol (FSTA A).Proof. The argument extends [KSW25, Lemma 6] to a general displacement ( λ, θ ) including a rotation, and to the internal states of FSTA . By Proposition 3.2 we have FSTA A ≡ FSTA ALC , so it suffices to exhibit, for an arbitrary FSTA algorithm A, an admissible ALC execution that violates DMSD. In the normalized frame of Lemma E.1(1), a robot in state s has displacement λsRθs (w), where w

is the vector to its peer, and its next state is determined by s alone. The local coordinate system may be chosen anew at each activation, and we supply this frame independently at every activation below. Normalize a(0) = 0, b(0) = e1 and D0 = 1. 

First stage. Run a fully synchronous prefix, which is admissible under ALC . By Lemma E.14 the two robots always carry the same state; let s0, s 1, . . . be that common state sequence. If λs = 0 for every state s occurring in the sequence, this fair execution contains no nonzero Move , so a and b stay at their initial positions and ∥a(t) − b(t)∥ = D0 for every t ∈ Σab , which violates (D2). Otherwise let s

be the first state with λs > 0 and truncate the prefix at the round in which both robots carry s; write 

λ := λs > 0 and R := Rθs . No nonzero Move has occurred, so the robots are at their initial positions and the ratio at every earlier joint stop is 1 ∈ Z[1 /2]. 

Second stage. Activate b alone. It performs a nonzero Move to 

pE = e1 − λRe 1̸ = b(0) ,

31 and for μ ∈ (0 , 1) we put pμ := (1 − μ)b(0) + μp E = e1 − μλRe 1. The robot a has finished its cycle and has no pending operation, so the hypotheses of Lemma E.7 are met and there is an execution in which 

a performs a Look at a time when b is at pμ. In state s the robot a then lands at 

λRp μ = λRe 1 − μλ 2R2e1.

Third stage. On that interval the distance equals

Since (D1) is of the form ∀t ∈ Σab , a single violating time suffices, and the joint stops produced in the first two stages need not be inspected. 

Corollary C.26 ([KSW25, Lemma 6]) . DMSD /∈ Sol (OBLOT A).Proof. In Q the class OBLOT A lies below FSTA A, so this follows from Lemma C.25 and Proposition 2.1. 

Solvability Lemma C.27 ([KSW25, Lemma 5]) . DMSD ∈ Sol (OBLOT AM ), as witnessed by the Go-to-midpoint algorithm. Proof. Each robot moves to the midpoint of its own position and the observed position of its peer. By Proposition 3.2 we have OBLOT ACM ≡ OBLOT AM , so it is enough to argue under AM .

Observed positions. Under AM a Look of one robot never falls inside the closed interval [ tB , t E ] of a 

Move operation of the other, so every observed position is an initial position or the landing point of a completed nonzero Move . Moreover a robot is itself at rest at its own Look , since tE (r, k −1) < t L(r, k ) <tB (r, k ). Consequently every Look time belongs to Σ ab .

Condition (D1). We claim that at every time the position of each robot is of the form (1 − α)a(0) + 

αb (0) with α ∈ Z[1 /2], whenever that robot is not inside a Move operation. This holds initially with 

α = 0 and α = 1. A robot lands at the midpoint of its own position and an observed position, and by the previous paragraph both are of the stated form; the midpoint of (1 −α)a(0)+ αb (0) and (1 −β)a(0)+ βb (0) is the point with parameter ( α + β)/2 ∈ Z[1 /2]. Since the destination is computed from the snapshot taken at the Look , a delayed Move does not change the landing point (Corollary E.6), so the claim is preserved even when a snapshot is stale. Hence for t ∈ Σab we get ∥a(t) − b(t)∥ = |β − α| D0 with 

β − α ∈ Z[1 /2], which is (D1). 

Condition (D2). Every landing point is a midpoint of two points of the segment [ a(0) , b (0)], so by induction both robots always lie in that segment. By fairness some robot, say a, is activated first; at its 

Look the two robots are still at their initial positions, so the observed distance is D0 > 0 and a performs a nonzero Move , landing at the midpoint m of a(0) and b(0). Let t := tL(a, 2) be the next Look of a,which exists by fairness and satisfies tE (a, 1) < t < t B (a, 2), so a(t) = m; by the first paragraph t ∈ Σab .Since b(t) lies in [ a(0) , b (0)] and m is the midpoint of that segment, 

∥a(t) − b(t)∥ ≤ D0 

> 2

< D 0,

which is (D2). Lemma C.25 and Lemma C.27 together prove Lemma 3.4 (6). 32 C.7 Convergence (Cv) 

Cv requires nothing but that the two robots converge to a single point. It occupies the row of the least class of Q, so its profile is the whole of Q.

Since OBLOT A is the least element of Q, we have NOBLOT A = Max( Q \ ↑OBLOT A) = ∅, so the negative half of the condition in Lemma 2.3, applied with U = ↑OBLOT A = Q, is vacuous for Cv. This is what the entry “Trivial” of Table 2 records. It concerns only the impossibility side: the positive half still has to be established, and we do so here. 

Solvability Algorithm 3 Cv for OBLOT under Asynch (robot r)

Model and scheduler: OBLOT , Asynch .

> 1:

r. des ← v/ 2 ▷ the midpoint of the two observed positions

Lemma C.29 ([CP05]) . Cv ∈ Sol (OBLOT A), as witnessed by Algorithm 3. Proof. Algorithm 3 is the two-robot case of the gravitational algorithm of [CP05]: each robot moves to the center of gravity of the configuration it has observed, which for two robots is the midpoint of the two observed positions. The destination is determined by the midpoint alone, so it requires neither a unit of length nor chirality, and it is computed from the current snapshot alone, so the algorithm is admissible in OBLOT .All the motion takes place on the line through a(0) and b(0), since the midpoint of two points of that line lies on it. Let H(t) be the convex hull, an interval of that line, of the current positions of the two robots together with the destinations determined by all Looks whose cycles have not yet completed at time t, including cycles awaiting Compute We claim that H is non-increasing. Indeed, the destination determined by a Look is the midpoint of two positions occupied at that time, both of which belong to 

H at that time, so the destination belongs to H as well; and every movement is a rigid motion along a segment whose two endpoints belong to H, so it never leaves H. In particular H(t) ⊆ H(0) = [ a(0) , b (0)] for every t, which is the containment hypothesis of [CP05]. Executions are fair, so each robot performs infinitely many LCM cycles, and the adversary’s freedom in Asynch is exactly the one allowed there: the destination carried into a Move may have been computed from a stale snapshot, and the two robots may be in flight simultaneously. Under these hypotheses the gravitational algorithm converges [CP05], so there is a point l with a(t), b (t) → l, which is Φ Cv .Lemma C.29 is the positive half of the boundary certificate for Cv; the negative half is empty, as noted above. By Lemma 2.3 we obtain Prof (Cv) = ↑OBLOT A = Q, as asserted in Theorem 4.3. 

# D The New and Revised Tasks 

# D.1 Expansion Preserving Gravity (EPG) 

This subsection and Appendix D.2 read their requirements off the same sequence of configurations, the one already used for SRO in Definition C.1 and for VEC in Definition C.19. Let Σab := { t ≥ 0 | ∀ r ∈ { a, b } : ¬Move r (t) }

List the configurations taken at the times of Σ ab in chronological order, remove consecutive repetitions, and write the resulting sequence as C0, C 1, C 2, . . . with Ci = ( ai, b i); thus C0 = (a(0) , b (0) ). (a(0) + b(0) ), di := bi − ai.

Since MoveSet( r) collects only the open intervals of the nonzero Move operations of r, at every t ∈ Σab 

each robot stands either at its initial position or at the landing point of its last completed nonzero Move ;in particular 0 ∈ Σab and the sequence is well defined. 

Remark D.1 (Preserving the center of gravity forces simultaneity) . Suppose 12 (ai+1 + bi+1 ) = c0 = 

> 12

(ai + bi). Then ai+1 + bi+1 = ai + bi, so bi+1 = bi would force ai+1 = ai and hence Ci+1 = Ci.Consecutive terms of the sequence being distinct, every step of the sequence therefore moves both robots. Equivalently, a nonzero Move performed by one robot while the other is stationary across the surrounding joint stops displaces the center of gravity by half that displacement. 

Definition D.2 (Expansion Preserving Gravity) . Two robots a, b start from distinct positions. The first condition (one expansion) requires the joint-stop sequence to consist of exactly the two terms C0 and 

C1. The second condition (expansion preserving gravity) requires the step from C0 to C1 to keep the center of gravity at c0 and to double the segment. The third condition (stopping) requires both robots to remain stationary from some time on. ΦEPG ≡ [ { (Ci)i≥0 has exactly the two terms C0, C 1 }

and { 12 (a1 + b1) = c0 and d1 = 2 d0

}

and { ∀ r ∈ { a, b } : Stopped( r) } ]

The two equalities determine C1 outright: a1 = c0 − d0 and b1 = c0 + d0, that is, each robot ends at twice its initial distance from c0 each robot ends at twice its initial distance from c0. The predicate mentions only the trajectory and γ(0): no floor function, no reference to the local coordinate systems, and no reference to activation counts, since the terms of ( Ci) are determined by the configurations at the joint stops and not by how many cycles produced them. 

Lemma D.3 (Bounds for EPG) . Under the assumptions of this paper, 

EPG ∈ Sol (LUMI F ), EPG /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ) ∪ Sol (LUMI R).

Impossibility Lemma D.5. EPG /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Fsynch . # D.2 Perpetual Expansion Preserving Gravity (EPG ∗)

Definition D.8 (Perpetual Expansion Preserving Gravity) . Two robots a, b start from distinct positions, and Σ ab , the sequence ( Ci)i≥0, c0 and di are as in Appendix D.1. The first condition (perpetuity) requires the sequence to be infinite. The second condition (expansion preserving gravity) requires every step of the sequence to keep the center of gravity at c0 and to double the segment. ΦEPG ∗ ≡ [ { (Ci)i≥0 is infinite }

and 

{

∀i ≥ 0 : 12 (ai+1 + bi+1 ) = c0 and di+1 = 2 di

} ]

35 Algorithm 5 EPG ∗ for OBLOT under Fsynch (robot r)

Model and scheduler: OBLOT , Fsynch .

> 1:

r. des ← − v/ 2 ▷ away from the observed midpoint, by the observed distance

Remark D.9 (Relation to CGE and CGE ∗). Center of Gravity Expansion and its perpetual variant [BFK +25, FSW20] prescribe each landing point by a coordinatewise floor, which makes the expansion irregular and moves the center of gravity. That formulation cannot be carried over to the present model. Evaluated on a lattice common to the two robots, as in the global formula of [BFK +25], the destination is not computable under variable disorientation, so the profile would be empty; evaluated on each robot’s own lattice, the predicate refers to the local frames chosen by the adversary and is therefore no longer a predicate on the trajectory, contrary to Section 2.3. We keep the exact doubling, drop the floor, and recover the same boundary by requiring the center of gravity to be preserved at every joint stop. By Remark D.1 that requirement rules out a solo expansion, which is the role the floor played in [FSW20]. 

Solvability Lemma D.10. EPG ∗ ∈ Sol (OBLOT F ), as witnessed by Algorithm 5. Proof. The destination is computed from the current snapshot alone and requires neither a unit of length nor chirality, so the algorithm is admissible in OBLOT . We show by induction on k ≥ 0 that at the start of round k + 1 the two robots are stationary with no pending operation in the configuration Γk := (c0 + 2 k(a0 − c0), c 0 + 2 k(b0 − c0)).

This holds for k = 0. Assume it for k and let δ := 2 kd0̸ = 0 be the distance. Under Fsynch both robots are activated in round k + 1 and compute from the same configuration; each moves away from the peer by δ/ 2, so the two displacements are opposite and of equal norm. The midpoint is therefore unchanged and the difference vector is doubled, which is Γ k+1 , and both Move operations are nonzero since δ̸ = 0. The two Move operations of a round occupy the same interval, so no time of Σ ab lies strictly between consecutive round boundaries at which a Move occurs, and the joint-stop sequence is exactly (Γ k)k≥0.It is infinite, consecutive terms are distinct because δ̸ = 0, and every step satisfies 12 (ak+1 + bk+1 ) = c0

and dk+1 = 2 dk. Hence Φ EPG ∗ holds. 

Impossibility Lemma D.11. EPG ∗ /∈ Sol (LUMI R).Proof. Apply Lemma 5.1(2) with K = R.Lemma D.11 is the negative half of Lemma 3.4 (1), whose positive half is Lemma D.10. Since Max( Q \ ↑OBLOT F ) = {LUMI R}, Lemma 2.3 gives Prof (EPG ∗) = ↑OBLOT F , as already recorded in Theorem 3.6. 

# D.3 Oscillating Points with One Move per Robot per Phase (OSPO) 

We introduce OSPO (Oscillating Points with One Move per Robot per Phase), a strengthened version of OSP [DFP +16]. The first approach phase starts at time 0, all phase boundaries are joint stops, and each robot completes exactly one nonzero Move in each phase. 36 Definition D.12 (Oscillating Points with One Move per Robot per Phase) . Two robots a, b start from distinct positions, and d(t) is their distance as in Definition C.5. For r ∈ { a, b }, let mr (t) denote the number of nonzero Move operations completed by r by time t.The task OSPO requires the robots to remain at distinct positions throughout the execution and to alternate approach and retreat forever, starting with approach at time 0. Each phase is delimited by joint stops, and each robot completes exactly one nonzero Move during that phase. More precisely, there is a strictly increasing sequence ( tj )j≥0 of joint stops, with t0 = 0 and tj → ∞ , such that on each interval [ t2i, t 2i+1 ] the distance is monotonically non-increasing and decreases overall, and on each interval [ t2i+1 , t 2i+2 ] it is monotonically non-decreasing and increases overall. The move-count condition is mr (tj+1 ) − mr (tj ) = 1 for every j ≥ 0 and r ∈ { a, b }.ΦOSPO ≡ { ∀ t ≥ 0 : d(t) > 0 }∧ ∃ (tj )j≥0 ⊆ Σab :

[

t0 = 0 , tj → ∞ ,

∀j ≥ 0 : tj < t j+1 ,

∀j ≥ 0 ∀r ∈ { a, b } : mr (tj+1 ) − mr (tj ) = 1 ,

∀i ≥ 0 : 

(

d(t2i+1 ) < d (t2i)

∧ ∀ h, h ′ ∈ [t2i, t 2i+1 ] : (h < h ′ ⇒ d(h′) ≤ d(h))

∧ d(t2i+2 ) > d (t2i+1 )

∧ ∀ h, h ′ ∈ [t2i+1 , t 2i+2 ] : (h < h ′ ⇒ d(h′) ≥ d(h)))] 

.

The specification Φ OSPO combines safety constraints with a requirement of infinite progress. Collision freedom is violated as soon as d(t) = 0 for some t. The move-count requirement also admits finite witnesses of failure: since t0 = 0 and each phase contains exactly one completed nonzero Move by each robot, every satisfying execution must have ma(tj ) = mb(tj ) = j and hence |ma(t) − mb(t)| ≤ 1 for every 

t ≥ 0. Thus, if one robot completes two nonzero Moves before the other completes its first, no extension can satisfy Φ OSPO . After either a collision or such a move-count violation, any admissible fair continuation witnesses failure. By contrast, monotonicity of d along a finite solo activation interval does not by itself establish failure: such an interval may lie within one phase, subject to the move-count requirement. Requiring phase boundaries to be joint stops also prevents a decrease and subsequent increase within a single simultaneous Move from being counted as two phases, since there is no intervening joint stop. For infinite executions, the divergence tj → ∞ provides another sufficient criterion for failure. If, for some T , the restriction of d to Σ ab ∩ [T, ∞) is monotone (non-increasing or non-decreasing), then ΦOSPO fails. Indeed, any witnessing sequence would eventually have both a strictly decreasing phase and a strictly increasing phase with endpoints in Σ ab ∩ [T, ∞), contradicting that monotonicity. This includes the case where d is eventually constant at joint stops. 

Impossibility Lemma D.13. OSPO /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. Fix any deterministic OBLOT algorithm and take the fully synchronous execution of Lemma E.13, with normalized local frames at every Look while the robots are distinct and with time-divergent round timings. If the two robots ever occupy the same point, the collision-freedom condition of Φ OSPO fails at a finite time. This covers c2 = 0, where they coincide at the end of round 1, and also the case in which they meet while travelling inside a round. Otherwise d(t) > 0 throughout. By Lemma E.13 every joint stop of the execution carries one of the round-boundary distances d(k) = c k 

> 2

d(0), so the restriction of d to Σ ab is monotone. The turning times 37 required by Φ OSPO are joint stops, so by the criterion stated after Definition D.12 their distances cannot alternate between strict decrease and strict increase. This admissible fair execution violates Φ OSPO .

Lemma D.14. OSPO /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. Fix any deterministic FCOM algorithm. Use normalized local frames and activate both robots synchronously as long as their computed displacements are zero. Their colors remain equal during this prefix. If no nonzero displacement ever occurs, continuing synchronously gives an admissible fair execution with no movement, which violates OSPO. Otherwise, let γ be the common color immediately before the first round that would produce nonzero displacements. Instead of activating both robots in that round, activate a alone for two complete consecutive cycles, keeping b inactive. If a collision occurs, OSPO already fails. Otherwise, b remains at its initial position with color γ. At both Looks of a, normalization presents the same relative position and the same peer color. Since an 

FCOM robot cannot read its own color, it computes the same nonzero normalized displacement in both cycles. At the completion time T of the second Move, 

ma(T ) = 2 , mb(T ) = 0 .

In any execution satisfying OSPO, the first phase starts at t0 = 0 and ends at a joint stop t1 with 

ma(t1) = mb(t1) = 1. Since b has not moved by time T , such a t1 would have to satisfy t1 > T ; but then ma(t1) ≥ 2, a contradiction. Thus the finite prefix already makes OSPO impossible. Append any admissible fair, time-divergent Ssynch continuation. 

Lemma D.15. OSPO /∈ Sol (FSTA S ).Proof. Fix any deterministic FSTA algorithm and use normalized local frames whenever the robots are distinct. Under these observations, a robot’s next private state and normalized displacement depend only on its current private state. Let 

s0, s 1, s 2, . . . 

be the resulting state sequence from the designated initial state, and let λj ≥ 0 be the displacement magnitude associated with state sj .Suppose first that this sequence contains at least two nonzero displacements. Activate a alone until it completes its second nonzero Move, while keeping b inactive. This takes finitely many cycles. If a collision occurs, the collision-freedom condition fails. Otherwise, at the completion time T ,

ma(T ) = 2 , mb(T ) = 0 .

No endpoint t1 of the first phase can then satisfy ma(t1) = mb(t1) = 1: it cannot occur by time T

because b has not moved, and it cannot occur later because a has already moved twice. The violation persists under any admissible fair, time-divergent continuation. Suppose instead that the state sequence contains at most one nonzero displacement. Choose an infi-nite fully synchronous execution with normalized frames. If the robots collide, OSPO fails. Otherwise, each robot follows the state sequence above and completes at most one nonzero Move. This is incom-patible with infinitely many phases, each requiring one nonzero Move from each robot. Hence this fair, time-divergent execution also violates OSPO. 

Solvability Lemma D.16. OSPO ∈ Sol (FSTA R).Proof. The algorithm. Each robot keeps two private states, approach and retreat , initially approach .Upon an activation, let p be its own position and q the observed position of its peer. In state approach 

its destination is p + 14 (q − p), in state retreat it is p − 14 (q − p); in both cases it then switches to the other state. The destination is a fixed fraction of the observed displacement, so it depends neither on the 38 orientation nor on the unit distance of the local frame and is admissible under variable disorientation. The two robots are always at distinct positions, as shown below, so q̸ = p and every Move is nonzero; hence every activation increases mr by exactly one. 

One round. Let D > 0 be the distance at the start of a round, and for each robot r let ur ∈ [0 , 1] be the fraction of its movement segment covered so far, set to 0 before its Move begins and to 1 after its completion; each ur is continuous and non-decreasing, and ua + ub runs from 0 to the number of robots activated in the round. If both robots are activated and share their state, the distance during the round is 

D

(

1 − ua + ub

4

)

(approach) , D

(

1 + ua + ub

4

)

(retreat) ,

since both displacements are collinear with the segment. Here ua + ub ∈ [0 , 2], so the approach factor lies in [ 12 , 1]: the distance is monotone in the required direction, stays positive, and ends at D/ 2 or 3 D/ 2. If a single robot is activated, the same expressions hold with ua + ub ∈ [0 , 1], the approach factor lies in [ 34 , 1], and the final distance is 3 D/ 4 or 5 D/ 4. 

Phases. If the execution is fully synchronous forever, both robots always share their state and successive rounds alternate between approach and retreat; take each round as one phase. Otherwise let ℓ ≥ 0 be the length of the fully synchronous prefix, after which the activations are alternating singletons (Lemma E.8). Both robots share their state throughout the prefix, and round 

k ≤ ℓ is an approach round exactly when k is odd; at the end of the prefix their common state is 

approach exactly when ℓ is even. Partition the singleton tail into consecutive pairs of rounds. In a pair the two robots are activated once each; the second one has not yet switched, so both use the state held in common at the start of the pair, and at the end of the pair both hold the switched state. Take each such pair as one phase. Consequently the first tail phase is an approach phase exactly when ℓ is even, which for ℓ ≥ 1 is the type opposite to round ℓ; the tail phases alternate, so the phase types alternate across the whole execution, beginning with approach. 

Within a phase. A phase of the first kind is a single round, treated above. A phase of the second kind is two singleton rounds of the same type. Each of them is monotone in the same direction and they agree at the round boundary, so the distance is monotone on the whole phase; starting from D it ends at 916 D in an approach phase and at 25 16 D in a retreat phase, so it also changes strictly. In every case the distance stays positive, so d(t) > 0 for all t ≥ 0. 

The times tj . Take t0 = 0 and let tj+1 be the end of the ( j + 1)-st phase. Each phase ends at a round boundary, where neither robot is inside a Move, so tj ∈ Σab for every j; the same holds at t0 = 0, where no cycle is pending. Each phase contains exactly one nonzero Move of each robot, so mr (tj+1 ) − mr (tj ) = 1 for every j ≥ 0 and r ∈ { a, b }. The phases exhaust the rounds of the execution and each contains at least one round, so tj → ∞ by time divergence. Together with the monotonicity, strictness and positivity established above, and with the first phase being an approach phase, all conditions of Definition D.12 hold. Lemma D.13, Lemma D.14, Lemma D.15 and Lemma D.16 supply the boundary certificate of Table 2 for OSPO, which by Lemma 2.3 gives Prof (OSPO) = ↑FSTA R as asserted in Theorem 4.3. 

# D.4 Collision-Free Single Move (CFSM) 

Definition D.17 (Collision-Free Single Move) . Two robots a and b, initially at distinct positions, must each perform exactly one nonzero Move and remain stationary before that Move begins and after it ends. They must never collide, including during their movements. With moves( r) denoting the number of nonzero Moves of robot r, the specification is ΦCFSM ≡ (moves( a) = 1 ) ∧ (moves( b) = 1 ) ∧ (∀t ≥ 0 : a(t)̸ = b(t)).

Impossibility Lemma D.18. CFSM /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).

39 Proof. Let A be any OBLOT algorithm and consider the fully synchronous execution of Lemma E.13. Since an OBLOT robot has no visible state and the normalized local input of each robot is the same in every round, the two robots apply one and the same pair ( λ, θ ) in every round, and the configuration is updated by the simultaneous contraction ratio c2 = c2(λ, θ ) of Lemma E.2(ii). If λ = 0, no robot ever performs a nonzero Move , so moves( a) = moves( b) = 0 and the single-move condition fails. If c2 = 0, that is ( λ, θ ) = (1 /2, 0), both robots land on the common midpoint in round 1, so a(t) = b(t) at that time and the collision-free condition fails. If c2 > 0, the distance stays positive, hence λ > 0 makes every round a nonzero Move for both robots and moves( a) = moves( b) = ∞, so the single-move condition fails again. The execution is fair in all three cases. 

Lemma D.19. CFSM /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic FCOM algorithm. 

First stage. Run a fully synchronous prefix, which is admissible under Ssynch . By Lemma E.14 the two robots always carry the same color, and we write γ0, γ 1, . . . for that common color sequence. If 

λγ = 0 for every color γ occurring in the sequence, then this fair execution contains no nonzero Move 

and moves( a) = 0, so the single-move condition fails. Otherwise let γ be the first color of the sequence with λγ > 0 and truncate the prefix at the round in which both robots carry γ. Nothing has moved so far, so the robots are still at their initial positions and the distance is d > 0. 

Second stage. From that configuration, activate a alone repeatedly (Lemma E.4; under Ssynch the robot that is not activated has no pending operation, and the activations of b are placed far enough in the future to keep the execution fair). The robot b is never activated, so its color remains γ, and the visible state of a, which is the color of its peer, is constant along the interval. By Lemma E.16(b) the robot a therefore applies the same pair ( λγ , θ γ ) at every activation. Since λγ > 0 and the distance is positive, the first activation is a nonzero Move . If it lands on b(0), the two robots occupy the same point and the collision-free condition fails. Otherwise the distance remains positive, so the second activation is a nonzero Move as well and moves( a) ≥ 2, which violates the single-move condition. 

Solvability 

Algorithm 6 uses one private bit to make each robot move exactly once. The move is directed away from the observed peer, so the two trajectories remain on opposite sides of the initial segment, even under 

Asynch .

Lemma D.20. CFSM ∈ Sol (FSTA A), as witnessed by Algorithm 6. Proof. Put a coordinate on the line through a(0) and b(0) so that b(0) < a (0). We claim the invariants 

a(t) ≥ a(0) and b(t) ≤ b(0) (∀t ≥ 0) ,

40 which give b(t) ≤ b(0) < a (0) ≤ a(t) and hence the collision-free condition at every instant, during movements included. Each robot moves at most once, namely at the activation at which its status is still 0; at that moment it stands at its initial position. Let p be the position of b recorded by the Look of a. If b has not yet moved then p = b(0); if b is moving or has already landed then p ≤ b(0) by the invariant for b. In both cases p < a (0), so D = a(0) − p > 0 and a moves to 

a(0) + 12

(a(0) − p) > a (0) ,

and it stays there because all its later Move operations are zero. The argument for b is symmetric. The two inductions use only the fact, valid from time 0 on, that neither trajectory has ever crossed the initial position of the other, so they are not circular. For the single-move condition, fairness guarantees that each robot is activated at least once. At its first activation the observed distance is positive, so its displacement D/ 2 is nonzero; every later activation produces a zero Move . Hence moves( r) = 1 for both robots. Lemma D.18, Lemma D.19 and Lemma D.20 together prove Lemma 3.4 (4). 

# D.5 The Shared Exchange Phase of S-RDV and VSS 

S-RDV (Definition D.24) and VSS (Definition D.29) differ in their terminal requirements, one demand-ing rendezvous and the other permanent stopping, but they impose the same initial instance and the same exchange phase. This subsection isolates that common part and proves the four statements that Appendices D.6, D.7, F and G use. 

The common setting 

Let a0 = a(0) and b0 = b(0) be the distinct initial positions, let d = ∥b0 − a0∥ > 0, and let 

A = 2 a0 − b0, B = 2 b0 − a0,

so that A, a 0, b 0, B occur in this order at equal spacing d on the initial line. Throughout this appendix we place a coordinate on that line with 

A = 0 , a0 = d, b0 = 2 d, B = 3 d, 

and write P := {0, d, 2d, 3d} for the set of designated points. The exchange configurations are 

C1 = ( B, b 0), C2 = ( a0, A ), C3 = ( B, A ).

In the notation of Definition D.29 these are A = A0, B = B0 and Ci = γi for i = 1 , 2, 3, while γ0 = ( a0, b 0)is the initial configuration. We write mr (T ) for the number of nonzero Move operations performed by r

up to time T .Both tasks require the following, which we refer to collectively as condition (P1). (P1-a) In the initial configuration the points A, a 0, b 0, B lie on a line in this order at equal spacing d.(P1-b) Every nonzero Move ends at a point of P : before T1 for S-RDV, and throughout the execution for VSS. (P1-c) At some time T1 one of C1, C 2, C 3 is formed, with the corresponding cumulative counts (ma(T1), m b(T1)) equal to (1 , 0), (0 , 1) or (1 , 1). 41 A a0 b0 Bγ0

C1

C2

C3

a bababab

Figure 2: The initial configuration γ0 and the three exchange configurations C1–C3 admitted in the first phase. 

The first nonzero Move Lemma D.21 (A nonzero Move must occur) . In every execution, starting from the initial configuration, of a deterministic algorithm that solves a task containing (P1) , at least one robot performs a nonzero Move. Proof. If neither robot ever performs a nonzero Move , then ma(T ) = mb(T ) = 0 for every T . Each of the three clauses of (P1-c) requires ma(T1) + mb(T1) ≥ 1, so no valid T1 exists, a contradiction. 

Lemma D.22 (Uniqueness of the first Move) . In every execution satisfying S-RDV or VSS , each nonzero Move with earliest completion time lands at B if performed by a, and at A if performed by b. If the Look associated with that Move observes the initial geometric configuration, its normalized displacement is 

(λ, θ ) = (2 , 0) .Proof. Such a Move exists by Lemma D.21. Suppose that a completes one at the earliest completion time t∗, and let p be its landing point. Then ma(t∗) = 1; the peer may complete its first nonzero Move 

simultaneously. Any valid exchange time T1 satisfies T1 ≥ t∗, since both completed-move counts are zero before t∗.As ma(T1) ≥ 1, the witness cannot be C2. The remaining possibilities, C1 and C3, both require 

a(T1) = B, ma(T1) = 1 .

Suppose that p̸ = B. Then T1 > t ∗, and reaching B requires another nonzero Move of a. In S-RDV, every movement remains in [ A, B ]. In VSS, every movement is a straight segment with both endpoints in P ⊆ [A, B ]. In either case, the exterior endpoint B cannot be reached in the interior of a nonzero movement. Thus reaching B requires completing another nonzero Move , giving ma(T1) ≥ 2, a contradiction. Therefore p = B. The argument for a first completion by b is symmetric and gives the landing point A.Now assume that the Look associated with this Move observes the initial geometric configuration. For a Move of a, the observed positions are a0, b 0, and 

B − a0 = 2( b0 − a0).

Hence the displacement is twice the observed distance in the direction of the peer, so its normalized form is ( λ, θ ) = (2 , 0). For a Move of b, the same conclusion follows from A − b0 = 2( a0 − b0). The proof uses the fact that every Move is contained in [ A, B ]. For S-RDV, this is an explicit requirement. For VSS, it follows from straight-line motion and the requirement that every Move ends in 

P ⊆ [A, B ], since the initial positions also belong to P .

Replaying the first Move 

The next lemma is the obstruction used whenever an exchange task is refuted in a model without peer-state information. It says that in FSTA and OBLOT the required first Move is necessarily imitated by the peer, which carries the pair out of the admissible region. 42 Lemma D.23 (The peer replays the first Move) . Let M ∈ {FSTA , OBLOT } and consider an execution that starts from the initial configuration, consists of alternating singleton activations (Lemma E.8), and is given normalized frames (Lemma E.1(1)) up to and including the round following the first nonzero Move. If that first nonzero Move complies with Lemma D.22, then the robot activated in the next round receives an isomorphic local input, applies the same displacement, and lands at 4d if it is b, and at −d

if it is a. In particular the landing point lies outside P and outside the segment [A, B ].Proof. As long as no nonzero Move has occurred, the configuration is unchanged, so under alternating singleton activation the two robots traverse the same sequence of states. In FSTA and OBLOT the internal state of the peer is not part of the local input, which consists of the relative position of the peer together with the robot’s own color in FSTA and of the relative position alone in OBLOT .Suppose the first nonzero Move is performed by a. By Lemma D.22 it lands at B = 3 d, while b is still at b0 = 2 d, so the distance is again d. In the next round b is activated, and since the adversary may supply the normalized frame, the local input of b is isomorphic to the one that a received. By determinism b applies the same displacement ( λ, θ ) = (2 , 0) and lands at 2 d + 2 d = 4 d. We have 4 d / ∈ P

and 4 d / ∈ [A, B ] = [0 , 3d]. The case in which b moves first is symmetric: b lands at A = 0, a is still at d,and a lands at d − 2d = −d / ∈ P ∪ [A, B ]. Lemma D.23 refutes the endpoint requirement (P1-b) of VSS and the interval requirement of S-RDV in a single step, and it is a safety violation, so it may be placed before any liveness argument in a concatenated execution (Lemma E.18(1)). 

# D.6 Swap–Rendezvous (S-RDV) 

This subsection gives the formal specification of S-RDV, informally described in Section 4.2, and proves Lemma 4.2. Recall from Appendix D.5 the auxiliary points A = 2 a0 − b0 and B = 2 b0 − a0, the exchange configurations C1 = ( B, b 0), C2 = ( a0, A ), C3 = ( B, A ), the coordinate A = 0, a0 = d, b0 = 2 d, B = 3 d

on the initial line, and the set P = {0, d, 2d, 3d} of designated points. 

Definition D.24 (Swap–Rendezvous) . An execution solves S-RDV if the following conditions hold. 1. Throughout the execution, both robots remain in the segment [ A, B ]. 2. There exists a time T1 > 0 at which one of C1, C 2, C 3 is formed, with the corresponding cumulative nonzero-move counts (1 , 0), (0 , 1), or (1 , 1). Before T1, every robot that is not moving is located at one of A, a 0, b 0, B .3. There exists T2 > T 1 such that a(t) = b(t) for every t ≥ T2.Conditions 1 and 2 are condition (P1) of Appendix D.5, with (P1-b) imposed before T1, and condition 3 is the terminal rendezvous requirement that distinguishes S-RDV from VSS. Consequently Lemmas D.21, D.22, and Lemma D.23 all apply to S-RDV. 

Impossibility Lemma D.25. S-RDV /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Stable Rendezvous is impossible in FCOM A under variable disorientation, regardless of the number of colors and without restricting the algorithm to the L-class [MSW26]. Suppose, for contradiction, that an algorithm A solves S-RDV in FCOM A. Every initial configuration of Rendezvous with distinct robot positions is an instance of S-RDV, since the auxiliary points A and B

are determined solely from the initial positions and are not landmarks available to the robots. Moreover, every execution satisfying S-RDV eventually satisfies 

a(t) = b(t) for all sufficiently large t, 

which is exactly the stable Rendezvous requirement. Hence A would solve Rendezvous in FCOM A, a contradiction. 43 Lemma D.26. S-RDV /∈ Sol (FSTA R).Proof. Suppose an FSTA R algorithm A solves S-RDV. The local input of an FSTA robot is the pair consisting of the relative position of the peer and its own state; the state of the peer is not included. The adversary chooses the alternating singleton activation {a}, {b}, {a}, . . . with an empty fully synchronous prefix (Lemma E.8). 

First stage. The configuration is unchanged until the first nonzero Move . The initial configuration is symmetric under the rotation by π about the midpoint combined with exchanging a and b, and this rotation preserves chirality, so in the normalized frame (Lemma E.1(1)) both local inputs have the form (e, own state) and are isomorphic. The two robots start from the same initial state s0, so a in round 2k − 1 and b in round 2 k both pass from sk−1 to sk. If no state occurring in s0, s 1, . . . produces a nonzero Move , the configuration never changes, contradicting Lemma D.21. Let j be the least index that produces a nonzero Move ; then a performs the first nonzero Move of the whole execution in round 2j + 1, from state sj .

Second and third stages. Since A is assumed to solve S-RDV, Lemma D.22 forces that Move to land at B = 3 d. The execution is an alternating singleton activation from the initial configuration under normalized frames whose first nonzero Move complies with Lemma D.22, which is exactly the hypothesis of Lemma D.23. Hence the robot b, activated in the next round, receives an isomorphic local input, performs the same displacement and lands at 4 d. Since 4 d / ∈ [A, B ], condition 1 of Definition D.24 is violated. 

Lemma D.27. S-RDV /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. Suppose an OBLOT F algorithm A solves S-RDV. The local input of an OBLOT robot is its current snapshot alone. In round 1 the local input of both robots is “the peer at distance d”. A zero 

Move would leave all later inputs unchanged and the configuration fixed forever, so by Lemma D.21 the 

Move is nonzero, and by Lemma D.22 the robot a lands at B and b lands at A, forming C3 at distance 3d.The local input in round 2, “the peer at distance 3 d”, coincides with the round-1 local input of the initial instance of initial distance 3 d, namely A′ = 0, a′(0) = 3 d, b′(0) = 6 d, B′ = 9 d, and an OBLOT 

robot cannot distinguish the two. In that instance the same argument as above shows that the first 

Move is nonzero, so by Lemma D.22 the output is twice the observed distance in the direction of the peer. Applying the same local rule in round 2 of the actual execution, a lands at 3 d − 6d = −3d and b

lands at 0 + 6 d = 6 d. Both lie outside [ A, B ] = [0 , 3d], which violates condition 1 of Definition D.24. 

Solvability Lemma D.28. S-RDV ∈ Sol (FCOM S ), as witnessed by Algorithm 7. Proof. Each robot carries a light with color set {0, 1, 2, 3}, initially 0. Apart from the coincidence test, every rule depends on the color of the peer alone. 

(i) Both robots always lie in [A, B ]. Under Ssynch , all active robots compute from the configuration at the beginning of the round. Rules R and 2 do not move, while rules 1 and 3 choose the midpoint and the peer’s position, respectively. These destinations belong to [ A, B ] whenever both current positions do. For rule 0, the peer still has color 0 and remains at its initial position. While that color remains 0, successive applications of rule 0 alternate the observing robot between its initial position and the opposite outer point. The possible configurations and destinations are (a, b ) = ( d, 2d) : a → 3d = B, (a, b ) = (3 d, 2d) : a → d = a0,

(a, b ) = ( d, 2d) : b → 0 = A, (a, b ) = ( d, 0) : b → 2d = b0.

Thus every destination lies in [ A, B ]. Since each Move follows a straight segment, both robots remain in [A, B ] throughout the execution. 44 Algorithm 7 S-RDV for FCOM under Ssynch (robot r)

Model and scheduler: FCOM , Ssynch .

(ii) The first phase. Let T1 be the end of the first round. If both robots are activated, C3 is formed with cumulative nonzero-move counts (1 , 1). If only a is activated, C1 is formed with counts (1 , 0); if only b is activated, C2 is formed with counts (0 , 1). Moreover, up to T1, every stationary robot is at its initial position or at its first destination, all of which belong to P . Hence T1 is a valid witness for the exchange phase. 

(iii) The second phase. By fairness, both robots are activated within finite time. At the end of the first round by which this has happened, the color pair is (1 , 1), (1 , 2), or (2 , 1). From (1 , 1), simultaneous activation brings both robots to their common midpoint; a singleton activation produces (1 , 2) or (2 , 1). Consider (1 , 2), the other case being symmetric. Until a is activated, only b moves, repeatedly halving the distance while preserving the color pair. By fairness, a is eventually activated: it sees color 2, stays in place, and changes its color to 3. Whether or not b is activated in that round, the resulting color pair is (3 , 2). Until the next activation of b, robot a remains stationary. When b is activated, it sees color 3 and moves to a; if a is activated simultaneously, it sees color 2 and stays. Thus the robots occupy the same point at the end of this round. Fairness ensures that this occurs at some finite time T2 > T 1.

(iv) Stability of the rendezvous. At the round boundary T2, both robots have completed their Moves and occupy the same point. Every subsequent Look therefore observes coincidence, so rule R applies and neither robot moves again. Hence a(t) = b(t) for every t ≥ T2.Lemma D.25, Lemma D.26, Lemma D.27 and Lemma D.28 together prove Lemma 4.2. 

# D.7 Visit Swap and Stop (VSS) 

Definition D.29 (VSS) . Let a0 = a(0) and b0 = b(0) be distinct, and define A0 = 2 a0 − b0, B 0 =2b0 − a0. The four points A0, a 0, b 0, B 0 occur in this order at equal spacing on the initial line. Write 

γ(t) = ( a(t), b (t)) and set 

γ0 = ( a0, b 0), γ1 = ( B0, b 0),γ2 = ( a0, A 0), γ3 = ( B0, A 0).

Every completed Move must end at one of the four designated points. The execution must first reach one of γ1, γ 2, γ 3, with cumulative nonzero move counts (1 , 0), (0 , 1), or (1 , 1), respectively. After this exchange configuration has been formed, the robots must eventually remain permanently stationary in a 45 configuration belonging to {γ0, γ 1, γ 2, γ 3}. The restriction to designated points concerns Move endpoints; straight-line travel between them, including passage through the peer’s position, is allowed. 

Impossibility Lemma D.30. VSS /∈ Sol (FSTA R).Proof. Use the alternating singleton execution of the proof of Lemma D.26. Its first stage holds word for word, since it uses only (P1), so the first nonzero Move of the whole execution is performed by a in round 2j + 1 under a normalized frame. Since the algorithm is assumed to solve VSS, Lemma D.22 forces that 

Move to land at B = 3 d, and Lemma D.23 then applies: the robot b, activated in the next round, receives an isomorphic local input, performs the same displacement and lands at 4 d / ∈ P . This contradicts the requirement of Definition D.29 that every completed Move end at one of the four designated points. 

Lemma D.31. VSS /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. As in the proof of Lemma D.27, in round 1 the robot a lands at B and b lands at A, forming C3,and in round 2 the robots cannot distinguish their input from the round-1 input of the initial instance of initial distance 3 d, so a lands at −3d and b lands at 6 d. Neither point belongs to P , so the endpoint requirement of Definition D.29 is violated.

Lemma D.32. VSS ∈ Sol (FCOM A), as witnessed by Algorithm 8. Proof. Here D is the distance recorded at the robot’s own Look .

(i) A robot of color 0 is at its initial position and is not moving. The color of a robot is 0 until its first 

Comp , and since tL < t C < t B < t E , its first Move starts after that Comp . Hence an observation of a peer of color 0 is an observation of a robot that has not begun any Move . In particular the observations during a peer’s movement that Asynch permits cannot affect the decision of rule 0, and no atomicity assumption is used below. 

(ii) When rule 0 fires, D = d and the destination belongs to P . By (i) the peer is at its initial position, and the observing robot is itself not moving at its own Look , so by induction it stands at a landing point of P . The possible cases and landing points are the four listed in part (i) of the proof of Lemma D.28, all of which belong to P . In particular each firing of rule 0 toggles the robot between its own initial position and B, respectively between its own initial position and A. The destination is computed from the snapshot taken at the Look , so delaying the Move (Corollary E.6) does not change the landing point. 46 (iii) The first phase. Let t be the earliest completion of a nonzero Move, say by a. By (ii), a(t) = B.If b has not begun a nonzero Move, then C1 holds at t. Otherwise, b is executing its first nonzero Move towards A (or completes it at t), and its color is already 1. Hence a remains at B until b completes this Move, when C3 holds with cumulative counts (1 , 1). The case in which b completes first is symmetric. 

(iv) Permanent stopping. A robot applies rule 0 only if its Look took place before the first Comp 

of its peer. That Comp occurs at some finite time T , and each cycle is finite, so each robot performs only finitely many Look operations before T and therefore fires rule 0 finitely often. Under either rule the activated robot sets its color to 1, and after the last of these finitely many nonzero Move operations has been completed, only rule 1 fires and neither robot moves again. Hence Stopped( a) and Stopped( b)hold. 

(v) The terminal configuration. By (ii), a robot that fired rule 0 an even number of times is back at its initial position, and one that fired it an odd number of times stands at B, respectively at A. The four combinations are exactly C0, C 1, C 2, C 3, and Definition D.29 admits each of them as a terminal configuration. Lemma D.30, Lemma D.31 and Lemma D.32 together prove Lemma 3.4 (5). 

# E Auxiliary Execution Constructions 

# E.1 Normalization and Distance Updates 

Throughout this appendix we fix two robots a, b at distinct positions and write d = ∥a − b∥ for their distance. We call the color-dependent part of a robot’s observation its visible state and denote it by 

γ. Thus OBLOT has no visible state, the visible state of an FSTA robot is its own color, that of an 

FCOM robot is the color of its peer, and that of a LUMI robot is the pair of both colors. Each lemma below carries applicability conditions on the starting state, namely the positions, the colors, and the pending operations, and on the scheduler class. When a lemma is applied inside a construction, these conditions are verified against the state actually reached by the preceding prefix. 

Lemma E.1 (Normalization) . Fix a reference vector e̸ = 0 of the plane. For every algorithm A there are constants λγ ≥ 0 and θγ , determined by A alone, and a map g on visible states, such that the following hold. (1) Suppose that at an activation of a robot r the peer is at global vector w̸ = 0 and the visible state of r

is γ. Then the adversary may choose the local coordinate system of r so that the global displacement of r is 

v = λγ Rθγ (w),

where Rθ denotes the rotation by angle θ, and the color set by r is g(γ).(2) If w = 0 , the local output is determined by γ alone, but the adversary may choose the direction and the magnitude of the resulting global displacement. In particular, two robots with equal visible states can be given equal global displacements. Unless stated otherwise, we use this coordinate system, called the normalized frame .Proof. (1) Place r at the origin. By chirality there is a unique orientation-preserving similarity ψ of the plane that maps w to e, and variable disorientation admits ψ as a local coordinate system. The local input is then ( e, γ ), so by determinism the output is a fixed pair ( qγ , g (γ)). Writing qγ = λγ Rθγ (e) and observing that ψ−1 is a composition of a rotation and a positive scaling, hence commutes with rotations, the global displacement is 

ψ−1(qγ ) = λγ Rθγ

(ψ−1(e)) = λγ Rθγ (w).

(2) If w = 0, the geometric part of the input is the origin in every local coordinate system, so the output 

q depends only on γ. The global displacement ψ−1(q) changes direction and magnitude with the choice 47 of ψ, and choosing the same ψ for two robots with equal visible states makes the two displacements equal. 

Lemma E.2 (Contraction ratios) . Put 

c1(γ) := 

√

1 − 2λγ cos θγ + λ2 

> γ

, c2(γ) := 

√

1 − 4λγ cos θγ + 4 λ2 

> γ

.

Under normalized frames the following hold. (i) If exactly one robot with visible state γ completes a cycle while its peer is stationary, then d 7 →

c1(γ) d.(ii) If two robots, both with visible state γ, complete cycles simultaneously from the same snapshot, then 

d 7 → c2(γ) d and the midpoint is invariant. Moreover 

c1(γ) = 0 ⇐⇒ (λγ , θ γ ) = (1 , 0) , c2(γ) = 0 ⇐⇒ (λγ , θ γ ) = (1 /2, 0) .

(iii) Identify the plane with the complex plane and Rθ with multiplication by eiθ . The transformations of the difference vector in (i) and (ii) are multiplication by 

σ1(γ) := 1 − λγ eiθγ , σ2(γ) := 1 − 2λγ eiθγ ,

and cj (γ) = |σj (γ)|. When cj (γ)̸ = 0 , put ψ(γ) := arg σ1(γ) and φ(γ) := arg σ2(γ). If ψ̸ ≡ 0(mod 2 π), then there is k ≥ 1 with cos( kψ ) ≤ − 12 , and for every θ0 there is k ≥ 1 with cos( θ0 +

kψ ) ≤ 0. The same statements hold for φ.Proof. Let w := b − a and ˆ u := w/d .(i) By Lemma E.1(1) the new difference vector is d(ˆ u−λR θ ˆu), whose squared norm is d2(1 −2λ cos θ +

λ2). (ii) The displacement of b is the negative of that of a, so the new difference vector is d(ˆ u − 2λR θ ˆu)and a + b is unchanged. The conditions for vanishing follow immediately. (iii) The first assertion is the computation in (i) and (ii) read as multiplication of complex numbers, together with |1 − λe iθ |2 = 1 − 2λ cos θ + λ2 and |1 − 2λe iθ |2 = 1 − 4λ cos θ + 4 λ2.For the second assertion, suppose first that ψ/ 2π is irrational. Then {kψ mod 2 π}k≥1 is dense in [0 , 2π): by the pigeonhole principle some integer multiple of ψ is arbitrarily close to 0, and its own multiples then fill the circle. Both assertions follow. Suppose next that ψ/ 2π = p/q in lowest terms with q ≥ 2. Then {kψ mod 2 π}k≥1 = {2πj/q : 0 ≤ j < q }. If q is even, the value j = q/ 2 belongs to this set and cos π = −1. If q is odd, and therefore q ≥ 3, the points are spaced by 2 π/q , so one of them lies within π/q of π, where cos ≤ − cos( π/q ) ≤ − cos( π/ 3) = − 12 . For the version with an offset, the set {θ0 + kψ mod 2 π}k≥1 consists of q equally spaced points with gap 2 π/q ≤ π, so the closed arc [π/ 2, 3π/ 2], which has length π, contains at least one of them, and there cos ≤ 0. The argument for φ

is identical. From now on we fix the reference vector of Lemma E.1 to be e = (1 , 0). The choice is only for concreteness: the arguments below use nothing about e beyond the existence, for each visible state, of the pair ( λγ , θ γ ) supplied by that lemma, and the displacement of an activation is λγ Rθγ (w) for the actual peer vector w.

Remark E.3 (The rule of EPG ∗ in a normalized frame) . Write ( λ⋆, θ ⋆) := ( 12 , π ), the displacement that moves a robot away from its observed peer by half the observed distance; this is the rule of Algorithm 5. In a round in which both robots are activated from the same configuration and apply the same rule ( λ, θ ), the midpoint is preserved and the difference vector is multiplied by σ2 = 1 − 2λe iθ (Lemma E.2(ii),(iii)). The expansion condition di+1 = 2 di of Definition D.8 therefore holds at such a step if and only if σ2 = 2, that is if and only if ( λ, θ ) = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆). In that case σ1 = 1 − λ⋆eiθ⋆

= 32 , so a solo activation multiplies the distance by c⋆
