= 32 and ψ⋆ ≡ 0. 48 E.2 Admissible Execution Segments 

The constructions of Appendices F and G are concatenations of a small number of execution segments. This subsection records each segment together with the scheduler classes that admit it and the state it requires at its beginning. Recall from Section 2.2 that we write F, R, S, A, A LC , A M for the scheduler classes, and that the phase times of the i-th cycle of a robot r are denoted tL(r, i ) < t C (r, i ) < t B (r, i ) <tE (r, i ). 

Lemma E.4 (Solo activation interval) . Let K ∈ { S, A LC , A M , A }, and suppose that at the end of a finite admissible prefix the robot b has no pending operation. For every k ≥ 1 there is an execution that extends this prefix by an interval in which a completes k consecutive LCM cycles while b is never activated, followed by a fair tail. Throughout this interval the position and the color of b are unchanged, and Stop b(t) holds at every time t of the interval. Proof. Under Ssynch it suffices to take {a} as the activation set of k consecutive rounds. Under Asynch 

and its subclasses, delay the next Look of b and place the k cycles of a one after another inside that delay. The atomicity constraints only forbid a Look of one robot during prescribed phases of the other robot’s cycle, and b executes no cycle during the interval, so no constraint is violated. Appending a tail that activates both robots infinitely often makes the execution fair. The class K = R is excluded. Indeed, in an Rsynch execution the same robot cannot be activated twice in a row after the fully synchronous prefix, by Lemma E.8 below. 

Lemma E.5 (Delay before a phase) . Under Asynch and its subclasses, the adversary may insert a finite delay before any of the first three phase times of a cycle of a robot r, that is, before tL(r, i ), inside 

(tL(r, i ), t C (r, i )) , or inside (tC (r, i ), t B (r, i )) , and place events of the other robot inside that delay, within the limits imposed by the atomicity constraints. No delay is inserted inside a Move operation: once the Move of r begins at tB (r, i ), the robot proceeds to the destination computed at tC (r, i ) and the Move ends at tE (r, i ) without interruption. The values used by r in the later phase are those obtained in the earlier one. Proof. The times tL, t C , t B , t E are chosen by the adversary subject only to the ordering constraint and to finiteness of each phase (Section 2.2); in particular the adversary controls the duration tE (r, i ) − tB (r, i )of a Move operation, but not an interruption of it, which is why every delay used here is placed before 

tB (r, i ). The second assertion is the determinism of the algorithm: the destination and the new color computed at tC (r, i ) do not depend on anything occurring afterwards. 

Corollary E.6 (Delayed move with a stale destination) . Let K ∈ { ALC , A M , A }. The adversary can construct a fair execution in which a computes its destination at tC (a, i ), is then delayed, the robot b

completes at least one nonzero Move during the delay, and only afterwards does a move to the destination it had already computed. The delay is placed before the Move of a begins, and the Move itself, once begun, is carried out in full. Writing wold and wnew for the vector from a to b at tC (a, i ) and at tB (a, i )

respectively, the difference vector after the move of a is 

wnew − λγ Rθγ (wold ).

Proof. Apply Lemma E.5 with the delay placed between tC (a, i ) and tB (a, i ), and place the whole cycle of b inside it. What ALC forbids is a Look in ( tL(a, i ), t C (a, i )], and what AM forbids is a Look in [tB (a, i ), t E (a, i )]; the delay interval ( tC (a, i ), t B (a, i )) meets neither, so the segment is admissible under 

ALC , AM and A alike. The displacement formula is Lemma E.1(1) applied to the snapshot that a

obtained at its Look , which is released unchanged when the Move begins. 

Lemma E.7 (Observation during movement) . Let K ∈ { ALC , A }, and suppose that b performs a nonzero Move from pB to pE̸ = pB while a has no pending operation. For every μ ∈ (0 , 1) there is a fair execution in which a performs a Look at a time when b is at 

pμ = (1 − μ)pB + μp E .

49 Proof. The movement of b is an uninterrupted continuous motion along the segment [ pB , p E ], whose duration the adversary may choose, so by the intermediate value theorem there is τμ ∈ (tB (b, i ), t E (b, i )) with b(τμ) = pμ. Setting tL(a, j ) = τμ is unconstrained under A, and it is admissible under ALC as well, because τμ > t C (b, i ). 

Lemma E.8 (Alternating singleton activation) . Let K = R. For every p ≥ 0 there is a fair execution whose first p rounds are fully synchronous and whose tail activates {a}, {b}, {a}, . . . or {b}, {a}, . . . . In this tail, (i) the distance d is updated by the factor c1 at every round; (ii) the robot that is not activated remains stationary during that round; (iii) if the displacement of the moving robot is v, then a + b is translated by v;(iv) the two colors may evolve independently. Once the alternating tail has begun, no fully synchronous round is admissible. Proof. This is the definition of Rsynch in Section 2.2, and (i)–(iv) follow from the fact that exactly one displacement is applied in each round of the tail; for (i) this is Lemma E.2(i). 

# E.3 Invisibility and State Orbits 

Several constructions insert a segment that the other robot cannot detect, so that its next activation reproduces the behavior it would have had without the insertion. The following notion isolates exactly what has to be preserved. 

Definition E.9 (r-invisible segment) . A finite execution segment σ is r-invisible if the normalized local input that r receives at its next activation is the same before and after σ.

Lemma E.10 (Invisible transitions) . Let M ∈ {FSTA , OBLOT } . If r is not activated during σ and the peer r occupies a position distinct from that of r both before and after σ, then σ is r-invisible. Consequently, however the internal state of r changes during σ, the robot r computes the same output at its next activation. Proof. Neither Obs FSTA (r) = ( pr , c r ) nor Obs OBLOT (r) = pr contains the color of the peer, so only two components have to be preserved. The own color cr is unchanged because r is not activated. The relative position of the peer may change by a similarity during σ, but as long as it is nonzero it is mapped to e in the normalized frame by Lemma E.1(1). The last assertion is then the determinism of the algorithm. 

Lemma E.11 (Period-wise pumping in FSTA ). Let M = FSTA and K ∈ { S, A LC , A M , A }. Consider a solo activation interval of a (Lemma E.4) in which every Look of a observes its peer at a position different from its own. Along such an interval, the color of a evolves as 

c0, g (c0), g 2(c0), . . . , 

which is eventually periodic, say with preperiod p and period q. For every n ≥ 1 such that the first 

p + nq activations of a satisfy this condition, there is a fair execution in which a is activated alone p + nq 

times; at the end of that interval the color of a is γp := gp(c0), and b, never being activated, stays at its position throughout the interval. If moreover a never reaches the position of b during the interval, then the inserted interval is b-invisible. Proof. The visible state of an FSTA robot is its own color. Every Look of a in the interval observes a nonzero relative position of its peer, so by Lemma E.1(1) each activation updates the color by g. The color sequence is therefore the orbit of g and is eventually periodic. After p + nq steps the orbit is back at γp. The robot b is not activated, so its own color is unchanged, and the color of a is not part of the input of b. The relative position of a changes by a similarity, but as long as it is nonzero the adversary can present the same normalized input to the next activation of b; hence the interval is b-invisible by Lemma E.10. 50 E.4 Synchronous Prefixes and Repetition 

This subsection collects the segments in which the same rule is applied repeatedly, either by both robots simultaneously or by one robot alone. 

Lemma E.12 (Maintaining coincidence) . Suppose that a(t0) = b(t0), that neither robot has a pending operation at t0, and that the scheduler class permits activating both robots in every round from t0 on. The last condition always holds under Fsynch ; under Ssynch and the subclasses of Asynch it holds for the full activation set with aligned phase times; under Rsynch it holds only while the execution is still in its fully synchronous prefix. Suppose moreover that in each such round the two local outputs are either both zero or both nonzero; this is in particular the case when the two visible states are equal. Then the adversary can construct a fair execution with a(t) = b(t) for every t ≥ t0.Proof. At a coincidence the geometric part of both local inputs is the origin (Section 2.1). By Lemma E.1(2) the direction and the magnitude of the global displacement obtained from a nonzero local output are chosen by the adversary, so two nonzero outputs can be mapped to the same global displacement, while two zero outputs produce no movement. Activating both robots in every round therefore preserves the coincidence. If one output is zero and the other is not, the coincidence is destroyed, so the condition on the outputs cannot be omitted. In OBLOT the output at a coincidence is unique, hence that condition always holds. 

Lemma E.13 (Synchronous rigidity in OBLOT F ). Let M = OBLOT and K = F , so that the displace-ment rule is a single pair (λ, θ ) by Lemma E.16(a), and put c2 := c2(λ, θ ). The adversary can construct a fair execution in which, in every round k with d(k) > 0, the midpoint is fixed at m and d(k) = c k 

> 2

d(0) ,and in which exactly one of the following occurs. (a) λ = 0 : neither robot moves, and moves( a) = moves( b) = 0 .(b) c2 = 0 : in round 1 the two robots coincide at m, and the coincidence is maintained by Lemma E.12. (c) 0 < c 2 < 1: the sequence d(k) decreases strictly to 0 but remains positive, and moves( a) = moves( b) = ∞.(d) c2 ≥ 1: d(k) ≥ d(0) for every k, and moves( a) = moves( b) = ∞.Moreover the adversary lets the two robots start and complete their Moves simultaneously, with the same fractional progress along their segments. The two robots are then simultaneously stationary only before and after the common Move interval of a round, so every joint stop of the execution carries one of the round-boundary distances d(k). In particular the restriction of d to Σab is monotone. Proof. Give the normalized frame in every round, so that Lemma E.2(ii) applies to each round. In case (b) the execution is fully synchronous and the model is OBLOT , so the output condition of Lemma E.12 is satisfied. In cases (c) and (d) a nonzero Move occurs in every round, and since tE (r, i ) < t L(r, i + 1) these are counted as distinct moves. Activating both robots in the same round with a common speed profile is admissible under Fsynch .In a round containing a nonzero Move the two Move intervals then coincide, so the instants at which both robots are stationary are those preceding the common Move, where the distance is d(k), and those following it, where it is d(k + 1). In a round containing no nonzero Move the distance is constant and equal to both. Hence d takes only the values d(k) = c k 

> 2

d(0) on Σ ab , and that sequence is monotone in each of the four cases: constant in (a), constant at 0 from round 1 on in (b), strictly decreasing in (c), and non-decreasing in (d). 

Lemma E.14 (Synchronous rigidity in the colored models) . Let M ∈ {LUMI , FCOM , FSTA} and let 

K be any scheduler class that permits activating both robots in every round; under Rsynch this means during the fully synchronous prefix. The adversary can construct a fair execution in which, in every round 

k, the two robots carry the same color γk, where γ0 = c0, and the distance satisfies d(k +1) = c2(γk) d(k);

51 moreover, in every round k with d(k) > 0 the midpoint is fixed at m. While the robots occupy distinct positions, their common color satisfies γk+1 = g(γk). After a coincidence, the common color instead follows the transition map for the zero geometric observation. In either case the two colors remain equal. Since coincidence is thereafter maintained, the common color sequence (γk) is eventually periodic. Proof. Activate both robots in every round and give them the normalized frames at every Look at which they are distinct. The designated initial colors are equal, and equal colors give the two robots equal visible states, so by Lemma E.1 their next colors are equal as well; while the robots are distinct, this common next color is g(γk). While the robots are distinct, the distance update and the invariance of the midpoint are Lemma E.2(ii). If c2(γk) = 0, the robots coincide at m at the end of round k; from then on their visible states are still equal, so the coincidence is preserved by Lemma E.12 and d(k+1) = 0 = c2(γk) d(k)holds trivially, whereas the common position may move, since by Lemma E.1(2) the adversary chooses the displacement at a coincidence. At every such round both robots observe the zero geometric configuration with equal visible states, so they apply the same transition map for that observation and their colors remain equal. Before a coincidence the common color sequence is an orbit of g; after it, the sequence is an orbit of the transition map for the zero observation. Either is the orbit of a map on a finite set, so (γk) is eventually periodic. 

Lemma E.15 (Solo pumping in the models without private memory) . Let M ∈ {FCOM , OBLOT } 

and K ∈ { S, A LC , A M , A }. Let γ⋆ be the visible state of a at the beginning of a solo activation interval of Lemma E.4, and put λ := λγ⋆ , θ := θγ⋆ and c := c1(γ⋆). Assume that the robots occupy distinct positions at the beginning of the interval. Throughout the interval Stop b(t) holds, so the peer remains stationary. At every cycle whose Look observes distinct positions, the vector from a to b is transformed by I − λR θ .Hence d(j) = c j d(0) up to and including the first landing at the peer, if such a landing occurs. Moreover: (a) if λ = 0 , then a does not move during the interval; in particular, if M = OBLOT then b obeys the same rule, so the configuration stays at ι along every fair infinite execution; (b) if c = 0 , the first activation makes a reach its peer. The normalized rule does not determine subsequent outputs at coincidence; those must be treated separately; (c) if λ > 0 and c > 0, every activation in the solo interval is a nonzero Move, and any prescribed finite number of such Moves can be scheduled. Proof. While b is not activated, the visible state of a is constant: the model OBLOT has no visible state, and in FCOM it is the color of b, which does not change, since a cannot observe its own color. Hence, at every Look that observes distinct positions, the same pair ( λ, θ ) is applied, and the transformation of the vector from a to b and the distance formula follow from Lemma E.1(1) and Lemma E.2(i). In case (c) the distance stays positive, so every Look observes distinct positions and every displacement has length 

λd (j) > 0. Concerning (a), when M = FCOM the color of a may change during the interval, so the subsequent behavior of b is not determined by λ alone; this is why immobility of the whole configuration is asserted only for M = OBLOT .

Lemma E.16 (A single displacement rule) . In each of the following two cases, the displacement rule of the activated robot is a single pair (λ, θ ):(a) any algorithm with M = OBLOT ;(b) an algorithm with M = FCOM , together with an interval during which the peer is not activated. When these activations are performed one at a time, with each cycle completed before the next begins, and every Look observes distinct positions, successive landing distances are multiplied by the same factor 

c1(λ, θ ), so that d(k) = c k 

> 1

d(0) after k activations. Their sequence is therefore monotone and cannot al-ternate between strict decreases and strict increases. A landing at the peer ends this normalized argument and must be handled separately. If λ = 0 , the robot does not move at all. 

52 Proof. By Lemma E.1 the displacement is determined by the pair ( λγ , θ γ ) attached to the visible state 

γ. In case (a) the model OBLOT has a unique visible state, so the family of constants degenerates to a single pair. In case (b) the visible state of an FCOM robot is the color of its peer alone, since it cannot observe its own color; as long as the peer is not activated that color is unchanged, hence the visible state is constant (Lemma E.15). For such singleton cycles, the landing distance is multiplied by c1(λ, θ )by Lemma E.2(i), so the landing distances form a geometric sequence and are monotone. If λ = 0 the displacement is identically zero. 

Corollary E.17 (The first nonzero rule along a synchronous prefix) . Let M ∈ {LUMI , FCOM , FSTA} 

with the designated common initial color c0, and let the execution begin with rounds activating both robots, as in Lemma E.14, so that the common color evolves by γ0 = c0, γk+1 = g(γk) and is eventually periodic. Exactly one of the following holds. (a) λγk = 0 for every k. Then this fair execution contains no nonzero Move at all, the configuration remains ι forever, and both robots are permanently stationary. (b) There is a least t with λγt̸ = 0 . Then the configuration at the beginning of round t is still ι,both robots are stationary with no pending operation, and their common visible state is γt. Hence any segment of Appendix E.2 may be appended there. In particular, replacing round t by a solo activation interval of a (Lemma E.4) leaves the local input of a unchanged, so the rule applied at its first activation is (λγt , θ γt ) with λγt̸ = 0 ; the visible state then stays equal to γt throughout the interval when M = FCOM (Lemma E.15), whereas for M = FSTA it follows the orbit of g

(Lemma E.11). In case (a) the joint-stop sequence of Definition D.2 has the single term C0, so the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails. In case (b) the configuration is unchanged until round t, so the first activation of a in the solo activation interval is the first nonzero Move of the execution and it is performed while b is not activated; Lemma 5.1(1) then makes the expansion condition fail at i = 0 . Since the color sequence is eventually periodic, the dichotomy is decided by a finite check. For M = OBLOT the same statement holds with the single rule (λ, θ ) of Lemma E.16(a), case (a) being λ = 0 .Proof. By Lemma E.14 the two robots carry the same color γk in round k, so by Lemma E.1(1) both apply ( λγk , θ γk ), and the displacement is nonzero exactly when λγk̸ = 0. In case (a) no displacement is ever nonzero, so the configuration never changes. In case (b) every round before t applies a zero displacement, hence the configuration at the beginning of round t is still ι; the robot a then has the same peer position and the same visible state as it would under simultaneous activation, so its rule there is (λγt , θ γt ). The finite check is the eventual periodicity of the orbit of g on a finite color set. 

# E.5 Preservation and Continuation of Violations 

Every common-failure execution in Appendices F and G is a concatenation of the segments collected above. The following lemma states when such a concatenation is admissible and which violations survive it. 

Lemma E.18 (Concatenation of segments) . The violations exhibited by a common-failure construction split into two kinds. A safety violation , namely landing at a forbidden point, a forbidden overtaking, a forbidden coincidence, a forbidden transition, or an expansion that moves the center of gravity, occurs at a finite time and cannot be repaired by any continuation. A liveness violation , namely the failure of infinitely many alternations, of perpetuity, of reaching, of convergence, or of stable gathering or stopping, is decided only on an infinite execution. The following hold. 1. A finite segment that forces a safety violation may be followed by any continuation permitted by the scheduler class, in particular by a fair tail, and the violation is preserved. 

53 2. Segments are concatenated starting from the state actually reached by the preceding prefix. Posi-tions, colors, internal states, and pending operations are carried over and are not reset. A segment may be appended only if its applicability conditions hold in that state and if the concatenation is an execution of the scheduler class under consideration. In particular, no fully synchronous round may be placed after an alternating singleton tail under Rsynch (Lemma E.8); a solo activation interval (Lemma E.4) requires the robot that is not activated to have no pending operation; and a delayed move (Corollary E.6) requires K ∈ { ALC , A M , A }.3. A liveness violation requires an explicit fair infinite continuation along which the violation persists; an arbitrary fair tail does not suffice. When a safety violation of one predicate is combined with a liveness violation of another, the segment forcing the safety violation is placed first, and the infinite continuation witnessing the liveness violation is built from the state that this segment leaves. Since the predicate of each task is evaluated relative to the fixed initial instance ι, the argument for a later segment remains valid even when earlier segments have already changed the configuration. Proof. A safety violation is witnessed by a finite prefix, and every extension of that prefix contains it; this gives (1). Claim (2) records that a concatenation is an execution of the algorithm in the given scheduler class exactly when each stage is admissible from the state already reached. The conditions listed are precisely those required by the corresponding segment lemmas. For (3), a liveness property is not decided by any finite prefix, so an infinite continuation violating it has to be exhibited. Finally, the task predicates refer to ι and to the trajectory, not to the configuration at which a segment happens to begin; hence a change of configuration by an earlier segment does not invalidate the argument for a later one. 

# E.6 The Solo-Expansion Obstruction 

Proof of Lemma 5.1. (1) Say ¯ r completes no nonzero Move , so ¯ ri+1 = ¯ ri. Consecutive terms of the sequence are distinct, hence ri+1 ̸ = ri and ai+1 + bi+1 = ( ai + bi) + ( ri+1 − ri)̸ = ai + bi.

(2) For K = R take the execution of Lemma E.8 with an empty fully synchronous prefix; every round activates exactly one robot. For K ∈ { S, A LC , A M , A } the adversary places the whole cycle of one robot after the whole cycle of the other, alternately: while one robot runs its cycle the other performs no operation, so no Look falls inside a Move interval of the peer and every atomicity constraint of Section 2.2 is met; both robots are activated infinitely often, so the execution is fair. In such an execution the boundaries between consecutive cycles are times of Σ ab . If no nonzero 

Move ever occurs, the configuration never changes and ( Ci) has the single term C0; then the first condition of EPG (exactly two terms) and the first condition of EPG ∗ (an infinite sequence) both fail. Otherwise consider the first cycle containing a nonzero Move , performed by r. Every earlier cycle left the configuration unchanged, so the joint stop preceding that cycle realizes C0 and the joint stop following it realizes C1̸ = C0, while ¯ r ran no cycle in between. By (1), 12 (a1 + b1)̸ = c0, so the second condition of both tasks fails at i = 0. This is a safety violation and is preserved by any fair continuation. 

# E.7 The Two-Nonzero-Move Obstruction for SRO 

We use the notation of Definition C.1: ( Ci)i≥0 is the sequence of configurations at the times when neither robot is inside a nonzero Move , with consecutive repetitions removed, di = bi − ai, and Qi is the square with diagonal aibi, interior included, with Q−1 = R2. Condition C1 is di+1 = R−90 ◦ di, condition C2 is 

di+1 = 2 −1/2R−45 ◦ di, and condition C3 is ai+1 , b i+1 ∈ Qi−1.

Lemma E.19 (Two solo nonzero moves defeat SRO) . Consider a situation in which only one robot moves while its peer remains stationary. The displacement that can satisfy C1 is unique, namely the one rotating the segment by 90 ◦ clockwise about the peer, and the displacement that can satisfy C2 is also unique, namely the one rotating the moving robot by 90 ◦ clockwise about the midpoint. No other 

54 displacement satisfies C1 or C2 . If the same robot performs two consecutive nonzero Move operations while its peer stays still, the rotation condition and C3 cannot both be satisfied. Consequently, two solo nonzero moves of one robot violate ΦSRO , irrespective of the internal states. Zero Move operations do not advance the sequence (Ci).Proof. After each nonzero Move of the moving robot, both robots are stationary until the next Look , so the configurations reached after the two moves are consecutive terms Ci+1 , C i+2 of the sequence ( Ci). Condition C1 requires ∥di+1 ∥ = ∥di∥ together with di+1 = R−90 ◦ di, so with the peer held fixed the new position of the moving robot is uniquely determined. Condition C2 requires di+1 = 2 −1/2R−45 ◦ di

and determines it uniquely in the same way. Both conditions are purely geometric and do not depend on the internal states. Exchanging the names of the robots replaces each dj by −dj , which leaves the conditions unchanged, and they are also invariant under orientation-preserving similarities. We may therefore assume that a is the moving robot and that 

bi = (0 , 0) , ai = ( −1, 0) ,

so that di = (1 , 0) and 

Qi =

{

(x, y ) : ∣∣x + 12

∣∣ + |y| ≤ 12

}

.

If the first move satisfies C1 , then ai+1 = (0 , 1), and the second move gives ai+2 = (1 , 0) under C1 

and ai+2 = ( 12 , 12 ) under C2 . If the first move satisfies C2 , then ai+1 = ( − 12 , 12 ), and the second move gives ai+2 = ( 12 , 12 ) under C1 and ai+2 = (0 , 12 ) under C2 . In each of the four cases ai+2 /∈ Qi, so C3 

fails at Ci+2 . This is the computation of [FSW20, Lemma 23, Fig. 5]. The lemma is applied in two ways. When the two solo nonzero moves are available, it refutes Φ SRO 

outright. When only one solo move is available, its first part still shows that a displacement which is neither the 90 ◦ clockwise rotation about the peer nor the 90 ◦ clockwise rotation about the midpoint already violates the rotation condition. The degenerate case in which the moving robot lands on its peer is covered by this observation, since di+1 = 0 satisfies neither C1 , which requires ∥di+1 ∥ = ∥di∦ = 0, nor 

C2 , whose right-hand side is nonzero. 

# E.8 The Overtaking Construction 

The delayed move of Corollary E.6 becomes an obstruction when the destination computed before the delay is long compared with the distance that remains when the move is released. The following lemma records the two forms in which this is used, one for MCv and one for ZCC. 

Lemma E.20 (Overtaking) . Let K ∈ { ALC , A M , A }. Suppose that b computes at its Comp a destination from its saved Look snapshot, with global displacement v̸ = 0 , that its Move is then delayed as in Corollary E.6, and that only a is activated during the delay. Let w be the vector from b to a at the moment at which the Move of b is released. Keep a stationary while b executes this pending Move. 1. If ∥w∥ < ∥v∥/2, then the distance after the Move of b is larger than ∥w∥. Hence the second condition of ΦMCv , monotonicity, is violated. 2. If b(0) , the current position of b, and a lie on a line in this order, if v points towards a, and if 

∥w∥ < ∥v∥, then b lands beyond a, and at its landing time t one has dis (b(0) , b (t)) > dis (b(0) , a (t)) .Hence the second condition of ΦZCC , collision freedom, is violated. The delay is placed between tC (b, i ) and tB (b, i ). By Lemma E.5 and Corollary E.6, it is admissible under ALC , AM and A, and any prescribed finite number of complete cycles of a may be placed inside it. Throughout the delay b stays at its position, and its pending Move uses the destination computed at 

tC (b, i ), independently of these cycles. When M ∈ {FCOM , OBLOT } the visible state of a is constant during the delay (for FCOM it is the color that b adopted at tC (b, i )); when M = FSTA the color of a

follows the orbit of g as long as every Look of a observes distinct positions. Hence the distance formulas of Lemmas E.15 and E.11 apply to these cycles, although b has a pending operation. 

55 Proof. (1) After the Move of b, the vector from b to a is w − v, and 

∥w − v∥ ≥ ∥v∥ − ∥ w∥ > ∥w∥.

(2) On the line we have 

dis (b(0) , b + v) = dis (b(0) , b ) + ∥v∥ > dis (b(0) , b ) + ∥w∥ = dis (b(0) , a ),

which is the stated inequality at the landing time. The admissibility of the delay and of the cycles of a placed inside it is Lemma E.5 together with Corollary E.6; the last assertion of Lemma E.5 shows that the pending Move of b is unaffected by them. The distance formulas follow from Lemma E.1(1) and Lemma E.2(i), exactly as in the proofs of Lemmas E.15 and E.11, since b does not move during the delay. 

Remark E.21 (The reachability hypothesis is not automatic) . Neither ∥w∥ < ∥v∥/2 nor ∥w∥ < ∥v∥ holds by construction: they require the solo activations of a during the delay to bring a close enough to b.When the solo contraction ratio of a satisfies c < 1, we have d(j) = c j d(0) → 0 by Lemma E.15, and the hypothesis can be met. For M = FSTA the relevant quantity is the product Γ = ∏ 

> j

c1(γj ) over one color period, by Lemma E.11, and the hypothesis can be met when Γ < 1. The reachability condition and, for part (2), the required collinearity and order must be verified in each application. The continuation arguments for MCv and ZCC are given directly in Lemmas C.7 and C.17. 

# E.9 Avoiding a Countable Set 

Lemma E.7 lets the adversary choose, by a continuous parameter, the point of the peer’s movement at which an observation is made. Several impossibility arguments combine this freedom with the fact that the set of outcomes admitted by a task predicate is countable, or even finite. 

Corollary E.22 (Avoiding a countable set) . Let K ∈ { ALC , A }, suppose that b performs a nonzero Move, and suppose that a observes it with parameter μ ∈ (0 , 1) as in Lemma E.7. Let h : (0 , 1) → R

assign to μ some quantity of the resulting execution. If h is continuous and nonconstant, then for every countable set G ⊂ R the adversary can choose μ with h(μ) /∈ G.Proof. The image of a nonconstant continuous function on an interval contains a nondegenerate interval, hence is uncountable, and therefore is not contained in G.The corollary is used with the following two observation parameters. 

Impossibility of VEC . In the proof that VEC /∈ Sol (FSTA A) (Lemma C.22), h is a coordinate of the segment determined by the two landing points, which varies continuously and nonconstantly with μ.The set G consists of the finitely many successor configurations admitted by the transition condition of ΦVEC , so a single μ avoids all of them. In Lemma F.11 the same h is used to avoid one further value, namely the unique successor admitted by the expansion condition of EPG ∗, and the countably many μ

at which the two robots coincide. 

Impossibility of DMSD . In the proof that DMSD /∈ Sol (FSTA A) (Lemma C.25), the normalization 

a(0) = 0, b(0) = e1, D0 = 1 gives 

h(μ) = ∥c + μw ∥, c := e1 − 2λRe 1, w := λ2R2e1̸ = 0 .

Here ∥c + μw ∥2 is a quadratic polynomial in μ whose leading coefficient ∥w∥2 is positive, so h is noncon-stant on (0 , 1). The set G is the countable set D0 Z[1 /2], and a value h(μ) /∈ G witnesses a joint stop at which the ratio is not dyadic. In both applications the exceptional set is countable, so several such requirements can be imposed simultaneously: a finite or countable union of countable sets is still countable, and a single μ avoids all of them at once. This is what allows one execution to refute several task predicates, as in Appendices F and G. 56 F Common-Failure Executions for Two-Task Disjunction

58 F.1 Disjunctions Containing EPG ∗

This subsection treats the seven antichains {EPG ∗, P } with P ∈ C exp = {HET , OSPO , S-RDV , ZCC ,

VSS , VEC , CFSM }, which carry sixteen boundary obligations ( A, Y ) in total. The two FSTA R obliga-tions, for EPG ∗ ∨ S-RDV and EPG ∗ ∨ VSS, are proved in the main text by Proposition 5.2 and are not repeated here. Every construction below obtains its EPG ∗ violation from Lemma 5.1 in one of two ways. If the execution contains no nonzero Move at all, the joint-stop sequence has the single term C0 and the perpetuity condition fails, which is a liveness violation. If it does contain one, and the first one is performed by a robot while its peer performs none, then the configuration has not changed before it, so the joint stops surrounding it realize C0 and C1, and Lemma 5.1(1) gives 12 (a1 + b1)̸ = c0: the expansion condition fails at i = 0, which is a safety violation. Each proof only has to record which of the two cases applies and to refute the companion task on the same execution. Throughout this subsection and the next, we use the normalized frames of Lemma E.1 and the contraction ratios of Lemma E.2, and we write ( λ⋆, θ ⋆) = ( 12 , π ) for the rule of Algorithm 5 in a normalized frame, as recorded in Remark E.3: a round in which both robots are activated from the same configuration and apply the same rule ( λ, θ ) satisfies the expansion condition di+1 = 2 di if and only if ( λ, θ ) = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆), and a solo activation under that rule multiplies the distance by c⋆

= 32 with ψ⋆ ≡ 0. 

F.1.1 EPG ∗ ∨ HET (EH) Lemma F.1. EH /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Ssynch . Normalize the edge length of the hexagon of ι to 1, with a(0) = v0 and b(0) = v3; the initial colors are common. The visible state of an FCOM robot is the color of its peer, so while both robots are activated in every round their colors stay equal and follow a common sequence c0, g (c0), . . . (Lemma E.14), which is eventually periodic since the palette is finite. During this prefix the configuration stays at ι.If λcj = 0 for every j, this fair execution contains no nonzero Move , so moves( a) = 0 and the perpetuity condition of HET fails, while the joint-stop sequence has the single term C0 and the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails as well (Lemma 5.1(2)). Both are liveness violations of the same fair infinite execution, so Lemma E.18(3) is satisfied. Otherwise let t be the least j with λcj̸ = 0, replace round t by an interval in which a alone is activated 

k ≥ 2 times in a row (Lemma E.4), and append a fair tail. Since b is not activated, the visible state of a

F.1.2 EPG ∗ ∨ OSPO (EO) Lemma F.3. EO /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Ssynch . Run fully synchronous rounds with normalized frames. By Lemma E.14 the colors follow a common sequence c0, g (c0), . . . , and the configuration stays at ι while every Move is zero. If λcj = 0 for every j, continue forever. Then d is constant, so Φ OSPO has no strictly decreasing phase. The joint-stop sequence is ( C0), so the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails. This fair execution witnesses both liveness violations. Otherwise let t be least with λct̸ = 0. Replace round t by two consecutive solo activations of 

a (Lemma E.4), and append a fair tail. Since b is inactive, the visible state of a stays ct, so by Lemma E.16(b) the robot a applies ( λct , θ ct ) with λct̸ = 0 both times. The first of these activations is the first nonzero Move of the execution, and it is solo. Hence the expansion condition of EPG ∗ fails at i = 0 (Lemma 5.1(1)). For OSPO: if d vanishes during the interval, the collision-freedom condition of Φ OSPO fails. Otherwise the second activation is also a nonzero Move . At its completion ma = 2 and mb = 0, which no execution satisfying Φ OSPO admits (as in Lemma D.14). In the latter case, all these violations are safety violations, preserved by the tail (Lemma E.18(1)). Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.4. EO /∈ Sol (FSTA S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under Ssynch . Give normalized frames at every Look at which the robots are distinct. Then each robot advances along c0, g (c0), . . . , one step per activation and independently of the schedule, and its displacement depends only on its color. Let 

J := {i ≥ 0 : λgi(c0)̸ = 0 }.A collision violates the collision-freedom condition of Φ OSPO as a safety violation, so we assume none below. Any witness ( ti) of Φ OSPO has ma(ti) = mb(ti) = i. Hence 

|ma − mb| ≤ 1 at all times, and ma, m b are unbounded. (∗)

Case J = ∅. Run fully synchronously forever. No nonzero Move occurs. So d is constant, and the joint-stop sequence is ( C0). Both Φ OSPO and the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fail, and this execution witnesses both liveness violations. 

Case J̸ = ∅. Let j := min J, and activate a alone j + 1 times (Lemma E.4). The last of these activations is the first nonzero Move , and it is solo. So the expansion condition of EPG ∗ fails at i = 0 (Lemma 5.1(1)); this is a safety violation. 60 If |J| ≥ 2, let j′ be the second element of J. Continue activating a alone up to its ( j′ + 1)-st activation, then append a fair tail. The robot a performs a second nonzero Move while b performs none. This contradicts ( ∗), a safety violation. If |J| = 1, append a fully synchronous tail. Each robot performs at most one nonzero Move in the whole execution, contradicting the unboundedness in ( ∗). This tail witnesses the liveness violation, and the safety violation of EPG ∗ precedes it. Lemma E.18(1)(3) is satisfied in every case, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.5. EO /∈ Sol (OBLOT R).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Rsynch . An OBLOT robot has a unique visible state, so in a normalized frame the rule of A is a single pair ( λ, θ ) (Lemma E.16(a)). 

Case (λ, θ )̸ = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆). Since Rsynch contains the fully synchronous executions, take the execution of Lemma E.13, with both robots activated in every round and a normalized frame at every Look . By Lemma D.13 it violates Φ OSPO , the degenerate cases λ = 0 and c2 = 0 being treated there as well. For EPG ∗: if λ = 0 the configuration never changes, the joint-stop sequence has the single term C0 and the perpetuity condition fails; if λ̸ = 0 then C1̸ = C0 and, by Remark E.3, d1 = σ2d0 with σ2̸ = 2, so the expansion condition fails at i = 0. 

Case (λ, θ ) = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆). Take the alternating singleton activation with an empty fully synchronous prefix (Lemma E.8) and a normalized frame at every Look . Since λ⋆̸ = 0, the first activation of a is the first nonzero Move of the execution and is performed while b is not activated, so by Lemma 5.1(1) the expansion condition of EPG ∗ fails at i = 0. For OSPO: each activation moves the active robot directly away from its peer by half the observed distance, so d is non-decreasing throughout the execution and in fact d 7 → 32 d at every activation, by Lemma E.2(i) with σ1 = 1 − λ⋆eiθ⋆

= 32 . Hence no interval on which d strictly decreases exists and Φ OSPO fails. In both cases a single execution violates both predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

F.1.3 EPG ∗ ∨ S-RDV (ES) Lemma F.6. ES /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Asynch . Fix distinct initial positions 

a0, b 0, and put A = 2 a0 − b0, B = 2 b0 − a0, and P = {A, a 0, b 0, B }.Run fully synchronous cycles, choosing local frames that give the two robots identical normalized observations. Their colors remain equal. If every computed Move is zero, this fair, time-divergent execution keeps the robots at their distinct initial positions. Thus Φ S-RDV fails, and the joint-stop sequence consists only of C0, violating the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗.Otherwise, consider the first round with a nonzero output. Let both robots perform their Looks before either performs its Compute, and then let both complete their Computes. They obtain the same normalized displacement ( λ, θ ), with λ > 0, and the same new color γ′. Delay the Move of b until after 

a completes its Move. At the landing of a, both robots are stationary, although b still has a pending Move. Writing p̸ = a0 for the landing point of a, this joint stop has midpoint 

p + b0

2̸ = a0 + b0

2 .

Hence Lemma 5.1(1) gives a finite violation of Φ EPG ∗ , which persists under every extension. Suppose first that ( λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0). Since the Look of a observed the initial configuration, its landing point satisfies p̸ = B. No valid exchange time T1 precedes this first completion, because both cumulative nonzero-move counts were zero. If p /∈ P , the stationary-position requirement of S-RDV is already violated. Otherwise p ∈ { A, b 0}. The exchange configuration requiring zero Moves by a is now impossible. The other two require a to reach B with exactly one completed nonzero Move. However, any subsequent arrival at B while respecting the segment constraint requires a second completed nonzero Move: the exterior endpoint B cannot lie in the interior of a straight movement contained in [ A, B ]. Thus no 61 continuation satisfies the exchange phase. Complete the pending cycle of b and append an arbitrary admissible fair, time-divergent continuation. Both predicates fail on the resulting execution. It remains to consider ( λ, θ ) = (2 , 0). Then a lands at B. Without reactivating a, let b complete its already computed Move, whose destination is A. The robots are now at the distinct positions ( B, A ), both have color γ′, and neither has a pending cycle. Let Aγ′ have exactly the same transition rule as A, but designated initial color γ′. Apply the general impossibility of stable RDV in FCOM A used in the proof of Lemma D.25 to Aγ′ . It supplies an admissible fair, time-divergent execution from some distinct initial positions that fails stable rendezvous. An orientation-preserving similarity maps those positions to ( B, A ). Transforming the local frames accordingly preserves every local observation and color transition, so variable disorientation allows this counterexecution to be reproduced from the reached configuration. Append that execution to the finite prefix. The resulting execution is admissible, fair, and time-divergent. Its failure of stable rendezvous violates the terminal condition of S-RDV, while the earlier violation of Φ EPG ∗ persists. Thus a single execution violates both predicates, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. The remaining obligation of this pair, the class FSTA R, is Proposition 5.2.

Lemma F.8. EZ /∈ Sol (FSTA AM ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under AM , which admits synchronous activations of one robot at a time (Lemma E.4). Start with alternating singleton activations, each cycle being completed before the next one begins, with normalized frames at every Look that observes distinct positions. This interval activates one robot at a time, so Lemma 5.1 applies to it. If no nonzero Move ever occurs, the two robots stay at ι at distinct positions, so the convergence condition of ZCC fails, and the joint-stop sequence has the single term C0, so the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails. This fair execution witnesses both liveness violations. Assume therefore that a nonzero Move occurs. The first one is performed while the peer is not activated, so by Lemma 5.1(1) the expansion condition of EPG ∗ fails at i = 0, a safety violation. End the prefix at the completion of the cycle containing this Move . This is a joint stop, with no pending operation. Every Look in the prefix observes distinct positions: before the first nonzero Move the configuration is ι, and that Move is the last operation of the prefix. Since the visible state of an FSTA robot is its own color, Lemma E.1(1) shows that each robot that has been activated k times carries gk(c0). Hence the two colors lie on the same normalized color orbit, the orbit of c0 under g.If the robots coincide, use the coincidence continuation from Lemma C.16. Otherwise their colors lie on the same normalized color orbit, so the continuation statement proved in Lemma C.17 applies. In either case it supplies a fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ ZCC , with the original initial positions kept fixed. The safety violation established in the prefix persists (Lemma E.18(1)). A single fair infinite execution therefore violates both predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.9. EZ /∈ Sol (OBLOT R).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Rsynch . An OBLOT robot has a unique visible state, so in a normalized frame the rule is a single pair ( λ, θ ) (Lemma E.16(a)). 

Case (λ, θ )̸ = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆). Since Rsynch contains the fully synchronous executions, take the execution of Lemma E.13, with both robots activated in every round and a normalized frame at every Look . By Lemma C.15 that execution violates Φ ZCC , the degenerate cases λ = 0 and c2 = 0 being treated there as well. For EPG ∗: if λ = 0 the joint-stop sequence has the single term C0 and the perpetuity condition fails; if λ̸ = 0 then C1̸ = C0 and, by Remark E.3, d1 = σ2d0 with σ2̸ = 2, so the expansion condition fails at i = 0. 

Case (λ, θ ) = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆). Take the alternating singleton activation with an empty fully synchronous prefix (Lemma E.8) and a normalized frame at every Look . Since λ⋆̸ = 0, the first activation of a is the first nonzero Move of the execution and is performed while b is not activated, so by Lemma 5.1(1) the expansion condition of EPG ∗ fails at i = 0, a safety violation. For ZCC: by Remark E.3, c1 = 32 , so by Lemma E.2(i) each activation maps d 7 → 32 d; hence d → ∞ , the two robots do not converge to a single point, and the convergence condition fails. 63 In both cases a single execution violates both predicates, the safety violation being placed first and a fair infinite continuation of the liveness violation being exhibited, so Lemma E.18(1)(3) is satisfied and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

F.1.5 EPG ∗ ∨ VSS (EV) 

This pair has the single boundary class FSTA R, and that obligation is discharged in the main text by Proposition 5.2, which exhibits one admissible fair execution violating EPG ∗, S-RDV and VSS simulta-neously. 

F.1.6 EPG ∗ ∨ VEC (EX) Lemma F.10. EX /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Ssynch . The initial colors being common, while both robots are activated simultaneously their colors follow a common eventually periodic sequence 

γ0, g (γ0), . . . (Lemma E.14) and the configuration stays at ι.If λcj = 0 for every j, the two robots never move, the joint-stop sequence reduces to C0, and 

d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition of VEC fails, while the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails as well (Lemma 5.1(2)). Otherwise let t be the least j with λcj̸ = 0, replace round t by an interval in which a alone is activated 

k ≥ 3 times in a row (Lemma E.4), and append a fair tail. The configuration was unchanged before the interval, so the first activation of a in it is the first nonzero Move of the execution and it is performed while b is not activated; by Lemma 5.1(1) the expansion condition of EPG ∗ fails at i = 0, a safety violation. The visible state of a is constantly ct, so by Lemma E.16(b) it applies the same displacement rule at every activation. With the peer at rest, the only displacements that satisfy the transition condition of VEC are those rotating the segment clockwise by 45 ◦ or by 90 ◦; in particular if c1 = 0 then di = 0, while all the permitted targets are nonzero, so the transition condition fails. If a departs from those two displacements the transition condition fails at that point, and otherwise a reaches a configuration with di ⊥ d0 in at most two moves. At that moment both robots are at rest, but a repeats the same displacement at its next activation, so the stopping condition fails (Lemma C.21, [NNW26, Lemma 21]). All of these are safety violations and may be concatenated by Lemma E.18(1)(2). In each case a single execution violates both predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.11. EX /∈ Sol (FSTA A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under Asynch . Begin with a fully synchronous prefix, which is admissible under Asynch ; by Lemma E.14 the two robots always carry the same state 

s0, s 1, . . . .If λsk = 0 for every k, this fair execution contains no nonzero Move , the joint-stop sequence reduces to C0, and d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition of VEC fails and the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails as well (Lemma 5.1(2)). Otherwise take the least k with λsk > 0. All earlier rounds left the configuration at ι. Activate a

alone in round k, so that it performs the first nonzero Move of the execution, and use the freedom of 

Asynch in the choice of Look times: by Lemma E.7 the robot b observes a at a point pμ, μ ∈ (0 , 1), of its movement segment. Let b begin its Move before the Move of a ends, so that no time of Σ ab lies between the two landings and the configuration after both landings is the term C1 of the joint-stop sequence following C0.An FSTA robot cannot consult the internal state of its peer and retains no previously observed angle or distance, so the displacement of b is determined by pμ alone, and C1 varies continuously and non-constantly with μ. The transition condition of VEC admits only finitely many successors of C0,and the expansion condition of EPG ∗ admits exactly one, namely the configuration with midpoint c0

and difference vector 2 d0. Adding the countably many values of μ at which the two robots coincide, 64 Corollary E.22 provides a μ avoiding all of them, and both the transition condition of VEC (Lemma C.22, [NNW26, Lemma 22]) and the expansion condition of EPG ∗ fail at i = 0. Both are safety violations witnessed at a finite time, hence preserved by any fair tail (Lemma E.18(1)), and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.12. EX /∈ Sol (OBLOT R).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Rsynch . Take the alternating singleton activation of a and b with an empty fully synchronous prefix (Lemma E.8) followed by an arbitrary fair tail, and supply normalized frames. If λ = 0, then since OBLOT has no colors the two robots do not move under any schedule, the joint-stop sequence reduces to C0, and d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition of VEC fails and the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails. Assume λ̸ = 0. The activation of a in round 1 is then the first nonzero Move of the execution and 

b is not activated in that round, so by Lemma 5.1(1) the expansion condition of EPG ∗ fails at i = 0, a safety violation. For VEC: if c1 = 0, then a lands on the position of b in round 1 and d1 = 0, while all the targets permitted by the transition condition are nonzero, so that condition fails. Otherwise the only single displacements satisfying the transition condition are those rotating the segment clockwise by 45 ◦ or by 90 ◦. If a departs from them the transition condition fails at that point; otherwise a reaches a configuration with di ⊥ d0 in at most two moves, and since the same displacement is applied at every activation the robots move again immediately afterwards, so the stopping condition fails. All of these are safety violations. In every case a single execution violates both predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

F.1.7 EPG ∗ ∨ CFSM (EC) Lemma F.13. EC /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Ssynch . The initial colors being common, while both robots are activated simultaneously their colors follow a common eventually periodic sequence

# F.2 Disjunctions Containing SRO 

This subsection treats the five antichains {SRO , P } with P ∈ E ∪ P 3 = {S-RDV , VSS } ∪ {ZCC , VEC , CFSM }, which carry eight boundary obligations in total. Every construction below uses the geometric fact of Lemma E.19: with the peer at rest, two nonzero Move operations of the same robot cannot both satisfy the rotation condition and C3 , and a landing on the peer satisfies neither C1 ,which requires ∥di+1 ∥ = ∥di∦ = 0, nor C2 , whose right-hand side is nonzero. Zero Move operations do not advance the sequence ( Ci). Two of the eight obligations are discharged by Proposition 5.3, stated in Section 5.2: for every algorithm in FSTA S it exhibits one admissible fair execution violating SRO and both exchange tasks simultaneously, which settles the whole maximal negative boundary of SRO ∨ VSS and the FSTA S

obligation of SRO ∨S-RDV. We give its proof first; the remaining six obligations, including the FCOM A

obligation of SRO ∨ S-RDV, are proved afterwards. 

Proof of Proposition 5.3. Fix an algorithm and initial positions a0 = d, b0 = 2 d, with outer points 0 and 3d. Under normalized observations, write the state transition as s 7 → g(s) and the global displacement as Λ sw, where w points to the peer and Λ s = λsRθs .If the state orbit from the designated initial state contains no nonzero output, fair alternating acti-vations leave both robots stationary. Neither the exchange phase nor the perpetual-motion requirement of SRO is satisfied. Otherwise, let s be the first state in that orbit with a nonzero output. Activate only 

a until it completes that first nonzero Move ; all preceding Move operations are zero. We distinguish two cases. 

Case 1: Λs = 2 I. Robot a moves to 3 d. The relative segment reverses direction without shrinking, violating both permitted rotation types of SRO. Keep a inactive and activate b until it reaches state s;its preceding normalized outputs are zero, so the configuration is unchanged and b sees a peer at distance 

d. By Lemma D.23 the robot b then lands at 4 d, outside the interval allowed by S-RDV and outside the endpoint set allowed by VSS. All three violations are irreversible, so append a fair suffix. 

Case 2: Λs̸ = 2 I. The first nonzero Move makes the common exchange phase unattainable, so both exchange tasks have already failed. If it also violates SRO, append a fair suffix. Otherwise the robots remain distinct. If the normalized state orbit of a after this Move contains another nonzero output, keep 

b inactive until that output is executed; Lemma E.19 then violates SRO, and a fair suffix again completes the construction. If no further nonzero output occurs in that orbit, keep a inactive and let b traverse the same initial state sequence and perform its first nonzero Move . If this violates SRO, we are done. Otherwise the robots are still distinct and both are now in state g(s), from which every subsequent normalized output is zero. Fair alternating activations therefore leave the configuration stationary forever, violating the perpetual-motion requirement of SRO. This specifies a fair infinite continuation rather than an infinite solo execution. 66 F.2.1 SRO ∨ S-RDV (RS) Lemma F.15. RS /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Asynch . Run fully synchronous cycles with identical normalized observations until the first nonzero output. Throughout this zero-Move prefix, the positions remain unchanged and the colors remain equal. If no nonzero output occurs, the resulting fair, time-divergent execution violates both the perpetuity condition of SRO and the terminal rendezvous condition of S-RDV. Otherwise, identify the plane with C using orientation-preserving coordinates in which a0 = 0 and 

b0 = 1. The outer exchange points are then A = −1 and B = 2. In the first round with a nonzero output, let both robots perform their Looks before either performs its Compute, and then let both complete their Computes. Write z = λe iθ̸ = 0 for their common normalized displacement coefficient and γ′ for their common updated color. Their computed destinations are z for a and 1 − z for b.Delay the Move of b until strictly after a has landed, and do not reactivate a in the meantime. There is therefore a joint stop with positions ( z, 1), whose relative-position vector is 

d1 = 1 − z, d0 = 1 .

Case z̸ = 2 . The first nonzero Move of a does not land at B. Since its Look observed the initial configuration, the first-move obstruction of Lemma D.22 applies, so no extension can satisfy the exchange phase of S-RDV. If

Case z = 2 . The first joint-stop vector is d1 = −1, which satisfies neither C1 nor C2 . Thus SRO has already failed. Without reactivating a, let b complete its stored Move. The robots are now at (B, A ) = (2 , −1), both have color γ′, and neither has a pending cycle. Let Aγ′ have the same transition rule as A, but designated initial color γ′. Apply the general impossibility of stable RDV in FCOM A used in the proof of Lemma D.25. It gives a fair, time-divergent counterexecution of Aγ′ from distinct initial positions. As in the proof of Lemma F.6, an orientation-preserving similarity and corresponding choices of local frames reproduce this counterexecution from (B, A ). The reached colors and absence of pending cycles make it a valid continuation of our prefix. This continuation violates the terminal rendezvous condition of S-RDV, while preserving the earlier violation of SRO. In every case, a single admissible fair, time-divergent execution violates both predicates. Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. (Lemma E.14) while both robots are activated simultaneously, and the configuration stays at ι.If λcj = 0 for every j, no nonzero Move ever occurs, so the sequence of configurations reduces to

If c1̸ = 0, taking k ≥ 2 makes a perform two nonzero Move operations with its peer at rest, so by Lemma E.19 the second condition of SRO fails. Either way this is a safety violation in the prefix. For ZCC: put σ1 := 1 − λe iθ and c1 := |σ1|, as in the proof of Lemma F.7. Under Ssynch every activation of a is a complete cycle, so neither robot has a pending operation at the end of any of them. (b(0) − a(0) ) and b is still at b(0). Thus the robots still lie in the original initial segment in their original order, and neither has a pending operation. Apply the continuation property proved in Lemma C.16, keeping the original initial positions fixed. It supplies a fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ ZCC , while preserving the safety violation of Φ SRO already obtained in the prefix. The safety violation of Φ SRO occurs in the prefix and precedes every continuation used above, so Lemma E.18(1)(3) is satisfied and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.17. RZ /∈ Sol (FSTA AM ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under AM , which admits synchronous activations of one robot at a time (Lemma E.4). Begin with an interval in which b is never activated and a alone is activated, one complete cycle at a time, with normalized frames at every Look at which the robots are distinct. Since in FSTA activations that only update the color may be interleaved, the length of this interval is measured in nonzero Move operations and not in activations. Continue it until a lands on the position of b or completes its second nonzero Move , whichever comes first. As long as a has not landed on b, every Look of a observes distinct positions, so its color follows c0, g (c0), g 2(c0), . . . (Lemma E.11). If in this interval a lands on the position of b, then di+1 = 0 and neither C1 nor C2 can hold, so the second condition of SRO fails at that point, a safety violation. Suppose next that a never lands on b and performs at most one nonzero Move along its whole solo orbit, which includes the case in which no state produces a nonzero Move . Then the periodic part of the orbit of c0 contains no nonzero rule. Stop activating a once its color has entered that periodic part, and activate b alone the same number of times. The color of b follows the same finite orbit as long as its Look s observe distinct positions. If b lands on the position of a, the second condition of SRO fails as in the previous case, and the prefix is ended there as described below. Otherwise both robots now carry colors in the periodic part, at distinct positions, and neither has a pending operation. Continue with alternating singleton activations forever. Every Look observes distinct positions and every output is zero, so the positions remain fixed. This fair, time-divergent execution contains only finitely many 68 nonzero Move operations, so the sequence ( Ci) is finite and the perpetuity condition of SRO fails, while 

fr is eventually constant and the zig-zag condition of ZCC fails. In the remaining case a does not land on b and performs two nonzero Move operations with its peer at rest, so by Lemma E.19 the rotation condition and C3 cannot both hold, whatever the internal states, and the second condition of SRO fails. In each case with a safety violation of Φ SRO , the violation is produced by the last Move of the prefix constructed so far, and every Look in that prefix observed distinct positions. By Lemma E.1(1), each robot that has been activated k times carries gk(c0). End the prefix at this joint stop, with no pending operation. If the robots coincide, use the coincidence continuation from Lemma C.16. Otherwise their colors lie on the same normalized color orbit, so the continuation statement proved in Lemma C.17 applies. In either case it supplies a fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ ZCC , with the original initial positions kept fixed. The safety violation established in the prefix persists. All the blocks may be concatenated by Lemma E.18(1)(2)(3), and a single execution violates both predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

F.2.3 SRO ∨ VSS (RV) 

The whole maximal negative boundary of this pair is the single class FSTA S , and that obligation is discharged in the main text by Proposition 5.3. 

F.2.4 SRO ∨ VEC (RX) Lemma F.18. RX /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Ssynch . The colors follow a common se-quence γ0, g (γ0), . . . (Lemma E.14) while both robots are activated simultaneously, and the configuration stays at ι.If λcj = 0 for every j, no nonzero Move ever occurs, so the sequence of configurations reduces to C0

and both the perpetuity condition of SRO and the attainment condition of VEC, which would require 

d0 ⊥ d0, fail. Otherwise let t be the least j with λcj̸ = 0, replace round t by an interval in which a alone is activated 

k ≥ 3 times in a row (Lemma E.4), and append a fair tail. The visible state of a is constantly ct, so by Lemma E.16(b) it applies the same pair ( λ, θ ), with λ̸ = 0, at every activation. If c1 = 0, the robot a lands on the position of b at the first activation and di+1 = 0; both C1 , C2 

and all the targets permitted by the transition condition of VEC are nonzero, so the second conditions of both tasks fail simultaneously. If c1̸ = 0, the same robot performs two nonzero Move operations with its peer at rest, so by Lemma E.19 the second condition of SRO fails. For VEC the argument of Lemma F.10 applies: the only single displacements satisfying the transition condition rotate the segment clockwise by 45 ◦ or by 90 ◦,so if a departs from them the transition condition fails, and otherwise a reaches a configuration with 

di ⊥ d0 in at most two moves and then repeats the same displacement, so the stopping condition fails. All of these are safety violations. In each case a single execution violates both predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.19. RX /∈ Sol (FSTA A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under Asynch . Define the execution by starting, as in the first stage of Lemma C.25, with a finite fully synchronous prefix that freezes the common state of the two robots, which Asynch admits (Lemma E.14), then placing an observation-during-movement interval (Lemma E.7), and finally appending an arbitrary fair tail. If λs = 0 for every state s, the configuration never changes, so the sequence ( Ci) is finite and d0̸ ⊥ d0;both the perpetuity condition of SRO and the attainment condition of VEC fail. Otherwise, in the round of the first common state s with λs > 0, activate a alone so that it performs a nonzero Move , and let the robot b, in state s, observe a point pμ, μ ∈ (0 , 1), of that movement segment 69 (Lemma E.7, which is admissible for K = A). Delay the Move of b until after a has landed, so that the landing configuration of a is a joint stop. Do not reactivate a before b completes this Move. If this Move 

of a is neither the clockwise 90 ◦ rotation about the peer ( C1 ) nor the clockwise 90 ◦ rotation about the midpoint ( C2 ), then by Lemma E.19 the second condition of SRO already fails. If it is one of them, the robot b performs the displacement it computes from pμ, and the distance at the time both robots are at rest is a non-constant continuous function of μ. The distances permitted by SRO belong to the countable set generated from d by the ratios of C1 and C2 , so by Corollary E.22 some μ avoids that set and Φ SRO fails. In the same observation, an FSTA robot b cannot consult the internal state of its peer and retains no previously observed angle or distance, so its displacement is determined by pμ alone. The segment obtained after both landings varies continuously and non-constantly with μ, whereas the transition condition of VEC admits only finitely many targets, so by Corollary E.22 some μ avoids them. Both excluded sets being countable, a single μ ∈ (0 , 1) avoids both, and the transition condition of VEC fails in the same execution. Both are safety violations, so they may be concatenated by Lemma E.18(1)(2), and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

F.2.5 SRO ∨ CFSM (RC) Lemma F.20. RC /∈ Sol (FCOM S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Ssynch . The colors follow a common se-quence γ0, g (γ0), . . . (Lemma E.14) while both robots are activated simultaneously, and the configuration stays at ι.If λcj = 0 for every j, no nonzero Move ever occurs, so moves( a) = 0 ̸ = 1 and the single-move condition of CFSM fails, while the sequence of configurations reduces to C0 and the perpetuity condition of SRO fails. Otherwise let t be the least j with λcj̸ = 0, replace round t by an interval in which a alone is activated twice in a row (Lemma E.4), and append a fair tail. The visible state of a is constantly ct, so by Lemma E.16(b) it applies the same pair ( λ, θ ), with λ̸ = 0, at both activations. If c1 = 0, the robot 

a lands on the position of b at the first activation, so a(t) = b(t) at that time and the collision-freedom condition of CFSM fails, while di+1 = 0 satisfies neither C1 nor C2 and the second condition of SRO fails. If c1̸ = 0, both activations are nonzero Move operations, so moves( a) ≥ 2̸ = 1 and the single-move condition fails, while Lemma E.19 makes the second condition of SRO fail as well. All of these are safety violations witnessed at a finite time, hence preserved by the fair tail (Lemma E.18(1)). In each case a single execution violates both predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

# F.3 Disjunctions of the Remaining Exchange Tasks 

This subsection treats the eight antichains {E, P } with E ∈ E = {S-RDV , VSS } and P ∈ P 4 =

{OSPO , ZCC , VEC , CFSM }, which carry eighteen boundary obligations in total. The exchange com-ponent is always supplied by the common first phase of Appendix D.5: either no nonzero Move ever occurs, so that Lemma D.21 is contradicted, or the first nonzero Move does not land where Lemma D.22 requires, or it does and Lemma D.23 carries the peer out of the admissible region. The companion component is obtained from a solo activation interval, a delayed-move interval, an observation-during-movement interval, or a violation of a move-count or stopping requirement, according to the companion task and the boundary class. 

F.3.1 OSPO ∨ S-RDV (OS) Lemma F.21. OS /∈ Sol (FCOM A).

70 Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Asynch . Use coordinates A = 0, a0 = d,

b0 = 2 d, B = 3 d on the initial line. Run fully synchronous cycles with identical normalized observations (Lemma E.14). While every Move is zero, the positions stay fixed and the colors stay equal. 

No nonzero output. If no nonzero output occurs, continue forever. The robots stay distinct at constant distance. So Φ S-RDV fails (no rendezvous), and Φ OSPO fails (no decreasing phase). This execution witnesses both liveness violations. 

A nonzero output occurs. Otherwise let γ be the common color at the start of the first round with a nonzero output. Let ( λ, θ ), with λ > 0, be the normalized displacement for the initial configuration with peer color γ. Since A is deterministic, ( λ, θ ) is known before that round is scheduled. 

Case (λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0) . Replace the round by two solo activations of a (Lemma E.4), followed by a fair tail. The peer color stays γ, so a applies ( λ, θ ) twice (Lemma E.16(b)). The first of these Move operations observes ι and does not land at B. So the exchange phase of S-RDV is unsatisfiable (Lemma D.22). As in Lemma F.3, either a collision occurs or ma = 2 , m b = 0; either way Φ OSPO is violated. All these violations are safety violations. 

Case (λ, θ ) = (2 , 0) . Let both robots Look, and then both Compute; their colors become a common 

γ′. Delay the Move of b so that it begins strictly after the Move of a ends (Corollary E.6). The robot a

moves from d to 3 d while b rests at 2 d, so they collide, and Φ OSPO fails. Then let b complete its Move to A. The robots are now at ( B, A ) with common color γ′, and neither has a pending operation. Let Aγ′ be A with initial color γ′. The impossibility of stable RDV in FCOM A

used in Lemma D.25 gives a counterexecution of Aγ′ . Transport it to ( B, A ) by an orientation-preserving similarity, as in Lemma F.6, and append it. This continuation violates the terminal rendezvous condition of S-RDV and witnesses that liveness violation. The safety violation of Φ OSPO precedes it. Lemma E.18(1)(2)(3) is satisfied in every case, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.22. OS /∈ Sol (FSTA S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under Ssynch . Use coordinates A = 0, a0 = d,

b0 = 2 d, B = 3 d on the initial line, and normalized frames at every Look at which the robots are distinct. The next color and the displacement of a robot then depend only on its own color. 

First part. Activate {a}, {b}, {a}, . . . , which Ssynch admits. By the π-symmetry of ι and Lemma E.10, both robots follow c0, g (c0), . . . until the first nonzero Move .If there is no nonzero Move , continue forever. The robots stay at ι, so they never meet; Φ S-RDV 

fails by Lemma D.21, and Φ OSPO fails because d is constant. This execution witnesses both liveness violations. Otherwise let k be least with λgk (c0)̸ = 0. The robot a performs the first nonzero Move at its ( k +1)-st activation; let that Move complete, whether or not the two robots meet during it. • If its landing point is not B, Φ S-RDV is unsatisfiable (Lemma D.22). • If it is B, let b be activated next; it lands at 4 d / ∈ [A, B ] (Lemma D.23), so Φ S-RDV fails. Either way Φ S-RDV suffers a safety violation at a finite time. This argument uses only the landing points, so it does not assume that the two robots stay distinct. If a collision has occurred in the prefix constructed so far, the collision-freedom condition of OSPO has already failed. Both predicates have then suffered safety violations, so append an arbitrary fair, time-divergent continuation. Otherwise the first move of a did not land at B, since a move from d to 3 d would pass through the stationary robot at 2 d. The safety violation of Φ S-RDV has therefore already occurred, and b has not yet performed its ( k + 1)-st activation. Activate b once. If a collision occurs during this cycle, both predicates again have safety violations and an arbitrary fair tail completes the construction. Otherwise let T be the end of this cycle. Both robots have observed distinct positions, so they now carry γ = gk+1 (c0), neither has a pending operation, and ma(T ) = mb(T ) = 1. 71 Every execution satisfying Φ OSPO has ma(tj ) = mb(tj ) = j at its phase boundaries. Consequently 

|ma − mb| ≤ 1 at all times, and both counts are unbounded. Let Jγ = {i ≥ 0 : λgi(γ)̸ = 0 }. If |Jγ | ≥ 2, let i′ be its second element and activate a alone for 

i′ + 1 cycles, stopping this construction at the first collision if one occurs. A collision violates Φ OSPO ;otherwise the resulting counts are ma = 3, mb = 1, which also violate it. Append an arbitrary fair tail. If |Jγ | ≤ 1, use a fully synchronous fair tail. A collision again violates Φ OSPO . If no collision occurs, each robot performs at most one further nonzero move, contradicting the required unboundedness of the counts. Thus one fair infinite execution violates both predicates, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.23. OS /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Fsynch . Use coordinates A = 0, a0 = d,

b0 = 2 d, B = 3 d on the initial line and take the infinite fully synchronous execution of Lemma E.13 from this instance, with a normalized frame at every Look . The visible state of an OBLOT robot is unique, so the rule is a single pair ( λ, θ ) (Lemma E.16(a)), and by Lemma E.2(ii) the distance at the end of round k is d(k) = c k 

> 2

d(0). 

Violation of ΦOSPO . If the two robots ever occupy the same point, the collision-freedom condition fails at a finite time; this is a safety violation. It covers c2 = 0, where they coincide at the end of round 1 and the coincidence is maintained by Lemma E.12, and also the case in which they meet while travelling inside a round. Otherwise d(t) > 0 throughout. By Lemma E.13 every joint stop of the execution carries one of the round-boundary distances d(k), so the restriction of d to Σ ab is monotone. The turning times required by OSPO are joint stops, so by the criterion stated after Definition D.12 no strictly decreasing phase can be followed by a strictly increasing one, and Φ OSPO fails. This is a liveness violation, and the fully synchronous execution is itself the fair infinite continuation along which it persists. 

Violation of ΦS-RDV . If λ = 0 no nonzero Move ever occurs, so the first nonzero Move demanded by Lemma D.21 does not exist. This is a progress violation, witnessed by the same infinite fully synchronous execution. If λ̸ = 0, both robots perform a nonzero Move in round 1, so an admissible exchange configuration must be ( B, A ) = (3 d, 0) with move counts (1 , 1). When ( λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0) the landing of round 1 is not this configuration, so the requirement of Lemma D.22 is unmet. When ( λ, θ ) = (2 , 0) round 1 does land at (3 d, 0), but round 2 applies the same rule and sends a to 3 d + 2(0 − 3d) = −3d

and b to 0 + 2(3 d − 0) = 6 d, both outside [ A, B ] = [0 , 3d] (Lemma D.27). These are safety violations witnessed at a finite time. In each case a single execution violates both predicates, so Lemma E.18(1)(3) is satisfied and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim.
