Lemma F.27. SZ /∈ Sol (FSTA AM ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under AM . Synchronous activations of one robot at a time are admissible under AM (Lemma E.4), so a and b may be activated alternately, each cycle being completed before the next one begins, and, under normalized frames, the argument of Lemma D.23 applies unchanged. If no nonzero Move ever occurs, which in FSTA is equivalent to λgj (c0) = 0 for every j since the color sequence is eventually periodic (Lemma E.11), the two robots stay at ι at distinct positions, so Φ S-RDV 

fails and the convergence condition of ZCC fails as well. Otherwise the first nonzero Move is performed by a. Either it does not land at B = 3 d, so that the first-phase clauses become unsatisfiable by the argument of Lemma D.22, or it does and Lemma D.23 makes the robot b, activated next, land at 4 d / ∈ [A, B ]; in either case Φ S-RDV fails, a safety violation. Let the prefix end with the cycle that establishes this violation, that of a in the first case and that of b

in the second. Every Look in this prefix observes distinct positions: before the first nonzero Move the configuration is ι, in the second case b observes a at B̸ = 2 d, and a landing on the peer can only be the last Move of the prefix. Since the visible state of an FSTA robot is its own color, Lemma E.1(1) shows that each robot that has been activated k times carries gk(c0). 74 End the prefix at this joint stop, with no pending operation. If the robots coincide, use the coincidence continuation from Lemma C.16. Otherwise their colors lie on the same normalized color orbit, so the continuation statement proved in Lemma C.17 applies. In either case it supplies a fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ ZCC , with the original initial positions kept fixed. The safety violation established in the prefix persists (Lemma E.18(1)). A single fair infinite execution therefore violates both predicates, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.28. SZ /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Fsynch and take the infinite fully synchronous execution of Lemma E.13 with a normalized frame at every Look . The visible state of an 

OBLOT robot is unique, so the rule is a single pair ( λ, θ ) (Lemma E.16(a)). For this execution Φ ZCC fails by Lemma C.15, which exhausts the degenerate cases λ = 0 and c2 = 0, the violation of the collision-freedom condition when φ̸ ≡ 0, and the violation of the zig-zag condition when φ ≡ 0. For Φ S-RDV the argument is that of Lemma F.23: if λ = 0 no nonzero Move occurs and the first nonzero Move demanded by Lemma D.21 does not exist; if λ̸ = 0 and ( λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0), the nonzero Move 

of round 1 does not meet the requirement of Lemma D.22; and if ( λ, θ ) = (2 , 0), then in round 2 the robots land at −3d and 6 d, leaving [ A, B ] = [0 , 3d] (Lemma D.27). A single execution violates both predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

F.3.4 S-RDV ∨ VEC (SX) Lemma F.29. SX /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Asynch . Use coordinates A = 0, a0 = d,

b0 = 2 d, B = 3 d on the initial line. The initial colors being common, while both robots are activated simultaneously their colors follow a common eventually periodic sequence c0, g (c0), . . . (Lemma E.14) and the configuration stays at ι.If λcj = 0 for every j, continue forever. This fair execution contains no nonzero Move , so the two robots never occupy one and the same point permanently and Φ S-RDV fails, while the sequence of joint-stop configurations reduces to C0 and d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition of VEC fails as well. This execution witnesses both liveness violations. Otherwise let γ be the common color at the start of the first round with a nonzero output, and let (λ, θ ), with λ̸ = 0, be the normalized displacement for the initial configuration with peer color γ. Since 

A is deterministic, ( λ, θ ) is known before that round is scheduled. 

Case (λ, θ ) = (2 , 0) . Let both robots Look, and then both Compute; their colors become a common 

γ′. Delay the Move of b so that it begins strictly after the Move of a ends (Corollary E.6). At the landing of a neither robot is inside a Move , although b still has a pending one, so that instant is a joint stop and realizes C1 = (3 d, 2d), whence d1 = −d0. This is neither the clockwise rotation by 45 ◦ nor the one by 90 ◦, so the transition condition of VEC fails at i = 0, a safety violation at a finite time. Then let b complete its Move to A. The robots are at ( B, A ) with common color γ′, and neither has a pending operation. As in Lemma F.6, the impossibility of stable RDV in FCOM A used in Lemma D.25, applied to A with initial color γ′, supplies a counterexecution that an orientation-preserving similarity transports to ( B, A ). Appending it violates the terminal rendezvous condition of S-RDV. The safety violation of VEC precedes that liveness violation. 

Case (λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0) . Replace the round by an interval in which a alone is activated k ≥ 3 times in a row (Lemma E.4), followed by an arbitrary admissible fair, time-divergent tail. The visible state of a is constantly γ, so by Lemma E.16(b) it applies the same pair ( λ, θ ) at every activation. The first of these Move operations observes ι, and the cumulative nonzero-move counts being (1 , 0), an admissible exchange configuration would require a to land at B. It does not, so the exchange phase of S-RDV is unsatisfiable (Lemma D.22), a safety violation at a finite time. For VEC: if c1 = 0, the robot a lands on the position of b at the first activation and d1 = 0, while all the targets permitted by the transition condition are nonzero, so that condition fails. If c1̸ = 0, then 75 as in Lemma F.10 the only single displacements satisfying the transition condition rotate the segment clockwise by 45 ◦ or by 90 ◦; if a departs from them the transition condition fails, and otherwise a reaches a configuration with di ⊥ d0 in at most two moves and then repeats the same displacement, so the stopping condition fails. Either way this is a safety violation in the prefix, preserved by the tail. In every case a single execution violates both predicates, so Lemma E.18(1)(3) is satisfied and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.30. SX /∈ Sol (FSTA A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under Asynch , and let E0 be the execution of alternating singleton activations {a}, {b}, . . . under normalized frames (Lemma E.4); E0 is fair and is determined by A.If E0 contains no nonzero Move , the two robots stay at ι, so Φ S-RDV fails, while the sequence of configurations reduces to C0 and d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition of VEC fails as well; Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. Otherwise, as long as no nonzero Move has occurred the two robots traverse the same sequence of states, so the first nonzero Move is performed by a, which is activated first, from some state s with 

λs > 0, while b is in the same state s and has no pending operation. Let pE be its landing point. 

Case pE = B. Follow E0 up to the round following this Move . When a has landed, d1 = b0 −B = −d0,which is none of the successors admitted by the transition condition of VEC; and Lemma D.23 makes b,activated next, land at 4 d / ∈ [A, B ], so Φ S-RDV fails. 

Case pE̸ = B. Follow E0 up to the start of this Move , and by Lemma E.7 let b perform its Look 

while a is at pμ = (1 − μ)a0 + μp E , μ ∈ (0 , 1), delaying the Move of b until a has landed. The Move of 

a is then the nonzero Move with earliest completion time and it does not land at B, so Φ S-RDV fails by Lemma D.22. By Lemma E.1(1) the robot b lands at b0 + λsRθs (pμ − b0), an affine function of μ with nonzero slope λsRθs (pE − a0), so a suitable coordinate of the segment after both landings is continuous and non-constant in μ. The transition condition of VEC admits only finitely many successors, so by Corollary E.22 some μ avoids them and the transition condition fails (cf. Lemma C.22). In both cases the two violations are safety violations forced by a finite prefix, which extends to a fair execution by Lemma E.18(1)(2); Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.31. SX /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Fsynch and take the fully synchronous execution in which both robots are activated in every round (Lemmas E.13 and E.14), with a normalized frame at every Look . The violation of Φ S-RDV is that of Lemma F.23. For Φ VEC the argument of Lemma C.20 applies to the same execution: if λ = 0 the attainment condition fails, if c2 = 0 the transition condition fails, and otherwise the robots move again immediately after any orthogonal configuration, so the stopping condition fails, or no such configuration is ever reached and the attainment condition fails. A single execution violates both predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim.

b0 = 2 d, B = 3 d on the initial line. The colors follow a common sequence c0, g (c0), . . . Otherwise let γ be the common color at the start of the first round with a nonzero output, and let (λ, θ ), with λ̸ = 0, be the normalized displacement for the initial configuration with peer color γ.

Case (λ, θ ) = (2 , 0) . Let both robots Look, and then both Compute; their colors become a common 

γ′. Delay the Move of b so that it begins strictly after the Move of a ends (Corollary E.6). The robot a

76 moves from d to 3 d while b rests at 2 d, so the two robots occupy the same point while a travels and the collision-freedom condition of CFSM fails, a safety violation at a finite time. Lemma F.33. SC /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. For Φ CFSM , Lemma E.13 leaves only three possibilities, namely moves( r) = 0, a permanent coincidence of the two robots, and moves( r) = ∞,each of which violates the single-move condition or the collision-freedom condition of CFSM. F.3.6 ZCC ∨ VSS (ZV) Lemma F.34. ZV /∈ Sol (FSTA AM ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under AM . Synchronous activations of one robot at a time are admissible (Lemma E.4), so begin with alternating singleton activations, each cycle being completed before the next one begins, under normalized frames. If no nonzero Move ever occurs, condition (P1) of VSS fails by Lemma D.21, and the two robots remain at distinct positions, so the convergence condition of ZCC fails as well. Otherwise the first nonzero Move is performed by a. Either it does not land at B = 3 d, so that the first-phase clauses become unsatisfiable by the argument of Lemma D.22, or it does and Lemma D.23 makes the robot b, activated next, land at 4 d / ∈ P ; in either case the endpoint condition of VSS fails, a safety violation. Let the prefix end with the cycle that establishes this violation, that of a in the first case and that of b in the second. As in the proof of Lemma F.27, every Look in this prefix observes distinct positions, so by Lemma E.1(1) each robot that has been activated k times carries gk(c0). End the prefix at this joint stop, with no pending operation. If the robots coincide, use the coincidence continuation from Lemma C.16. Otherwise their colors lie on the same normalized color orbit, so the continuation statement proved in Lemma C.17 applies. In either case it supplies a fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ ZCC , with the original initial positions kept fixed. The safety violation established in the prefix persists (Lemma E.18(1)). A single fair infinite execution therefore violates both predicates, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim.

F.3.7 VSS ∨ VEC (VX) Lemma F.36. VX /∈ Sol (FSTA A).Proof. s with λs > 0, while b is in the same state s and has no pending operation. Let pE be its landing point.

F.3.8 VSS ∨ CFSM (VC) Lemma F.38. VC /∈ Sol (OBLOT F ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Fsynch and take the fully synchronous execution in which both robots are activated in every round (Lemmas E.13 and E.14), with a normalized frame at every Look .By Lemma E.13, if λ = 0 then moves( r) = 0 and the single-move condition of CFSM fails, while by Lemma D.21 the first phase of VSS cannot be achieved. If c2 = 0 the two robots coincide in round 1, so the collision-freedom condition of CFSM fails, and their landing point is the midpoint 1 .5d / ∈ P , so ΦVSS fails as well. Otherwise both robots perform a nonzero Move in every round, so moves( r) = ∞

and the single-move condition fails, while as in Lemma D.31 the landings of round 2 leave the set P of designated points. In each case a single execution violates both predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 78 F.4 Disjunctions Containing MCv 

This subsection treats the three antichains {MCv , P } with P ∈ { VSS , VEC , CFSM }, which carry four boundary obligations in total. For FCOM , the constructions use the delayed-Comp argument of Lemma C.6. For FSTA , after establishing the companion safety violation, we apply the continua-tion statement of Lemma C.7 from a configuration with distinct positions, a common internal color, and no pending operation. 

F.4.1 MCv ∨ VSS (MV) Lemma F.39. MV /∈ Sol (FSTA AM ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under AM , which admits both the simultaneous activation of the two robots and synchronous activations of one robot at a time (Lemma E.4). Use coordinates A = 0, a0 = d, b0 = 2 d, B = 3 d on the initial line, with P = {0, d, 2d, 3d}, and normalized frames at every Look at which the robots are distinct. Activate both robots simultaneously while every output is zero. By Lemma E.14 they carry the same color at every round boundary and the configuration stays at ι.If every output along this execution is zero, continue forever. Condition (P1) of VSS fails by Lemma D.21, and the two robots remain at distinct positions, so the convergence condition of MCv fails as well. This execution witnesses both liveness violations. Otherwise let γ be the common color at the start of the first round with a nonzero output, let ( λ, θ ), with λ̸ = 0, be the corresponding normalized displacement, and put c1 := c1(γ). Since A is deterministic, these are known before that round is scheduled. 

Case c1 = 0 , that is (λ, θ ) = (1 , 0) . Activate both robots in that round. Each moves to the position of its peer, so they exchange positions and the distance returns to d after passing through 0; the monotonic-ity condition of MCv therefore fails, a safety violation at a finite time. The cumulative nonzero-move counts are (1 , 1), so an admissible exchange configuration would have to be ( B, A ) = (3 d, 0), whereas the landing is (2 d, d ); no valid T1 exists and VSS fails by Lemma D.22. Append an arbitrary admissible fair, time-divergent tail, which preserves both violations. 

Case c1̸ = 0 . Define the execution as an interval of alternating singleton activations carried up to the round following the first nonzero Move , each cycle being completed before the next one begins, followed by the continuation described below. The first nonzero Move is performed by the robot a, which is activated first. Either it does not land at B, so that the first-phase clauses become unsatisfiable by the argument of Lemma D.22, or it does and Lemma D.23 makes the robot b, activated next, land at 4 d / ∈ P ; in either case the endpoint condition of VSS fails, a safety violation. Since c1̸ = 0, neither of these two Move operations lands on the peer, so the two robots are at distinct positions at the end of this interval. Both robots have now been activated equally often, and every Look so far has observed distinct posi-tions, so by Lemma E.1(1) they carry the same color. At this point the robots occupy distinct positions, carry the same internal color, and have no pending operation. Apply the continuation statement proved in Lemma C.7. It supplies a fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ MCv , while all safety violations established in the prefix persist. In every case a single execution violates both predicates, the violations being finite except where stated, so Lemma E.18(1)(2)(3) is satisfied and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

F.4.2 MCv ∨ VEC (MX) Lemma F.40. MX /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Asynch . Run fully synchronous cycles with normalized frames; by Lemma E.14 the two robots carry the same color at every round boundary, and the configuration stays at ι while every output is zero. 79 If every output along this execution is zero, continue forever. No nonzero Move occurs and the two robots remain at distinct positions, so the convergence condition of MCv fails, while the sequence of joint-stop configurations reduces to C0 and d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition of VEC fails as well. This execution witnesses both liveness violations. Otherwise stop the zero-Move prefix just before the first round with a nonzero output, let γ be the common color at this point, so that λγ > 0, and put c := c1(γ). Build from here the delayed-Comp interval of Lemma C.6: let b perform its Look and delay its Comp , the saved snapshot determining the nonzero displacement v = λγ Rθγ (wold ), where wold is the vector from b to a at that Look . Throughout the delay the position of b and its visible color γ are unchanged, so by Lemma E.16(b) the robot a may be activated alone for any prescribed finite number of complete cycles, applying the same pair ( λγ , θ γ ), with λγ̸ = 0, at each of them. Activate a alone k times, where k = 1 if c = 0 and otherwise k ≥ 3 is large enough for what follows, and append an arbitrary admissible fair, time-divergent tail. 

Violation of ΦVEC . If c = 0, the robot a lands on the position of b at the first activation and 

d1 = 0, while all the targets permitted by the transition condition are nonzero, so that condition fails. If 

c̸ = 0, then as in Lemma F.10 the only single displacements satisfying the transition condition rotate the segment clockwise by 45 ◦ or by 90 ◦; if a departs from them the transition condition fails, and otherwise 

a reaches a configuration with di ⊥ d0 in at most two moves and then repeats the same displacement, so the stopping condition fails. Either way this is a safety violation inside the interval. 

Violation of ΦMCv . If c > 1, the first of these Move operations already increases the distance between its starting and ending times. If c = 1, its starting and ending positions are distinct points at the same distance from the stationary robot b, so the midpoint of the movement segment is strictly closer to b than either endpoint and the distance increases between the time a passes that midpoint and the completion of the Move. In both cases the monotonicity condition fails. If c < 1, choose k above so large that the vector wk from b to a after k complete cycles satisfies 

∥wk∥ = c k∥wold ∥ < ∥v∥/2; for c = 0 this already holds with k = 1. Keep a stationary and let b complete its Comp using the saved snapshot, update its color, and perform the resulting Move with displacement 

v. The distance immediately before that Move is ∥wk∥, whereas at its completion it satisfies 

∥wk − v∥ ≥ ∥v∥ − ∥ wk∥ > ∥wk∥,

so the monotonicity condition fails again. Both violations are finite and occur inside the interval, so they may be concatenated by Lemma E.18(1) and are preserved by the fair tail. Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma F.41. MX /∈ Sol (FSTA A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under Asynch . Run a fully synchronous prefix, which Asynch admits; by Lemma E.14 the two robots carry the same state at every round boundary. If λs = 0 for every state s of that common sequence, this fair execution contains no nonzero Move :the robots stay at their distinct initial positions, so the convergence condition of MCv fails, and the sequence of configurations reduces to C0 with d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition of VEC fails as well. Otherwise truncate the prefix at the round in which both robots carry the first state s with λs > 0; the configuration is still ι. Activate a alone, so that it performs a nonzero Move from a0 to some pE̸ = a0,and by Lemma E.7 let b, in state s, perform its Look while a is at pμ = (1 − μ)a0 + μp E , μ ∈ (0 , 1), delaying the Move of b until a has landed. By Lemma E.1(1) the displacement of b is v = λsRθs (pμ − b0), so its landing point is affine in μ with nonzero slope λsRθs (pE − a0). We exclude the at most one μ with 

pμ = b0, so that v̸ = 0, and the at most one μ for which b lands at pE .

VEC. The successors admitted by the transition condition of VEC are finitely many and independent of μ, whether or not the configuration ( pE , b 0) is counted, so by Corollary E.22 some μ avoids them as well, and the transition condition fails at a finite time (cf. Lemma C.22). 

MCv. If pE = b0, then a lands on b, and the Move of b raises the distance from 0 to ∥v∥ > 0, so the monotonicity condition fails. Otherwise the robots are at distinct positions after both landings, and we use the following dichotomy, applied to every nonzero Move whose peer is at rest. Along such a Move the 80 distance to the stationary peer is non-increasing and then non-decreasing. If the landing point is not the point of the segment closest to the peer, the distance strictly increases at the end and the monotonicity condition fails; we may therefore assume that every such Move ends at its closest approach. Both robots have performed the same number of activations, and every Look so far has observed distinct positions, so by Lemma E.1(1) they carry the same color. At this point the robots occupy distinct positions, carry the same internal color, and have no pending operation. Apply the continuation statement proved in Lemma C.7. It supplies a fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ MCv , while all safety violations established in the prefix persist. In every case a single execution violates both predicates. Whenever a nonzero output occurs, the violation of Φ VEC is a safety violation at a finite time. In the case without nonzero Move , both live-ness violations are witnessed by the explicit fair execution given there. Otherwise Φ VEC has already suffered a safety violation. The continuation from Lemma C.7 witnesses either a finite monotonicity violation or a failure of convergence. Thus all predicates fail on the same fair infinite execution. Hence Lemma E.18(1)(2)(3) is satisfied, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

F.4.3 MCv ∨ CFSM (MC) Lemma F.42. MC /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Asynch . Run fully synchronous cycles with normalized frames; by Lemma E.14 the two robots carry the same color at every round boundary, and the configuration stays at ι while every output is zero. If every output along this execution is zero, continue forever. No nonzero Move occurs, so moves( a) = 0̸ = 1 and the single-move condition of CFSM fails, while the two robots remain at distinct positions and the convergence condition of MCv fails as well. This execution witnesses both liveness violations. Otherwise stop the zero-Move prefix just before the first round with a nonzero output, let γ be the common color at this point, so that λγ > 0, and put c := c1(γ). Build from here the delayed-Comp interval of Lemma C.6, exactly as in Lemma F.40: the robot b performs its Look and delays its Comp , the saved snapshot determining the nonzero displacement v = λγ Rθγ (wold ), and during the delay the robot 

a is activated alone k times, applying the same pair ( λγ , θ γ ) at each of them (Lemma E.16(b)). Take 

k = 1 if c = 0 and otherwise k ≥ 2 large enough for what follows, and append an arbitrary admissible fair, time-divergent tail. 

Violation of ΦCFSM . If c = 0, the robot a lands on the position of b at the first activation, so 

a(t) = b(t) at that time and the collision-freedom condition fails. If c̸ = 0, the k ≥ 2 activations are all nonzero Move operations, so moves( a) ≥ 2̸ = 1 and the single-move condition fails. Either way this is a safety violation inside the interval. 

Violation of ΦMCv . The three cases c > 1, c = 1 and c < 1 are those of Lemma F.40 and the arguments there apply verbatim: a single Move of a already breaks monotonicity when c ≥ 1, and when 

c < 1 the choice ∥wk∥ < ∥v∥/2 makes the distance grow from ∥wk∥ to ∥wk − v∥ ≥ ∥ v∥ − ∥ wk∥ > ∥wk∥

when b releases its Move .Both violations are finite and occur inside the interval, so they may be concatenated by Lemma E.18(1) and are preserved by the fair tail. Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

# F.5 DMSD ∨ CFSM (DC) 

This subsection treats the single antichain {DMSD , CFSM }, whose maximal negative boundary is the one class OBLOT A. The construction uses an observation made during the movement of the peer to produce a non-dyadic distance at a joint stop, and then one further nonzero Move of a robot that has already moved. 

Lemma F.43. (DMSD ∨ CFSM) /∈ Sol (OBLOT A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Asynch . Define the execution as a finite fully synchronous prefix freezing the common state (Lemma E.13), then an observation-during-movement 81 interval (Lemma E.7), then an interval consisting of one solo activation of a, and finally an arbitrary fair tail. The first prefix is the first stage of Lemma C.25; an OBLOT algorithm is an FSTA algorithm with |C| = 1, so that lemma applies here and yields Corollary C.26. Normalize a(0) = 0, b(0) = e1 and 

D0 = 1, and write λ := λs and R := Rθs for the frozen common state s.If λ = 0 for every state, no nonzero Move ever occurs, so moves( a) = 0 ̸ = 1 and the single-move condition of CFSM fails, while the distance stays equal to D0 at every joint stop and the progress condition (D2) of DMSD fails as well. Otherwise, in the observation interval the robot b performs a nonzero Move from b(0) to pE =

e1 − λRe 1, and by Lemma E.7 the robot a performs a Look at a time when b is at pμ = (1 − μ)b(0) + μp E

for a prescribed μ ∈ (0 , 1). If pμ ever coincides with the position of a, the two robots occupy the same point and the collision-freedom condition of CFSM fails at once, so we may assume that this does not happen; then λ > 0 makes the resulting Move of a nonzero, and a lands at λRp μ.Let both Move operations complete and delay the next Look of each robot; throughout that delay neither robot is inside a nonzero Move , so the whole interval is contained in Σ ab . The distance there is the non-constant continuous function 

h(μ) = ∥c + μw ∥, c := e1 − 2λRe 1, w := λ2R2e1̸ = 0 ,

of μ, as in the third stage of Lemma C.25. Since Z[1 /2] is countable, Corollary E.22 provides a μ ∈ (0 , 1) with h(μ)/D 0 /∈ Z[1 /2], so the dyadic condition (D1) of DMSD fails. As 0 ∈ Z[1 /2], this μ also keeps the two robots apart, so the distance stays positive. At that point a has already performed one nonzero Move . In the last interval a is activated alone; the distance being positive, in the normalized frame it receives a local input isomorphic to the earlier one, and λ > 0 makes this Move nonzero as well, so moves( a) ≥ 2̸ = 1 and the single-move condition of CFSM fails. Both are safety violations, so they may be concatenated by Lemma E.18(1)(2) and are preserved by the fair tail, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. This completes the forty-seven boundary obligations of the two-element antichains: fourteen in Ap-pendix F.1, six in Appendix F.2, eighteen in Appendix F.3, four in Appendix F.4, one here, and four in the main text, namely the two FSTA R obligations of Proposition 5.2 and the two FSTA S obligations of Proposition 5.3. Together they establish the case |A| = 2 of Proposition 6.2. 

# G Common-Failure Executions for Three-Task Disjunctions 

Each row lists one three-element antichain A, given by the corresponding disjunction BA of principal tasks from Table 2, together with NA = Max( Q \ UA) and, for each Y ∈ N A, the statement exhibiting one admissible fair execution of an arbitrary algorithm in Y that violates all three task predicates. Every Y ∈ N A also satisfies Y /∈ UA′ for each two-element antichain A′ ⊂ A, so with two exceptions the execution is the one already constructed for A′ in Table 4, and the proof only adds the failure of the third predicate on that execution. The exceptions are the boundary classes OBLOT S and OBLOT AM , which occur in no row of Table 4: for them the execution is constructed from scratch, once in Lemma G.21 and once in Lemma G.33, and reused by the later rows of the same family. The column sum is the number 34 of three-task boundary obligations certified in Table 9 (Appendix H.3) and recorded in Table 3; together with Table 4 this discharges all 47 + 34 = 81 boundary obligations, and Lemma 6.1 then gives

83 G.1 Three-Task Disjunctions Containing EPG ∗

Throughout Appendix G we use the following principle. If A = {X1, X 2, X 3} is a three-element antichain and Y ∈ N A, then for every two-element antichain A′ ⊂ A we have UA′ ⊆ UA and hence Y /∈ UA′ . In every triple treated below, except for the boundary classes OBLOT S and OBLOT AM , some such A′

satisfies Y ∈ N A′ , so the execution constructed for A′ at Y in Appendix F applies to the algorithms of 

Y and we only have to show that the same execution violates the third predicate as well. For OBLOT S

and OBLOT AM a new execution is constructed; those cases occur in Appendices G.2 and G.3. This subsection treats the eight antichains {EPG ∗, E, P } with E ∈ E = {S-RDV , VSS } and P ∈P4 = {OSPO , ZCC , VEC , CFSM }, which carry eighteen boundary obligations in total. When the reused pair is one of Cexp , the EPG ∗ component is the violation already recorded in Appendix F.1. Otherwise no nonzero Move other than the first one begins in the reused execution before the first nonzero Move 

has been completed. Hence either that execution contains no nonzero Move at all, in which case the joint-stop sequence is ( C0) and the perpetuity condition fails, or the completion of the first nonzero Move 

is a joint stop realizing C1 at which only one robot has moved, so by Lemma 5.1(1) the centre of gravity has shifted and the expansion condition fails at i = 0. The peer may still have a pending Move at that instant; this does not affect the argument, since a robot that has not begun to move is stationary. We keep the notation ( λ⋆, θ ⋆) = ( 12 , π ) of Remark E.3. 

G.1.1 EPG ∗ ∨ OSPO ∨ S-RDV (EOS) Lemma G.1. EOS /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Asynch . Use the execution of Lemma F.21, which violates Φ OSPO and Φ S-RDV . We show that it also violates Φ EPG ∗ . Recall c0 = 32 d.

No nonzero output. The joint-stop sequence is ( C0), so the perpetuity condition fails. The same infinite execution witnesses this. 

Case (λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0) . The first solo activation of a is the first nonzero Move . The joint stops before and after it realize C0 and C1, and b does not move in between. So the expansion condition fails at i = 0 (Lemma 5.1(1)). 

Case (λ, θ ) = (2 , 0) . The Move of b begins strictly after that of a ends. So the landing time of a is a joint stop, with configuration (3 d, 2d) and midpoint 52 d̸ = c0. The expansion condition fails at i = 0, as in Lemma F.6. In each case a single execution violates all three predicates; in the last two cases the violation of ΦEPG ∗ is a safety violation in the prefix, so Lemma E.18(1)(3) is satisfied, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.2. EOS /∈ Sol (FSTA S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under Ssynch . Use the execution of Lemma F.22, which violates Φ OSPO and Φ S-RDV . To verify the failure of EPG ∗, only its first part, the alternating singleton activation, is needed. If no nonzero Move occurs, the joint-stop sequence is ( C0) and the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗

fails; the execution of Lemma F.22 itself is the fair continuation witnessing this. Otherwise, as in that lemma, the two robots traverse the same states until the first nonzero Move , and 

a is activated first, so that Move is performed by a in a round that activates a alone. The surrounding round boundaries are joint stops realizing C0 and C1, and b does not move in between, so the expansion condition fails at i = 0 (Lemma 5.1(1)). This is a safety violation at a finite time, and it precedes the continuation witnessing any liveness violation of Φ OSPO .In either case a single execution violates all three predicates; Lemma E.18(1)(3) is satisfied, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.3. EOS /∈ Sol (OBLOT R).

84 Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Rsynch and use the execution of Lemma F.5, which violates Φ EPG ∗ and Φ OSPO . The visible state of an OBLOT robot is unique, so in a normalized frame the rule is a single pair ( λ, θ ) (Lemma E.16(a)). If λ = 0 no nonzero Move occurs, so the first nonzero Move demanded by Lemma D.21 does not exist and Φ S-RDV fails. If λ̸ = 0 and ( λ, θ )̸ = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆), the execution of Lemma F.5 is the fully synchronous one. When (λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0), the nonzero Move of round 1 does not meet the requirement of Lemma D.22, namely a landing of a at B = 3 d or of b at A = 0, so Φ S-RDV fails at that landing. When ( λ, θ ) = (2 , 0), round 1 takes a to 3 d and b to 0, and round 2 takes a to −3d and b to 6 d, both outside [ A, B ] = [0 , 3d](Lemma D.27), so Φ S-RDV fails. If ( λ, θ ) = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆), the execution of Lemma F.5 is the alternating singleton one. Since ( 12 , π )̸ = (2 , 0), the robot a moves from d to d/ 2 at its first activation, which is not the landing at B = 3 d required by Lemma D.22, so Φ S-RDV fails. In each case, a single execution violates all three predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

G.1.2 EPG ∗ ∨ OSPO ∨ VSS (EOV) Lemma G.4. EOV /∈ Sol (FSTA S ).Proof. The execution of Lemma F.24 is that of Lemma F.22 and violates Φ OSPO and Φ VSS . The argument of Lemma G.2 also gives a violation of Φ EPG ∗ on that same execution. Hence Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.5. EOV /∈ Sol (OBLOT R).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Rsynch and use the execution of Lemma F.5, which violates Φ EPG ∗ and Φ OSPO . By Lemma E.16(a) the rule is a single pair ( λ, θ ). If λ = 0 no nonzero Move occurs and condition (P1) of VSS fails by Lemma D.21. If λ̸ = 0 and ( λ, θ )̸ = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆), the execution is the fully synchronous one. When ( λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0), the nonzero Move of round 1 does not meet the requirement of Lemma D.22, so no valid T1 exists and Φ VSS 

fails. When ( λ, θ ) = (2 , 0), round 2 takes a to −3d and b to 6 d, neither of which lies in P = {0, d, 2d, 3d},so the endpoint condition (P1-b) fails. If ( λ, θ ) = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆), the execution is the alternating singleton one and a moves from d to d/ 2 /∈ P at its first activation, so (P1-b) fails there. In each case, a single execution violates all three predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

G.1.3 EPG ∗ ∨ S-RDV ∨ ZCC (ESZ) Lemma G.6. ESZ /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Use the execution of Lemma F.26, which violates Φ S-RDV and Φ ZCC . If it contains no nonzero move, the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails. Otherwise consider the landing of the first nonzero move of a. In both branches of that construction, 

b remains at its initial position until after this landing, even when it already has a pending move. Thus the landing time is a joint stop, and the center of gravity has changed by half the nonzero displacement of a. The expansion condition of EPG ∗ therefore fails at this first transition. This safety violation persists along the continuation constructed in Lemma F.26. Hence the same fair execution violates all three predicates, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.7. ESZ /∈ Sol (FSTA AM ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under AM and use the execution of Lemma F.27, where Φ S-RDV and Φ ZCC were shown to fail. Its first phase is an interval of alternating singleton activa-tions, which activates one robot at a time, and the configuration is unchanged before the first nonzero 

Move .85 In the branch in which no nonzero Move ever occurs the joint-stop sequence has the single term C0

and the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails. Otherwise the first nonzero Move occurs in that first phase and is performed while the peer is not activated, so by Lemma 5.1(1) the expansion condition fails at 

i = 0, a safety violation. The prefix of Lemma F.27 is then followed by the continuation used there, namely the coincidence continuation from Lemma C.16 or the continuation from Lemma C.17, and the violation persists along it (Lemma E.18(1)). In each branch a single fair execution violates all three predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.8. ESZ /∈ Sol (OBLOT R).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Rsynch and use the execution of Lemma F.9, which violates Φ EPG ∗ and Φ ZCC . By Lemma E.16(a) the rule is a single pair ( λ, θ ). If λ = 0 no nonzero Move occurs, so the first nonzero Move demanded by Lemma D.21 does not exist and Φ S-RDV fails. If λ̸ = 0 and ( λ, θ )̸ = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆), the execution of Lemma F.9 is the fully synchronous one. When (λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0), the nonzero Move of round 1 does not meet the requirement of Lemma D.22 and Φ S-RDV 

fails at that landing. When ( λ, θ ) = (2 , 0), the robots land at 3 d and 0 in round 1 and at −3d and 6 d in round 2, leaving [ A, B ] = [0 , 3d] (Lemma F.23, Lemma D.27), so Φ S-RDV fails. If ( λ, θ ) = ( λ⋆, θ ⋆), the execution is the alternating singleton one and a moves from d to d/ 2 at its first activation, which is not the landing at B = 3 d required by Lemma D.22, so Φ S-RDV fails. In each case a single execution violates all three predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

G.1.4 EPG ∗ ∨ S-RDV ∨ VEC (ESX) Lemma G.9. ESX /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Asynch and use the execution of Lemma F.29, which violates Φ S-RDV and Φ VEC . We show that it also violates Φ EPG ∗ . The initial centre of gravity is 12 (a0 + b0) = 32 d.

No nonzero output. The execution contains no nonzero Move , so the joint-stop sequence has the single term C0 and the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails. The same infinite execution witnesses this, together with the two liveness violations of Lemma F.29. 

Case (λ, θ ) = (2 , 0) . The Move of b begins strictly after that of a ends, so at the landing of a neither robot is inside a Move , although b still has a pending one. That instant is a joint stop and realizes 

C1 = (3 d, 2d), of centre of gravity 52 d̸ = 32 d; equivalently, b completes no nonzero Move between C0 and 

C1, so Lemma 5.1(1) applies. The expansion condition of EPG ∗ fails at i = 0, a safety violation in the prefix. 

Case (λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0) . The configuration is unchanged before the solo activation interval of a, so the first activation of a in it is the first nonzero Move of the execution and b is not activated during it. The maximal joint-stop intervals before and after it realize C0 and C1, so by Lemma 5.1(1) the expansion condition fails at i = 0, a safety violation in the prefix. In the first case all three violations are liveness violations of one infinite execution. In the other two the violation of Φ EPG ∗ is finite and precedes the continuations supplied by Lemma F.29. In either case Lemma E.18(1)(3) is satisfied, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.10. ESX /∈ Sol (FSTA A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under Asynch and use the execution of Lemma F.30, which violates Φ S-RDV and Φ VEC .If no nonzero Move occurs, the joint-stop sequence has the single term C0 and the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails. Otherwise, in both cases of that proof the first nonzero Move is performed by a from the initial configuration, and b completes no nonzero Move before a has landed: for pE = B it is not activated, and for pE̸ = B its Move is delayed until after the landing. The landing of a is therefore a joint stop realizing C1, and by Lemma 5.1(1) the expansion condition of EPG ∗ fails at i = 0, a safety violation preserved by the rest of the execution (Lemma E.18(1)(2)). 86 In each case a single execution violates all three predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.11. ESX /∈ Sol (OBLOT R).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Rsynch and use the execution of Lemma F.12, which violates Φ EPG ∗ and Φ VEC and which is in every case the alternating singleton one with an empty fully synchronous prefix and normalized frames. By Lemma E.16(a) the rule is a single pair ( λ, θ ). If λ = 0 no nonzero Move occurs, so the first nonzero Move demanded by Lemma D.21 does not exist and Φ S-RDV fails. If λ̸ = 0 and ( λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0), the landing of round 1 is not the B = 3 d required by Lemma D.22, so Φ S-RDV fails at that landing. If ( λ, θ ) = (2 , 0), then c1 = |1 − 2| = 1 ̸ = 0 and Lemma D.23 applies: the robot b, activated immediately after a lands at 3 d, receives an isomorphic local input and lands at 4 d / ∈ [A, B ], so Φ S-RDV fails. In each case a single execution violates all three predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

G.1.5 EPG ∗ ∨ S-RDV ∨ CFSM (ESC) Lemma G.12. ESC /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. That execution has the same three cases as the one of Lemma F.29, and the argument of Lemma G.9 applies verbatim to each of them: with no nonzero output the joint-stop sequence has the single term C0 and the perpetuity condition of EPG ∗ fails; when (λ, θ ) = (2 , 0) the landing of a is a joint stop realizing C1 = (3 d, 2d) and Lemma 5.1(1) makes the expansion condition fail at i = 0; and otherwise the first nonzero Move is the first activation of a in its solo interval, with b not activated, so Lemma 5.1(1) applies again. In the first case all three violations are liveness violations of one infinite execution; in the other two the violation of Φ EPG ∗ is finite and precedes the continuations supplied by Lemma F.32. In either case Lemma E.18(1)(3) is satisfied, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.13. ESC /∈ Sol (OBLOT R).Proof. In each case a single execution violates all three predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 88 G.2 Three-Task Disjunctions Containing SRO 

This subsection treats the six antichains {SRO , E, P } with E ∈ E and P ∈ P 3 = {ZCC , VEC , CFSM },which carry thirteen boundary obligations in total. The class OBLOT S occurs here as a boundary class for five of these antichains, and no corresponding lemma exists in Appendix F; the execution for it is constructed from scratch in Lemma G.21 and reused by the four later OBLOT S lemmas. 

G.2.1 SRO ∨ S-RDV ∨ ZCC (RSZ) Lemma G.19. RSZ /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Use the construction of Lemma F.26. If no nonzero output occurs, the perpetuity condition of SRO fails as well. If the first nonzero rule is (2 , 0), the first solo move of a reverses the segment and satisfies neither C1 

nor C2 . Thus Φ SRO has a safety violation before the continuation used in Lemma F.26. Otherwise keep the peer inactive during the initial solo interval of a. If the first move lands on the peer, the resulting zero segment violates the rotation condition of SRO. If it does not, choose the interval to contain at least two nonzero moves of a. Lemma E.19 then gives a safety violation of Φ SRO .For the ZCC component, use the finite safety violation or the continuation property of Lemma C.16, as in the proof of Lemma F.26. In the contraction case σ1 ∈ (0 , 1) the robots remain in the original initial segment in their original order after any number of solo activations of a, so the continuation property applies at the end of the interval chosen above. The earlier safety violations persist. Thus one fair infinite execution violates all three predicates, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.20. RSZ /∈ Sol (FSTA AM ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under AM and modify the execution of Lemma F.17 as follows. If no nonzero Move ever occurs, Φ S-RDV fails by Lemma D.21, and the first condition of SRO and the third condition of ZCC fail as shown in Lemma F.17. If the first nonzero Move of a does not land at the B = 3 d required by Lemma D.22, then Φ S-RDV 

fails at that point, and the execution of Lemma F.17 may be used unchanged, so Φ SRO and Φ ZCC fail as shown there. If it does land at B, then ( λs, θ s) = (2 , 0), and this displacement is neither the 90 ◦ clockwise rotation about the peer required by C1 nor the one about the midpoint required by C2 , so by Lemma E.19 the second condition of SRO fails already at this move and no second nonzero Move of a is needed. Insert, immediately after this move, a solo activation interval of b: activating b alone until it reaches the state s

leaves the configuration unchanged, since in all earlier states λ = 0 and the state transition of an FSTA 

robot depends only on its own state; then b in state s observes a peer at distance d and, in the normalized frame, receives a local input isomorphic to that of a, so it applies the same (2 , 0) and, by Lemma D.23, lands at 4 d / ∈ [A, B ], which violates Φ S-RDV . Moreover, the first move of a alone takes it from d to 3 d

while b rests at b0 = 2 d, so 

∥a − a0∥ = 2 d > d = ∥b − a0∥

and the collision-freedom condition of ZCC has already failed. No additional delayed Move is needed; append an arbitrary fair, time-divergent tail. In every case a single fair execution violates all three predicates, and the safety violations established in its prefix persist (Lemma E.18(1)), so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.21. RSZ /∈ Sol (OBLOT S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Ssynch . No lemma of Appendix F has 

OBLOT S as a boundary class, so we construct the execution directly. The visible state of an OBLOT 

robot is unique, so in a normalized frame the rule is a single pair ( λ, θ ) (Lemma E.16(a)). 

Case λ = 0 . Since OBLOT has no colors, the two robots do not move under any schedule. Hence the sequence of configurations reduces to C0 and the perpetuity condition of SRO fails, the robots remain 89 at distinct positions and the convergence condition of ZCC fails, and no nonzero Move exists, so Φ S-RDV 

fails by Lemma D.21. All three are independent of the schedule, hence persist along any fair infinite continuation. 

Case (λ, θ ) = (2 , 0) . Define the execution by one solo activation of a, then one solo activation of 

b, then alternating singleton activations and an arbitrary fair tail, all admissible under Ssynch . The displacement of a is neither the 90 ◦ clockwise rotation about the peer ( C1 ) nor the one about the midpoint (C2 ), so by Lemma E.19 the second condition of SRO fails at this move. Moreover σ1 = 1 − 2 = −1, so c1 = 1 and ψ ≡ π (Lemma E.2(iii)), whence fa(1) = d |1 − eiπ | = 2 d > d = ∥b(0) − a(0) ∥ and the collision-freedom condition of ZCC fails. Finally a has landed at B = 3 d, so the robot b, activated immediately afterwards, receives in the normalized frame an isomorphic local input, applies the same (2 , 0) and, by Lemma D.23, lands at 4 d / ∈ [A, B ], so Φ S-RDV fails. 

Case λ̸ = 0 and (λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0) . Define the execution by an interval in which a alone is activated k

times in a row (Lemma E.4, with k large enough for what follows), then alternating singleton activations and an arbitrary fair tail. The first nonzero Move of a is not the landing at B required by Lemma D.22, so Φ S-RDV fails at that point. For Φ SRO : if that move is neither of type C1 nor of type C2 , the second condition fails at once, which includes the case c1 = 0, since then di+1 = 0 while both types require a nonzero value; if it is of one of the two types, the second solo nonzero Move of a makes the second condition fail by Lemma E.19. For Φ ZCC , put σ1 := 1 − λe iθ and regard A as an FCOM algorithm with a single color, so that the constructions in the proof of Lemma C.16 apply. Every activation is a complete cycle under Ssynch , so no operation is pending at the end of the solo interval. If c1 = 0, the robots coincide after the first activation; end the solo interval there and use the coincidence continuation described in the proof of Lemma C.16. This either keeps both positions fixed forever or produces a finite violation of the collision-freedom condition. If c1̸ = 0 and σ1 /∈ (0 , 1), the collision-freedom condition fails at a finite time for k large enough, as in the proof of Lemma F.26. In the remaining contraction case 

σ1 ∈ (0 , 1), the robots still lie in the original initial segment in their original order after the k activations, and neither has a pending operation; the continuation property proved in Lemma C.16 supplies a fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ ZCC , while preserving the safety violations already obtained in the prefix. In every case a single execution violates all three predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

G.2.2 SRO ∨ S-RDV ∨ VEC (RSX) Lemma G.22. RSX /∈ Sol (FCOM A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FCOM under Asynch and use the execution of Lemma F.29, which violates Φ S-RDV and Φ VEC . We show that it also violates Φ SRO , under the same case distinction. 

No nonzero output. No nonzero Move ever occurs, so the sequence of joint-stop configurations reduces to C0 and the perpetuity condition of SRO fails. The same infinite execution witnesses this, together with the two liveness violations of Lemma F.29. 

Case (λ, θ ) = (2 , 0) . The landing of a is a joint stop realizing C1 = (3 d, 2d), so d1 = −d0. Neither 

C1 , which requires d1 = R−90 ◦ d0, nor C2 , which requires d1 = 2 −1/2R−45 ◦ d0, is satisfied by a rotation by 180 ◦ of the same norm. The second condition of SRO therefore fails, a safety violation in the prefix. 

Case (λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0) . In the solo activation interval of a the robot applies the same pair ( λ, θ ), with 

λ̸ = 0, at every activation (Lemma E.16(b)). If c1 = 0 then a lands on the position of b at the first activation and d1 = 0, while C1 preserves the norm and C2 scales it by 2 −1/2, so neither can hold with 

d0̸ = 0; the second condition of SRO fails. If c1̸ = 0, the interval has length k ≥ 3, so a performs two nonzero Move operations with its peer at rest, and by Lemma E.19 the rotation condition and C3 cannot both hold, so the second condition fails again. Either way this is a safety violation in the prefix. In the first case all three violations are liveness violations of one infinite execution. In the other two the violation of Φ SRO is finite and precedes the continuations supplied by Lemma F.29. In either case Lemma E.18(1)(3) is satisfied, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 90 Lemma G.23. RSX /∈ Sol (FSTA A).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under Asynch and use the execution of Lemma F.19, which violates Φ SRO and Φ VEC .If λs = 0 for every state s, no nonzero Move occurs and Φ S-RDV fails by Lemma D.21, while Φ SRO 

and Φ VEC fail in the first case of Lemma F.19. Otherwise the robot a performs the first nonzero Move in the round of the first common state s with 

λs > 0. If its landing is not the B = 3 d required by Lemma D.22, then Φ S-RDV fails at that point. If it is, then ( λ, θ ) = (2 , 0), which is neither the C1 rotation about the peer nor the C2 rotation about the midpoint, so by Lemma E.19 Φ SRO fails at once. In this case the points of the movement segment of a

are pμ = (1 + 2 μ)d with μ ∈ (0 , 1), and the robot b, observing pμ in state s, applies the same (2 , 0) in the normalized frame and lands at 2 d + 2 ((1 + 2 μ)d − 2d) = 4 μd . Taking μ ∈ (3 /4, 1) gives 4 μd > 3d,so the robots leave [ A, B ] = [0 , 3d] and Φ S-RDV fails. The set of parameters to be excluded for the VEC violation is countable, so such a μ can be chosen inside (3 /4, 1) and all three violations occur in the same execution. All three are safety violations, so they may be concatenated by Lemma E.18(1)(2), and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.24. RSX /∈ Sol (OBLOT S ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under Ssynch and use the execution of Lemma G.21, in which a alone is activated and then a and b are activated alternately. The violations of ΦSRO and Φ S-RDV were shown there. By Lemma E.16(a) the rule is a single pair ( λ, θ ). If λ = 0 the robots do not move, the sequence of configurations reduces to C0, and d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition of VEC fails. If c1 = 0, the robot a lands on the position of b at its first activation and di+1 = 0, while all the targets permitted by the transition condition of VEC are nonzero, so that condition fails. Otherwise the only single displacements satisfying the transition condition rotate the segment clockwise by 45 ◦ or by 90 ◦; if a departs from them the transition condition fails at that point, and otherwise a reaches a configuration with di ⊥ d0 within its two activations, at which moment both robots are at rest, but the same displacement is repeated in the alternating singleton activations that follow, so the robots move again and the stopping condition fails. All of these are safety violations, and a single execution violates all three predicates, so Lemma 6.1 gives the claim.

In either case Lemma E.18(1)(3) is satisfied, and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.26. RSC /∈ Sol (OBLOT S ).Proof. # G.3 Three-Task Disjunctions Containing MCv 

This subsection treats the two antichains {MCv , VSS , P } with P ∈ { VEC , CFSM }, which carry three boundary obligations in total. The class OBLOT AM occurs here as a boundary class for both antichains, and no lemma of Appendix F has it as a boundary class; the execution for it is constructed from scratch in Lemma G.33 and reused in Lemma G.34. We record once the two activation patterns used there. Under AM the adversary may activate one robot at a time (Lemma E.4, whose statement includes K = AM ), and it may also activate both robots in lockstep: giving the two robots identical phase times tL < t C < t B < t E in each round places every 

Look before both Move intervals, so no Look falls inside the Move interval of the peer and M -atomicity is respected. In such a round both robots compute from the same snapshot, so Lemma E.2(ii) applies and gives d 7 → c2d with the midpoint fixed. We refer to the latter pattern as a fully synchronous round; note that Lemma E.13 is stated for K = F and is not invoked below. 

G.3.1 MCv ∨ VSS ∨ VEC (MVX) Lemma G.32. MVX /∈ Sol (FSTA A).

93 Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of FSTA under Asynch . Use coordinates A = 0, a0 = d,

b0 = 2 d, B = 3 d on the initial line, with P = {0, d, 2d, 3d}, and normalized frames at every Look at which the robots are distinct. Activate both robots simultaneously while every output is zero; they then carry the same state at every round boundary and the configuration stays at ι.

No nonzero output. Continue forever. Condition (P1) fails by Lemma D.21, so Φ VSS fails; the sequence of joint-stop configurations reduces to C0 and d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition of VEC fails; and the two robots remain at distinct positions, so the convergence condition of MCv fails as well. This execution witnesses all three liveness violations. Otherwise let σ be the common state at the start of the first round with a nonzero output, let ( λ, θ ), with λ̸ = 0, be the corresponding normalized displacement, and put c1 := c1(σ). 

Case c1 = 0 , that is (λ, θ ) = (1 , 0) . Activate both robots in that round. Each moves to the posi-tion of its peer, so they exchange positions and the distance returns to d after passing through 0; the monotonicity condition of MCv fails. The cumulative nonzero-move counts are (1 , 1), so an admissible exchange configuration would have to be ( B, A ) = (3 d, 0), whereas the landing is (2 d, d ); no valid T1

exists and Φ VSS fails by Lemma D.22. Finally C1 = (2 d, d ) gives d1 = −d0, which is neither the clockwise rotation by 45 ◦ nor the one by 90 ◦, so the transition condition of VEC fails at i = 0. All three are safety violations at a finite time; append an arbitrary admissible fair, time-divergent tail. 

Case c1̸ = 0 . At the round boundary at which both robots carry σ the configuration is still ι; from there continue as in Lemma F.36, activating a first, so that a performs the first nonzero Move from σ

while b is in σ with no pending operation. Let pE be its landing point. If pE = B, then as in that lemma d1 = −d0 violates the transition condition of VEC, and b lands at 4d / ∈ P by Lemma D.23, so Φ VSS fails. Moreover the Move of a from d to 3 d passes through b0 = 2 d, so the distance to the stationary peer drops to 0 and returns to d, and the monotonicity condition of MCv fails. All three are safety violations; append an arbitrary fair tail. If pE̸ = B, then as in that lemma b observes pμ and its Move is delayed until a has landed; Φ VSS 

fails by Lemma D.22, and the transition condition of VEC fails for all μ outside a finite set. The landing point of b is affine in μ with nonzero slope, so it equals pE for at most one μ; excluding it as well, and since c1̸ = 0 gives pE̸ = b0, the robots are at distinct positions after both landings. It remains to violate MCv in this case. For MCv we use the following dichotomy, applied to every nonzero Move of the execution whose peer is at rest. Along such a Move the distance to the stationary peer is the distance from a fixed point to a point travelling along a segment, hence non-increasing and then non-decreasing. If the landing point is not the point of that segment closest to the peer, the distance strictly increases on a nonempty final part of the Move , so the monotonicity condition of MCv fails at a finite time and we are done. We may therefore assume that every such Move ends exactly at its closest approach, so that the distance is non-increasing along the execution so far. Both robots have now performed the same number of activations, and every Look so far has observed distinct positions (excluding also the at most one μ with pμ = b0), so by Lemma E.1(1) they carry the same color. At this point the robots occupy distinct positions, carry the same internal color, and have no pending operation. Apply the continuation statement proved in Lemma C.7; its schedules are admissible under AM , hence under Asynch . It supplies a fair, time-divergent continuation violating Φ MCv , while all safety violations established in the prefix persist. In the case without nonzero output, all three liveness violations are witnessed by the explicit fair execution given there. In the cases c1 = 0 and pE = B, and whenever the dichotomy above yields a monotonicity violation, all three violations are safety violations at a finite time. In the remaining case, the companion predicates have already suffered safety violations. The continuation from Lemma C.7 witnesses either a finite monotonicity violation or a failure of convergence. Thus all predicates fail on the same fair infinite execution. Hence Lemma E.18(1)(2)(3) is satisfied and {MCv , VSS , VEC } has the common failure property on FSTA A. Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

Lemma G.33. MVX /∈ Sol (OBLOT AM ).Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under AM . No lemma of Appendix F has 94 OBLOT AM as a boundary class, so we construct the execution directly, using the two activation patterns recorded above. Use coordinates A = 0, a0 = d, b0 = 2 d, B = 3 d on the initial line, with P =

{0, d, 2d, 3d}. The visible state of an OBLOT robot is unique, so in a normalized frame the rule is a single pair ( λ, θ ) (Lemma E.16(a)). Put c1 := c1(λ, θ ) and c2 := c2(λ, θ ) as in Lemma E.2. 

Case λ = 0 . Neither robot ever moves, under any schedule. Hence (P1) fails by Lemma D.21 and ΦVSS fails; the sequence of joint-stop configurations reduces to C0 and d0̸ ⊥ d0, so the attainment condition of VEC fails; and the robots remain at distinct positions, so the convergence condition of MCv fails. All three are independent of the schedule and persist along any fair infinite continuation. 

Case c1 = 0 , that is (λ, θ ) = (1 , 0) . Activate both robots simultaneously in round 1, which AM

permits. Each moves to the position of its peer, so they exchange positions and the distance returns to d

after passing through 0; the monotonicity condition of MCv therefore fails. The cumulative nonzero-move counts are (1 , 1), so an admissible exchange configuration would have to be ( B, A ) = (3 d, 0), whereas the landing is (2 d, d ); no valid T1 exists and Φ VSS fails by Lemma D.22. Finally C1 = (2 d, d ) gives 

d1 = −d0, which is neither the clockwise rotation by 45 ◦ nor the one by 90 ◦, so the transition condition of VEC fails at i = 0. All three are safety violations at a finite time; append an arbitrary admissible fair, time-divergent tail, which preserves them. 

Case 0 < c 1 < 1. Define the execution by an interval of alternating singleton activations of a and 

b, carried up to the round following the first nonzero Move and given normalized frames (Lemma E.4), followed by a delayed-Move interval of b (Corollary E.6; the suspension is placed between tC (b, i ) and 

tB (b, i ) and is therefore admissible under AM ), followed by an arbitrary fair tail. In the first interval, if the landing point of the first nonzero Move is not the point required by Lemma D.22, no valid T1 exists; and if it is that point, the robot activated in the next round receives an isomorphic local input and lands at 4 d / ∈ P by Lemma D.23. Either way Φ VSS fails. For Φ VEC the argument of Lemma F.12 applies verbatim: the only single displacements that the transition condition admits while the peer stands still are the 45 ◦ and 90 ◦ clockwise rotations of the segment, so if a departs from them that condition fails at that move, and if it does not, a perpendicular configuration is reached within at most two moves, after which the same displacement is applied again and the stopping condition fails. Since c1̸ = 0, neither of the two Move operations of this interval lands on the peer, so the robots are at distinct positions at its end. In the second interval, if no robot performs a nonzero Move from that point on, the two robots stay at these distinct positions and the convergence condition of MCv fails. Otherwise b computes a destination with displacement v̸ = 0 approaching a, suspends its Move , and in the meantime a alone is activated repeatedly. Since OBLOT has no colors, every such activation applies the same ( λ, θ ), so d(j) = c j 

> 1

d → 0by Lemma E.2(i) and the vector w from b to a at the release can be made to satisfy ∥w∥ < ∥v∥/2. The distance immediately before the Move of b is ∥w∥, whereas at its completion it satisfies 

∥w − v∥ ≥ ∥v∥ − ∥ w∥ > ∥w∥,

so the monotonicity condition of MCv fails [CP05, Lemma 4]. 

Case c1 ≥ 1. The solo activations of a no longer bring the distance below ∥v∥/2, so define the execution to be an infinite sequence of fully synchronous rounds. For λ > 0 the inequality c1 ≥ 1 is equivalent to 

λ ≥ 2 cos θ, which implies λ ≥ cos θ, that is c2 ≥ 1. Hence Lemma E.2(ii) gives d(k) = c k 

> 2

d(0) ≥ d(0) > 0for every k, so the two robots do not converge to a single point and the convergence condition of MCv fails. For Φ VEC the argument of Lemma C.20 applies verbatim to this execution, since it uses only that both robots are activated simultaneously in every round and that the displacement rule is the single pair ( λ, θ ). For Φ VSS : if ( λ, θ )̸ = (2 , 0), the nonzero Move of round 1 does not meet the requirement of Lemma D.22; and if ( λ, θ ) = (2 , 0), then round 1 takes a to 3 d and b to 0, so in round 2 the robot a

lands at −3d and b at 6 d, both outside P , which violates (P1-b). In every case a single execution violates all three predicates, so {MCv , VSS , VEC } has the common failure property on OBLOT AM and Lemma 6.1 gives the claim. 

G.3.2 MCv ∨ VSS ∨ CFSM (MVC) Lemma G.34. MVC /∈ Sol (OBLOT AM ).

95 Table 6: The eighteen covering pairs of the signature-inclusion order. Each pair μ, ν in a row satisfies 

μ ≥ ν by previously established inclusions and equivalences.

Proof. Let A be any deterministic algorithm of OBLOT under AM and use the execution of Lemma G.33, where the violations of Φ MCv and Φ VSS were established. We show that Φ CFSM fails under the same case distinction. 

Case λ = 0 . Neither robot ever moves, so moves( a) = 0 ̸ = 1 and the single-move condition fails. 

Case c1 = 0 . The two robots exchange positions in round 1 and therefore occupy the same point while they travel, so the collision-freedom condition fails. 

Case 0 < c 1 < 1. The robot a performs its first nonzero Move in the interval of alternating singleton activations, and in the delayed-Move interval of b the distance stays positive, so each of the repeated solo activations of a is again a nonzero Move ; hence moves( a) ≥ 2̸ = 1 and the single-move condition fails.

# H Order-Theoretic Certificates and Enumeration 

# H.1 A Certificate for the Signature Order 

Let R be the thirteen representative settings in Table 1. Define 

Cσ (μ) = {ν ∈ R : σ(ν) ⊊ σ(μ),

∄ξ ∈ R : σ(ν) ⊊ σ(ξ) ⊊ σ(μ)}.

Thus ν ∈ C σ (μ) means that the signature of ν is immediately below the signature of μ among the thirteen signatures. This definition uses only the six-column table, not an assumed computational comparison between its rows. Reading the thirteen binary rows gives Table 6. Every displayed comparison follows from Propo-sition 3.1 and transitivity, except for the following two comparisons, which also use the previously established equivalences: 

LUMI R ≡ FCOM R ≥ FCOM S , FCOM A ≡ FCOM AM ≥ OBLOT AM .

96 No strictness or principality is assumed in these derivations. For any proper inclusion σ(μ) ⊋ σ(ν), finiteness supplies a chain from σ(μ) to σ(ν) whose successive inclusions are covering relations among the thirteen signatures. The table certifies the corresponding computational inclusion at each step. Transitivity therefore gives μ ≥ ν. Equality of two signatures occurs only for the same row. This proves the reverse implication used in Theorem 3.5 without relying on a previously established complete comparison table. 

# H.2 Meet, Join, and Join-Irreducibility 

This subsection certifies the lattice claims made after the computational order has been established by Theorem 3.5. It is not used to prove that theorem: the earlier signature-order certificate relies only on the covering comparisons and the task-specific boundary results. 

Lemma H.1 (The computational lattice and its join-irreducibles) . The poset Q is a finite lattice with least element OBLOT A and greatest element LUMI F . Its nonleast join-irreducible elements are exactly 

J(Q) = {OBLOT F , OBLOT R, OBLOT S , FSTA A, FCOM A, OBLOT AM }.

Let Sσ = {σ(X) : X ∈ Q} . The family contains ∅ = σ(OBLOT A) and B6 = σ(LUMI F ). For every incomparable pair, the intersection of its signatures is the signature of the class in the meet column of Table 7. For a comparable pair X ≤ Y , the intersection is σ(X). These checks exhaust all pairs, so Sσ

is closed under intersection. For any T ⊆ B 6, define cl( T ) = ⋂{S ∈ S σ : T ⊆ S}.

The family being intersected is nonempty because it contains B6. Finiteness and intersection closure give cl( T ) ∈ S σ . It is the least signature containing T . Therefore the lattice operations are characterized by 

σ(X ∧Q Y ) = σ(X) ∩ σ(Y ),σ(X ∨Q Y ) = cl (σ(X) ∪ σ(Y )). (2) The join column of the table is obtained by this closure operation. For comparable X ≤ Y , one has 

X ∧Q Y = X and X ∨Q Y = Y , so those pairs need not be listed. 

Identifying the join-irreducibles. In any finite lattice, a nonleast element j with a unique lower cover j∗ is join-irreducible. Indeed, every x < j lies below j∗, and hence the join of two elements strictly below j is also at most j∗. Conversely, if j has two distinct lower covers u, v , they are incomparable. Their join lies below j, but cannot lie strictly below j without contradicting that u and v are lower covers. Thus j = u ∨ v is not join-irreducible. The covering relations in Table 6 give the six elements and lower covers in Table 8. For explicit certificates excluding every other nonleast element, Table 7 gives 

FSTA AM = FSTA A ∨Q OBLOT AM ,

FSTA S = FSTA A ∨Q OBLOT S ,

FSTA R = FSTA A ∨Q OBLOT R,

FCOM S = FCOM A ∨Q OBLOT S ,

LUMI R = FSTA R ∨Q FCOM S ,

LUMI F = OBLOT F ∨Q LUMI R.

In each equality, both joinands are strictly smaller than the left-hand side. The least element OBLOT A

is excluded from J(Q) by definition. This proves the complete list in Lemma H.1. 97 Table 7: Meet and join for all twenty-four incomparable pairs. The order and signatures are those already established in the main text.

Join-irreducible generation. For completeness, in any finite lattice L with least element 0, 

x = ∨{j ∈ J(L) : j ≤ x}, (3) where the empty join is 0. To prove this, induct on the number of elements at most x. For x = 0 it is immediate, and for join-irreducible x the right-hand side includes x. Otherwise x = u ∨ v for two strictly smaller elements. The induction hypothesis expresses each of them as a join of join-irreducibles at most 

x, proving the claim. Consequently, if x ≰ y, some j ∈ J(L) satisfies j ≤ x and j ≰ y. This is the sufficiency argument in Proposition 3.8. For necessity, let j ∈ J(Q) and let j∗ be its unique lower cover. A principal upset ↑ x distinguishes j

from j∗ only if x ≤ j and x̸ ≤ j∗. Since every x < j lies below j∗, this forces x = j. Thus every principal separating family contains ↑ j for every j ∈ J(Q), proving uniqueness. 98 Order embedding versus lattice embedding. The six-task signatures preserve meet, but their joins are not in general set unions. For example, 

FSTA A ∨Q FCOM A = LUMI R,

whose signature properly contains σ(FSTA A) ∪ σ(FCOM A). No distributivity of the model lattice is asserted. In fact, 

OBLOT R ∧Q (FSTA A ∨Q FCOM A) = OBLOT R,

(OBLOT R ∧Q FSTA A) ∨Q (OBLOT R ∧Q FCOM A) = OBLOT AM .

This does not affect distributivity of the upset lattice Up( Q), whose operations are union and intersection. 

# H.3 Antichain and Maximal-Complement Enumeration 

All counts in this subsection are determined solely by the finite order of the thirteen rows. They certify the number of abstract profiles and boundary obligations, not the existence of algorithms or of common-failure executions. 

Proposition H.2 (Antichains and maximal-complement counts) . The antichain polynomial of Q is 

AQ(z) = ∑

> A∈Ant( Q)

z|A| = 1 + 13 z + 24 z2 + 16 z3,

so Up( Q) has 54 elements, of which 53 are nonempty. For an antichain A put UA = ⋃ 

> X∈A

↑X and 

NA = Max( Q \ UA). Then 

∑

> A∈Ant( Q)
> |A|=2

|N A| = 47 , ∑

> A∈Ant( Q)
> |A|=3

|N A| = 34 .

Lemma H.3 (Width of the computational lattice) . The width of Q is three. Proof. The thirteen classes are partitioned into the three chains 

C1 : OBLOT A < OBLOT AM < OBLOT S < OBLOT R < OBLOT F < LUMI F ,C2 : FSTA A < FSTA AM < FSTA S < FSTA R,C3 : FCOM A < FCOM S < LUMI R.

Each Ci is a chain in the order established by Theorem 3.5, and the three cover all thirteen classes. An antichain meets each chain at most once, so its size is at most three. Conversely 

{OBLOT F , FSTA R, FCOM S } is a three-element antichain, since no two of its signatures are comparable. Hence width( Q) = 3. 

Lemma H.4 (Deletion recurrence for the antichain polynomial) . For a finite poset P and x ∈ P , let 

AP (z) = ∑  

> A∈Ant( P)

z|A|. Then 

AP (z) = AP \{ x}(z) + z AP \(↓P x∪↑ P x)(z), A∅(z) = 1 ,

where deletions carry the induced order. Proof. Antichains not containing x are precisely those of P \ { x}. If an antichain contains x, its other elements must be incomparable with x, and form an antichain of P \ (↓P x ∪ ↑ P x). Conversely, adjoining 

x to any such antichain is valid and increases its size by one, accounting for the factor z.99 Table 9: Finite enumeration certificate, grouped by the smallest row index. The omitted row indices have zero in all four columns.

Number the classes q1, . . . , q 13 in the row order of Table 1, and let Si = σ(qi). Thus (q1, . . . , q 13 ) = ( LUMI F , OBLOT F , LUMI R, FSTA R, OBLOT R, FCOM S , FCOM A,

FSTA S , FSTA AM , FSTA A, OBLOT S , OBLOT AM , OBLOT A).

For k ∈ { 2, 3}, let ak(i) be the number of k-element antichains whose smallest row index is i, and let 

bk(i) be the sum of |N A| over those antichains. The term “smallest” here refers to the numerical row index, not to a least element in the computational order. Here is an explicit counting rule using the signature table. Put 

Ii = {j > i : Si ⊈ Sj and Sj ⊈ Si}.

Then 

a2(i) = |Ii|, a3(i) = ∣∣{(j, k ) : j, k ∈ Ii, j < k, q j ∥ qk}∣∣.

Thus the incomparable-pair list in Table 7 also lists exactly the possible pairs used by this counting rule. Alternatively, the polynomial follows by applying Lemma H.4 to the same order. For the boundary counts, Y belongs to NA exactly when 

σ(X) ⊈ σ(Y ) for every X ∈ A, 

for every Z > Y there is X ∈ A with σ(X) ⊆ σ(Z).

The first condition puts Y outside UA, and the second makes it maximal there. In the second condition it suffices to test the upper covers of Y , because UA is an upset and Q is finite. Applying these rules gives Table 9. 

Proof of Proposition H.2. There is one empty antichain and there are thirteen singleton antichains. Lemma H.3 excludes antichains of size four or more, and the columns a2 and a3 of Table 9 give 24 and 16. Thus AQ(z) = 1 + 13 z + 24 z2 + 16 z3, and by the antichain–upset bijection of Proposition 2.2 the number of upsets is 1 + 13 + 24 + 16 = 54. The columns b2 and b3 give the totals 47 and 34 for the maximal-complement obligations. 100
